## OF THE REVEREND AND MOST FAMOUS
### MAN
# JOHN AMOS
## COMENIUS’S
## PRECURSOR TO UNIVERSAL WISDOM,
<note>original: "Pansophiæ Prodromus". The term <term>Pansophia</term>—literally "all-wisdom"—refers to Comenius’s ambitious philosophical project to harmonize all human knowledge (scientific, religious, and philosophical) into a single educational framework.</note>

->*indicated in the preface by S. H.* <note>Samuel Hartlib (c. 1600–1662), a German-British reformer and close associate of Comenius who promoted his works in England.</note> *and the*<-

<note>Bookstores in 17th-century London were identified by hanging signs rather than street numbers. The "Brazen Serpent" refers to the biblical story of Moses, a common symbol of healing and wisdom.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for the London edition of Jan Amos Comenius’s "Pansophiæ Prodromus," a foundational text outlining his vision for a unified system of all human knowledge. The edition was facilitated by Samuel Hartlib and published during a period of intense intellectual and educational reform in England.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, Samuel Hartlib, 17th-century printing, London book trade, St. Paul's Churchyard, educational reform</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>The previous page featured 18th-century library stamps and shelf marks from the British Museum. This page (signature A 2) begins the original 17th-century preface to the reader, marking the transition from the physical history of the book to the actual text of Jan Amos Comenius's work on Universal Wisdom.</meta>

<image-desc>The page begins with a decorative horizontal printer's ornament strip featuring alternating floral and geometric patterns. Below the heading, the body text starts with a large ornamental drop cap 'Q' decorated with foliage and blossoms.</image-desc>

### *To the Readers, Desirous of True Wisdom,*
### *Greetings.* <note>original: "S.", an abbreviation for "Salutem," the standard Latin greeting meaning "health" or "greetings."</note>

**Q**UITE <note>The text begins with a large decorative 'Q'.</note> some two years ago, at the urging of many and without the Author's knowledge, we published somewhat timidly the **PRELUDES** or **FORERUNNER** to the **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: A concept championed by Comenius meaning "all-knowledge," aiming to organize all human learning into a single, harmonious system.</term> of the most renowned **COMENIUS**. Those same works we now present again more confidently to the sight of all, having obtained approval for our previous daring from the Author himself, as well as the applause of many other distinguished men. We could have refrained from this labor until the works promised therein were brought to completion <note>original: "ad umbilicum deducerentur," literally "led to the navel." In the ancient world, this referred to reaching the end of a scroll, which was rolled onto a central stick called an *umbilicus*.</note>, if only the previous edition...

<meta>catchword: [if the previous edition] had [stayed/lasted]</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the editor's preface to the reader, explaining that Comenius's work on "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia) is being reissued with the author's formal blessing after a successful but unauthorized initial printing.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, preface, publishing history, 17th century, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's preface, picking up from the previous page's discussion regarding the initial reception of Comenius's work and the motivations for this new, expanded edition.</meta>

had secured for the Author those resources which such arduous and extraordinary endeavors no less deserved than required. Now, however, we have discovered that the generosity of many was limited by nothing more than the fact that they were either still entirely ignorant of **Comenius’s** <term>Jan Amos Comenius (1592–1670): A Moravian philosopher and pedagogue considered the father of modern education.</term> true goal, or they desired to be more certain of his character and abilities. In a sense, we have labored diligently to satisfy everyone with this second edition.

For we have taken care not only that everything contained in the previous edition should appear here again—and indeed significantly more corrected—but also that other matters of no less importance from the workshop of the same Master <note>original: "Artificis," meaning the "Artist" or "Craftsman," here referring to Comenius as the architect of this philosophical system.</note> might be added to them, which will easily provide proof of those things which

<summary>The editor explains that the second edition was produced to clarify Comenius’s goals to potential patrons and to provide a more accurate, expanded version of his "Pansophic" project.</summary>
<keywords>Comenius, second edition, patronage, Universal Wisdom, Pansophia, editorial corrections</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's preface, fulfilling the promise from the previous page to provide proof of Comenius’s abilities. It describes the "good fortune" of receiving additional manuscripts just as the printing process for the second edition was nearing completion.</meta>

previously had seemed beyond belief, even to many who were not unlearned. In this, it happened by some good fortune that we were able to be more generous than we had initially hoped. For besides the *Dissertation on the Study of the Latin Language* <note>original: "Dissertationem de studio sermonis Latini"</note>, etc., and the title of the *Great Didactic* <note>original: "Didacticae Magnae"</note> <term>The Great Didactic: One of Comenius’s most famous works, outlining his philosophy of "teaching all things to all people."</term> transcribed from the Author’s own manuscript, we had nothing at hand to join to the *Forerunner* <note>original: "Prodromo," referring to the *Prodromus Pansophiae* or "Prelude to Wisdom"</note>. And just as the work on nearly all of these was reaching its end, the very *Explanation of his <term>Pansophic: Relating to "Pansophia" or Universal Wisdom, a system where all knowledge is integrated and accessible to everyone.</term> Endeavors* <note>original: "Conatvvm ejus Pansophicorvm dilucidatio"</note> was sent to us by **Comenius**—a gift as long-desired as it was entirely unexpected. And although these were not [sent] by the Author with that intention—

<summary>The editor recounts how he unexpectedly received new manuscripts from Comenius, including his "Explanation of Pansophic Endeavors," just as the current volume was being finished.</summary>
<keywords>Comenius, Latin study, Great Didactic, Pansophia, manuscript, publishing history</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher's explanation for including Comenius’s "Explanation of Pansophic Endeavors" in this volume. The previous page ended mid-sentence, noting that Comenius had not sent the manuscript with the intention of publication.</meta>

...[that it] had been committed to print <note>original: "typis mandata"</note>, so that it might become public property <note>original: "publici juris"</note>, but only so that it could be shared much more easily in private with friends. Nevertheless, we did not believe he would be greatly offended if—having more regard for the present opportunity than for his own wishes—we added it as a companion to the previous works in this manner. Indeed, in this work we see the genius of the Man and the feasibility of such great undertakings <term>Undertakings: Translated from "moliminum," referring to the massive scope of Comenius’s "Pansophia" project to categorize all human knowledge.</term>—hitherto scarcely believed—shining forth more clearly than in any of his shorter treatises <note>original: "lucubratiunculis," literally "little night-studies" or minor works</note> published until now. By doing this, we are at once freed from the annoyance of having to deal with the quibbles and doubts of others in advance. For those who are not satisfied by the arguments brought forward by this excellent man on his own behalf, to them—as though...

<meta>catchword: oblo- (likely "obloquuturi" or "objectors")</meta>

<summary>The publishers justify printing Comenius's private manuscript, arguing that its brilliance and the clarity it brings to his grand "Pansophic" vision outweigh his original desire for private circulation.</summary>
<keywords>Comenius, publishing history, Pansophia, manuscript, intellectual property, scholarly defense</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher’s address to the reader, picking up from the previous page's discussion about critics of Comenius. The catchword from the previous page "oblo-" completes here as "obloquendi" (speaking ill or contradicting).</meta>

...to those who are inflamed more by a desire for contradiction <note>original: "obloquendi"</note> than by a love of truth, we would struggle in vain to justify his undertakings. Therefore, as it is the only course remaining and as the situation itself demands, I seriously advise and implore all of you, *Candidates of true Wisdom*, into whose hands these writings may come: do not read these things merely for your own amusement <note>original: "animi gratiâ," implying reading for pleasure rather than for serious study</note>, and even less should you condemn them before you have even read them. 

Instead, once you observe that all these things rest upon sufficiently firm foundations <term>Foundations: Latin "fundamentis," referring to the logical and philosophical basis of Comenius’s Pansophic system.</term>, you should strive to ensure that through your counsel, your assistance, and most especially through your financial support <note>original: "pecuniariis," literally "pecuniary" or monetary help</note>, these well-founded projects may at last begin to rise. For when you have received the ground-plan <term>Ground-plan: Latin "Ichnographia," an architectural term for a floor plan, used here to describe the structural outline of Comenius's universal knowledge system.</term> of so remarkable a work, and many other no less significant things from the storehouse <note>original: "penu"</note> of that man’s rich genius, free...

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: of charge (Latin: gratis)</meta>

<summary>The publisher appeals to serious scholars—"Candidates of Wisdom"—to support Comenius’s massive project not just with interest, but with practical and financial aid to help his "foundations" become a reality.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Jan Amos Comenius, intellectual funding, scholarly patronage, Ichnographia, architectural metaphor, scientific foundations</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher’s address to the reader. The catchword from the previous page "gratis" (meaning "for free" or "of charge") is completed here as "tis," finishing the sentence regarding the materials already provided to the reader.</meta>

...of charge that you have already received, would you be reluctant to bind yourselves to the <term>Architect: Referring to the author, Jan Amos Comenius, who is metaphorically "building" a house of universal knowledge.</term>—who, through a few small coins, might purchase the stones, wood, and other things necessary for so great a structure, and hire several porters and <term>fellow-workers</term> <note>original: "cooperarios"</note>, and so forth? In turn, should his exceptional piety alone, and the hardships of his long exile, not earn the generosity of any wise person? 

Will you allow, O Great and Noble Souls, that <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Sapientia"</note>, who is greater than any price, should offer herself to you for sale at so cheap a rate and yet find no buyer among you? Should offspring so distinguished—and otherwise immortal—be extinguished for lack of <note>original: "obstetricationis," or midwifery; the publisher suggests that without funding, Comenius’s "offspring" (his ideas) will die in the womb rather than being published.</note> assistance? 

May God, the Thrice Best and Greatest <note>original: "Deus ter Opt. Max.," a standard Latin honorific for the Divine</note>, forbid that the fear of Him and reverence for posterity should so depart from your minds that you might think...

<meta>catchword: ...shameful (Latin: pudenda)</meta>

<summary>The publisher uses architectural and biological metaphors to plead for financial support for Comenius, arguing that his "Structure of Wisdom" cannot be built without "stones and laborers" funded by generous patrons.</summary>

<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, patronage, exile, Wisdom, architectural metaphor, midwifery metaphor, financial support, Pansophia</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher’s direct appeal to the reader, completing the thought from the previous page's catchword "pudenda" (shameful) with "dendâ." The publisher is arguing that withholding financial support for Comenius is a moral failing that outweighs the small cost of the contribution.</meta>

...with [shameful] tenacity, would you rather that a perpetual 
stain be cast upon your **conscience and reputation**, than 
to obtain a perennial serenity for both 
from the surplus of your wealth? For—lest you be 
deceived—it is not the cause of **Comenius**, but of **GOD**, 
and of **His people**, that is at stake. 
His glory, and their tranquility and safety, are promoted 
by nothing more effectively than by this unusual 
**restoration** <note>original: "instauratio," a term often used by 17th-century scholars like Francis Bacon to describe a "renewal" or "repairing" of human knowledge</note> of **universal wisdom** <note>original: "sapientiæ omnimodæ," referring to Comenius's concept of Pansophy, or "all-knowledge"</note>. 
No Christian heart would hold the 
care for these things in disregard. 
As for **Comenius** himself, it will be easy enough to find 
the means to sustain his life <note>original: "unde vivat"</note>; 
but see to it that you, by despising this rare treasure, 
do not one day lack the means to **live well** <note>original: "unde bene vivatis." The author makes a distinction between merely surviving and "living well" through wisdom.</note>. 
As for the fruit of your 
immense generosity in this matter, 
there will never be a reason for you to rightly 
doubt it: You who [possess] so much of...

<vocab>Comenius, conscience, reputation, wisdom, restoration, generosity</vocab>

<summary>The publisher frames the funding of Comenius’s work not as a personal favor, but as a religious and civic duty to God and humanity. He warns the wealthy that while Comenius can survive without them, they cannot "live well" in a spiritual or intellectual sense without the "treasure" of his universal wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, Pansophy, universal wisdom, religious duty, patronage, restoration of knowledge, 17th-century education, philanthropy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory preface by Samuel Hartlib. He finishes his appeal for the financial support of Comenius’s work, which he began on the previous page, by envisioning a partnership between the scholar’s talent and the readers’ wealth.</meta>

the fruits of his own genius, he has freely bestowed upon you; what then might he not achieve when spurred on by your kindness? O, would that it may one day be our lot to witness a most beautiful pair, and one most worthy of our age which trembles under so many calamities—not, as that author says, *a wise man struggling with ill fortune* <note>original: "Sapientem cum mala fortunâ." This is a common Stoic sentiment, often associated with Seneca, describing the tragic nobility of a scholar who lacks the means to live.</note>, but rather *the industriousness of **Comenius** joined with the generosity of patrons* <note>original: "Mecœnatū munificentiâ." Maecenas was the famous patron of the Roman poets Virgil and Horace; his name became a standard term for a wealthy supporter of the arts and sciences.</note>, *so that the victory between the two remains forever in doubt.* With this auspicious hope I conclude, and I send you, **Kind Readers**, with a steady step toward the works themselves. If these works do not move a man's heart, then my own speech will certainly not set him on fire. Farewell, and kindly forgive the boldness <note>original: "libertati meæ." Hartlib is apologizing for his "liberty" or forwardness in asking for money so directly.</note> I have taken for the sake of the public good.

->London, from my house.<-
->The Kalends of January <note>January 1st</note>,<-
->In the Year 1639.<-

->**Samuel Hartlib**<-

<summary>Samuel Hartlib concludes his preface by praying for a "perfect match" between Comenius's hard work and the financial backing of wealthy patrons. He then invites the reader to proceed to the main text, hoping the work will speak for itself.</summary>
<keywords>Samuel Hartlib, Jan Amos Comenius, patronage, charity, 17th-century education, London, 1639</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as the Table of Contents for the collection published by Samuel Hartlib in 1639. It follows Hartlib’s introductory appeal for funding, moving from the persuasive preface to the specific academic and pedagogical works of Jan Amos Comenius contained within the volume.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal row of nine decorative floral ornaments, resembling fleur-de-lis.</image-desc>

## Included in this small book:

1. **The Forerunner of the Work on Universal Knowledge** <term>Pansophic: From the Greek 'pan' (all) and 'sophia' (wisdom); Comenius’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</term>, published two years ago under the title of **The Preludes**.
2. **A Didactic dissertation** <term>Didactic: Related to the art or science of teaching.</term>, concerning the study of the Latin Language, to be fully completed through a four-part progression: the *Vestibule*, the *Gate*, the *Palace*, and the *Treasuries of Latinity* <note>These represent Comenius's famous series of graded textbooks, which moved students from basic naming of objects (Vestibule) to complex literary style (Treasuries).</note>, etc.
3. *A Clarification of the Efforts toward Universal Knowledge* <note>original: "Conatuum Pansophicorum dilucidatio"</note> NB: Because this came to us as an unexpected gift from the Author when the work on the preceding sections was already nearly finished, it had to be placed in this position.
4. The **Title** and the **Chapter Headings** of *The Great Didactic*, now completed, transcribed from the Author’s own manuscript. NB: Although these should have been placed before the earlier dissertation, they were relegated to this final place through the oversight of the printers <note>original: "operarum incuriâ." Literally 'the carelessness of the workers' in the printing house.</note>.

<meta>This page serves as the internal title page for the first section of the collection: the Prodromus (Forerunner) to Comenius's project on universal wisdom. ## THE FORERUNNER
# OF THE PANSOPHIC WORK <term>Pansophic: Derived from the Greek words for "all" (pan) and "wisdom" (sophia). Comenius used this term to describe his lifelong project of organizing all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system to reflect the divine order of the universe.</term>
## COMMENCED BY JAN AMOS
### COMENIUS,

->**Published two years ago under the**<-
->**title of THE PRELUDES.**<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a flowering plant, likely a carnation or pink (Dianthus), with a large fringed blossom and two long, narrow leaves at the base of the stem, set upon a small dark pedestal or plot of earth.</image-desc>

->Ecclesiastes 5, verse 18 <note>In many 17th-century editions, this refers to the Book of Sirach (also known as Ecclesiasticus) 5:15: "In a great matter or a small, be not ignorant of anything."</note><-
> In a matter great as well as small, remain ignorant of not even one thing.

->Nothing is sweeter than to know all things. <note>original: "οὐδὲν γλυκύτερον ἢ πάντ' εἰδέναι."</note><-

->L O N D O N,<-
->IN THE YEAR 1639.<-

<summary>This is the title page for Jan Amos Comenius's "Prodromus" (The Forerunner), a foundational work outlining his vision for a unified system of universal knowledge. It establishes the philosophical grounding of the book through citations from the Bible and Cicero, emphasizing the value of comprehensive learning.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page follows the internal title page of the 1639 London edition of the "Prodromus." It serves as the formal opening of the text, addressed to all seekers of knowledge. It uses the traditional "Praises of Wisdom" (Encomia Sapientiae) to establish the book's philosophical and theological foundations.</meta>

<image-desc>A row of decorative fleurons at the top of the page. Below it, a formal salutation or title in large type. A large ornamental initial 'S' begins the main text, decorated with foliate scrolls and flowers.</image-desc>

# TO ALL LOVERS OF WISDOM, LIGHT, AND TRUTH,
## health and blessing from Christ, the fountain of wisdom, light, and truth.

<margin>Praises of Wisdom.</margin>

**S** <note>The text begins with a large, ornamental initial 'S' in the original Latin.</note> That Wisdom <note>original: *Sapientia*</note> (which to Aristotle is the knowledge of many and wonderful things; to Cicero indeed the knowledge of things divine and human, and of the causes by which they are contained; and finally to Solomon, the craftswoman of all things, teaching all things) has been celebrated with great praises since antiquity, cannot be unknown to those who pursue the studies of wisdom. *“She is more precious than all riches,”* (says the wisest of mortals <note>Referring to King Solomon, traditionally considered the author of the Book of Proverbs.</note>) *“and all things that are desired are not able to be compared to her. Length of days is in her right hand, and in her left hand riches and glory. Her ways are beautiful ways, and all her paths are peaceful. She is a tree of life to those who lay hold of her, and they who possess her are blessed.”* Proverbs 3:15–18. Cicero, moreover, says: *“No more excellent gift has been given to the race of mortals, nor ever shall be given.”* Horace <note>The Roman lyric poet.</note> indeed speaks thus: *“In short, the wise man is less than Jupiter <note>original: "Jove." The king of the gods in Roman mythology; here used to signify the highest possible state.</note> alone: he is rich,”*

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: The book (original: "Liber")</meta>

<summary>This page opens the main text with a formal greeting to all seekers of truth. It defines wisdom by blending the perspectives of Greek philosophy (Aristotle), Roman statesmanship (Cicero), and Biblical tradition (Solomon), emphasizing that wisdom is the most valuable human pursuit.</summary>
<keywords>Wisdom, Sapientia, Aristotle, Cicero, King Solomon, Horace, Proverbs, Philosophy, Enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Praises of Wisdom" section. It transitions from a poetic description of the wise man to citations of classical and biblical authorities (Seneca, Cicero, Solomon) justifying the supremacy of wisdom. The catchword "num" from the previous page suggests the opening line completes a thought about the "Wise Man."</meta>

*Free, honored, beautiful, and finally a king of kings.* <note>original: "Liber, honoratus, pulcher, rex deniq; regum." This line concludes a sequence of adjectives from the previous page—likely quoting the poet Horace—describing the idealized character of the Wise Man.</note>

If you ask the reason why they value this one virtue so highly, they will answer. Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Roman Stoic philosopher and advisor to Nero.</note> indeed says: *Without the study of wisdom, no one can live well, nor even tolerably.* Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator and philosopher whose works were central to Renaissance education.</note> claims: *Wisdom is the mother of all the arts; she first educates us in the worship of God, then in human justice—which is rooted in the fellowship of the human race—and then in modesty and greatness of soul.* He continues: *She dispels darkness from the mind as if from the eye, so that we may see all things: those above, those below, the first, the last, and everything in between.* Finally, he calls it *the medicine of the soul.* And Solomon <note>The biblical King Solomon, traditionally the author of the wisdom books of the Bible.</note> adds: *Wisdom strengthens the wise man more than ten princes of the city* (Ecclesiastes 7:19 <note>The original Latin text cites verse 20, following a common 17th-century numbering; in modern Bibles, this is verse 19.</note>) and that it is *an unfailing treasure for men, and those who use it become partakers in the friendship of God, commended by the gifts that come from learning.* For *God loves no one except him who dwells with wisdom* (Wisdom of Solomon 7:14, 28).

<margin>The pursuit of wisdom.</margin>
Rightly, therefore, the most excellent men of every age have set aside the care for fleeting things—riches, personal desires, and honors—and focused their thoughts, prayers, and studies there. By dedicating themselves to the contemplation of reality, they sought to comprehend whatever the mind can grasp, and in that way, to subdue the world. Such men truly are, amidst the remaining crowd of mortals, what the gift of wisdom itself <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword indicates the next word is "num," likely beginning "numen" (divine power or spirit).</note>

<summary>This page presents a collection of classical and biblical testimonies from Seneca, Cicero, and Solomon to establish wisdom as the ultimate human pursuit and a divine gift. It argues that wisdom is the foundation of morality, religion, and social order, elevating the wise person above worldly concerns.</summary>
<keywords>Seneca, Cicero, King Solomon, Stoicism, biblical wisdom, Book of Wisdom, Ecclesiastes, human justice, medicine of the soul, divine friendship, contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where the author cited the praises of wisdom from Seneca, Cicero, and Solomon, this page transitions into how wisdom is granted by God and accumulated through human experience over time.</meta>

<page-num>(3)</page-num>
<sig>B 2</sig>

[...whether] indeed, among other goods granted to mortals, they are most shining gems, or rather stars dispelling the fog of worldly darkness. Therefore, we must gratefully acknowledge the work of divine mercy, not only because He has opened the "theaters" of **Nature** and **Scripture** to us for His wisdom, <note>Early Modern thinkers often spoke of the "Two Books" through which God revealed himself: the Book of Nature (the physical world) and the Book of Scripture (the Bible).</note> but also because He has equipped us with the instruments for observing them and gathering the light of wisdom—namely, through **sense** and **reason**—and through His own divine **revelation**, which supplies the defects of sense and reason.

Furthermore, we owe it to divine beneficence that the **study of letters**, <note>The term "letters" (*literarum*) refers to the humanities, literature, and classical learning.</note> by which that pursuit of wisdom is fostered and passed down to us from others, has been preserved until our own age. Indeed, these studies flourish in our century as never before; so that the present age may, not undeservedly, take pride and congratulate itself in the name of **erudition**, while the hope of an even greater light shines forth.

<margin>Wisdom comes with late years.</margin> 

For it is necessary that the same thing happens to the world as happens to a man: that wisdom does not come to it except with late years. This is because, by the nature of things, it cannot be otherwise. Since wisdom is acquired through the **experience** of many things, and experience necessarily requires both a long duration of time and a variety of opportunities. It follows that the longer one lives, the more things are done; and the frequent handling of affairs increases experience, and experience increases wisdom, according to Sirach: *A man experienced in many things will think many thoughts.* <note>original: "Vir in multis expertus cogitabit multa." Citing the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 34:9, a book of the Apocrypha/Deuterocanon focused on ethical teachings.</note> And 

<meta>catchword: Poets</meta>

<summary>The author argues that wisdom is a divine gift revealed through nature, scripture, and human reason, and that the current age enjoys a peak of learning because wisdom naturally accumulates through experience over time.</summary>
<keywords>divine mercy, Nature and Scripture, sense and reason, erudition, experience, Sirach, progress of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author moves from the theoretical idea that wisdom increases with the "age" of the world to the practical evidence of this growth in the 17th century—specifically citing the printing press and the expansion of education.</meta>

<page-num>(4)</page-num>

<margin>
The hope of a more brilliant wisdom.
</margin>

Consider that saying of the Poet: *Experience has produced art through various trials.* <note>original: "Per varios usus artem experientia fecit." A quote from the Roman poet Manilius, suggesting that human skill is the cumulative result of practice and observation.</note> We, therefore, in the present age, are equipped with so many aids from experiments as no previous age could have been; why, then, should we not understand something more deeply? For not only by the benefit of **Printing** <note>The invention of the printing press was often viewed by 17th-century scholars as a "divine" technology reserved by God for the final age of the world to spread knowledge.</note> (an art which God, not without mystery, reserved for these latter times), whatever was wisely thought by the ancients—which was previously suppressed in darkness—has now been brought into the light and made known. But also, modern men themselves, stirred by new opportunities, have begun to attempt new things; and wisdom is multiplied daily through various experiments, increasing even to the point of a miracle, just as God predicted concerning the end times in *Daniel 12:4*. <note>The verse reads: "Many shall run to and fro, and knowledge shall be increased." Early modern reformers often cited this as a prophecy of their own scientific and educational revolutions.</note> 

Added to this is a passion for opening schools among all nations, the likes of which histories do not record having happened in any previous centuries. From this comes such a multiplication of books across all languages and nations that they are now known even to farmers and common women—books of which, previously, even the learned and the wealthy (buying them at great cost) could scarcely have a sufficient supply. Now, finally, a persistent effort by some is emerging to bring the **Method of study** <term>Methodum studiorum: a systematic way of teaching and learning, a major obsession of 17th-century educational reformers like Comenius</term> to such perfection that all human things worthy of knowledge can be instilled into minds with the easiest effort. If this should succeed (as I hope) and that which is sought is found, all

<meta>catchword: all</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the 17th century is a unique "golden age" of knowledge due to the printing press, the fulfillment of biblical prophecy, and new educational methods that make learning accessible to everyone, including the poor and women.</summary>
<keywords>Printing press, Daniel 12:4, Universal education, Method of study, Progress of knowledge, Literacy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed how the invention of printing and the expansion of schools have made knowledge accessible even to the common people. This page continues by arguing that while a new method for teaching languages has been found, a similar "gate" is needed for understanding the world itself.</meta>

<page-num>(5)</page-num>

...people, and a way for teaching all things quickly; I do not see what stands in the way of our recognizing and gratefully welcoming that long-foretold and long-expected, enlightened **Golden Age**. <note>The "Golden Age" (Aureum seculum) was a common trope for a future era of peace and perfect knowledge, often linked by 17th-century reformers to the improvement of education.</note>

<margin>
What is still lacking for the progress of wisdom.
</margin>
To reach this goal, however, one thing of great importance still seems to be missing: just as a fruitful shortcut for teaching **languages** more easily was recently found (in the recently published ***Gate of Tongues***), <note>original: "januâ L.L." referring to Comenius’s famous textbook *Janua linguarum reserata*, which revolutionized language learning by using sentences about the real world.</note> so too must a way be devised to build a **Gate of Things** <note>original: "rerum januam"</note> so that the mind may easily penetrate whatever exists in the **Arts and Sciences**. I will first demonstrate that taking up serious thoughts on this matter is more than necessary (if indeed we wish to look out for human affairs as best we can). Next, I will seek out the method for such a great endeavor; finally, I will explain on what occasion and with what success I myself undertook this unusual work.

<margin>
Wisdom should be the goal of literary study.
</margin>
First, I take it as an admitted truth that the study of letters, in which youth among all nations are now occupied, ought to be the cultivation of minds **toward Wisdom**; outside of this goal, it can be nothing but empty vanity. For whether we suppose that knowledge is desired for the sake of knowing out of curiosity, or for the sake of the mind for pleasure, or for reputation to stand out, or for the sake of profit to hunt for external goods: we are foolish if, for the sake of a goal so sublime...

<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while his "Gate of Tongues" simplified language learning, a "Gate of Things" is now needed to unlock the Arts and Sciences. He asserts that all education must aim toward true Wisdom rather than mere curiosity or profit.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, Gate of Tongues, Gate of Things, Arts and Sciences, Wisdom, educational reform, Jan Amos Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 5, where the author began to define the true purpose of education as the pursuit of Wisdom rather than empty vanity. Here, he outlines the specific biblical and philosophical characteristics of Wisdom to justify his proposed "Pansophic" method.</meta>

<page-num>(6)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Necessarily</meta>

...and we fix such humble and temporary things upon a divine gift. Let it therefore be settled that Wisdom is to be sought. But since Wisdom is said to be the ***Artisan of all things, teaching all things*** <note>original Latin: "Omnium rerum Artifex, omnia docens," quoting Wisdom of Solomon 7:21.</note> (Wisdom 7:21), it is clear that through the benefit of letters we ought to be moved toward a ***universal knowledge of things***, (pansophy <note>original Greek: "πανσοφίαν" (pansophian). This is a central concept for the author, Jan Amos Comenius, referring to a system of universal knowledge where all facts are interconnected and lead to God.</note>, that is, a full wisdom embracing all things within itself and coherent with itself on all sides) so that nothing manifest or hidden is left unknown (Wisdom 7:21). This is so that, truly, the mind of man may become what it ought to be: the image of the All-Wise God.

Secondly, since wisdom is said to grant ***understanding and knowledge to youth*** (Proverbs 1:4), and ***her ways are ways of pleasantness*** (Proverbs 3:17), it is clear that studies of wisdom ought to be without the rough paths and thorns of difficulties—level, unimpeded, accessible to all, and pure enticements for the soul.

Thirdly, since the path of wisdom is said to be a ***shining light*** (Proverbs 4:18), it follows that it must be safe from the darkness of errors.

Fourthly, wisdom is said to provide this use to man: ***that he may understand his way*** (Proverbs 14:8) <note>The OCR cites Proverbs 4:18 again here, but the quote "intelligat viam suam" traditionally refers to Proverbs 14:8.</note> and ***all things that he does*** (Deuteronomy 29:9). It follows, therefore, that studies of wisdom ought to prepare minds for all the things by which this life is passed, in both doing and enduring.

Finally, wisdom is said to ***make men blessed*** (Proverbs 3:13), by leading them, truly, to God, the fountain of eternal happiness.

<summary>The author defines Wisdom (Pansophy) as a universal, accessible, and joyful pursuit that mirrors the omniscience of God and prepares a person for all aspects of life. He uses biblical citations from Proverbs and the Book of Wisdom to argue that education should be a "shining light" free from unnecessary difficulty or error.</summary>

<keywords>Wisdom, Pansophy, Image of God, Universal Knowledge, Christian Education, Solomon, Blessedness</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression from page 6, where the author argued that Wisdom should be the ultimate goal of education. Having defined Wisdom’s divine characteristics, he now turns to the practical application of study, arguing that if education does not lead to God, it is a failure. He begins a critique of the contemporary schooling system, which he finds inefficient and ill-suited to human life.</meta>

<page-num>(7)</page-num>

Necessarily, therefore, the study of Wisdom ought to instill stimuli for seeking God, show the paths for finding Him, and prepare the bonds of love for embracing Him most closely; otherwise, that whole apparatus <note>The "apparatus" refers to the system of learning and scholarly tools.</note> will be useless. For if one has strayed from this goal in the end, it would have been better to know nothing, or even never to have been born.

Let us see, however, whether the customary practice of studies provides these things in such a way that, if we notice anything lacking, a thought about reform can more easily be undertaken.

<margin>Common literary studies are not proportioned to their goal.</margin>
Regarding *learning*, as it now swells the schools and such as they propose: there are many complaints from many people that it is a *prolix* thing for the brevity of a fleeting life; *laborious* for the capacity of ordinary minds; too *narrow* for the vastness of reality; and in many ways *maimed* and *defective* for the subtlety and solidity of the truth of things. Wiser men have even noted that it does *not sufficiently correspond to its own end*: indeed, it rarely reaches the solid uses of life, mostly ending in opinions, quarrels, and smoke <note>original Latin: "fumos," used here to mean vanity or empty pretension.</note>. We must show that all these things are not said just for the sake of talking, but are entirely so, before we come to the investigation of remedies. Specifically, that the studies of wisdom, as they are now arranged and taught in the schools, *are not proportioned*—

I. *To our life, because of their lengthiness; according to that saying, "Art is long, life is short."* <note>original Latin: "Ars longa, vita brevis est." A famous aphorism originally from Hippocrates, suggesting that there is too much to learn and too little time to learn it.</note>

<meta>catchword: II. To the Mind</meta>

He begins a numbered list of these deficiencies, starting with the mismatch between the vastness of knowledge and the shortness of human life.</summary>

The author is listing the specific ways in which modern studies fail to align with their proper goal, focusing here on the overwhelming volume of information and the resulting fragmentation of knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>(8)</page-num>

II. To our **natural talent**, because of its **Difficulty**.
III. To **things themselves**, because of their **frequent Deviation** <note>The author suggests that current studies often wander away from the true nature or essence of the subjects being studied.</note>.
IV. To the **use of life**, because of the **Alienation** of almost everything that is taught from the things that happen in common life.
V. To **God Himself**, because of the **Lack of Subordination** of all things; they are not sufficiently directed toward this goal of eternity.

<margin>1. *Because of Prolixity*</margin>
I take the first argument regarding **Prolixity** <note>The quality of being unnecessarily long-winded or wordy; here, it refers to the overwhelming length and volume of the curriculum.</note> from the common confession of everyone. For who is there who does not sing the chant of Hippocrates: *Life is short, art is long*? <note>original Latin: "Vita brevis, ars longa." A famous aphorism from the ancient Greek physician Hippocrates, lamenting that a human lifetime is insufficient to master a complex craft or science.</note>

The second argument comes from the grandeur of the books in which these matters are described. Good God, what vast volumes have already been built up on almost every single subject! If these were piled together into one, they would create such heaps that thousands of years would be needed just to read through them.

Thirdly, the reality itself teaches us that doctrines have been spread out beyond the measure of our capacity. Among the many learned men who fill the world today, you would find barely one in a hundred—or even one in a thousand—who has even tasted <term>universal learning</term> <note>The ideal of "Pansophia" or "Universal Wisdom," where a person understands the interconnectedness of all knowledge.</note>, and who could reasonably discuss everything that occurs in the works of God or in human affairs. So rare are those who are <term>all-wise</term> <note>original Greek: "pansophoi."</note> and <term>all-learned</term> <note>original Greek: "pammatheis."</note>, that even those who are simply <term>widely-learned</term> <note>original Greek: "polymathis."</note> or <term>Polyhistors</term> <note>A scholar who is exceptionally well-read in many different subjects.</note> are seen as a miracle.

Because of this, we see that commonly practiced "fragmentation" of learning: one person selects this art or science, another selects that, while others they do not even [greet] from the...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the current state of education, specifically addressing the extreme specialization and fragmentation of knowledge. He argues against the common excuse that the human mind is too limited to grasp the foundations of all sciences.</meta>

...having greeted them only at the threshold <note>original Latin: "limine quidem salutatis." A metaphor for having only a superficial or introductory acquaintance with a subject.</note>. You may find theologians who have barely deigned to look at philosophy; and again, philosophers who hold theology in no regard. Legal experts are, for the most part, untouched by any concern for natural matters, nor do physicians care for the distinctions of law and equity. Each faculty has established for itself separate kingdoms, without common, certain, and immovable foundations and laws that would bind them all together. 

But even within philosophy itself, one person chooses this for himself, another that. Some wish to be natural scientists while being most ignorant of **mathematics** <note>original: "Matheseos." In this period, mathematics was considered the essential foundation for understanding the physical laws of nature.</note>; and vice versa. Others wish to be moral philosophers while being equipped with no knowledge of natural things. Logicians, rhetoricians, and poets wish to be heard, yet they are entirely devoid <note>original: "expertissimi." Here used in the sense of being "most lacking" or "without experience" (from *expers*), rather than being "experts."</note> of the **real sciences** <term>real sciences (scientiae reales): The study of actual "things" or "realities" (like nature or physics) as opposed to "vocal" or "instrumental" sciences like grammar and logic which deal with words.</term>. 

Who does not know it is so? And who does not notice that such a distribution of arts, sciences, and faculties among so many people comes from the presupposition that it is impossible for everything to be grasped conveniently enough by the intellect of a single man? As if, indeed, God had made man—the master of things <note>original: "rerum Dominum." A traditional view of humanity's role in the created order.</note>—so ill-suited to those very things. Not that I dream that one man can excel in all things; but I am certain that for anyone equipped with even a mediocre intellect, it is both possible and necessary to be able to know all things to the extent of necessity, and to excel in one's own profession.

First, there are the public complaints, not only of the boys who are learning the arts, but also of the men who teach them. Second, the schools themselves are full of shouting, beatings, and welts. 

For certainly, Holy Scripture commends wisdom and the study of wisdom to us as a delight. The ancients seem to have felt the same, as they called the workshops of letters **literary games** and **schools** <note>original: "ludos literarios & scholas." The Latin "ludus" means both "game/play" and "school," while the Greek "schole" originally meant "leisure."</note> (that is, places of leisure). What monster is this then, that for a long time now we have seen *leisure* turned into *gall* <note>original: "$\sigma\chi o\lambda\dot{\alpha}\varsigma$ in $\chi\acute{o}\lambda\alpha\varsigma$" (scholas in cholas). A Greek pun: "scholas" means leisure/school, while "cholas" refers to bile or bitterness.</note>, and games turned into torture? From where, I ask, can this come, if not from the troublesome difficulty of making progress? 

Indeed, that difficulty makes the cultivation of studies not only laborious, but also causes the greater part of those who are applied to that cultivation to contract a nausea. They hurry away from schools as if from **workhouses** <note>original: "pistrinis." A pistrinum was a grain mill, often used as a place of punishment or hard labor for slaves; here it serves as a metaphor for the drudgery of the classroom.</note> with the greatest eagerness, never to return to studies which they have experienced as thickets of thorns—even though they ought to be gardens and the common delights of the human race. Finally, **practice** <term>practice (praxis): The practical application of knowledge or a craft, as opposed to theoretical study.</term> teaches the same. For who does not know that a language is more easily learned by use among a crowd of people than through several years in a school? Regarding the acquisition of some expertise in actual things, the judgment is almost exactly the same.

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the current state of learning. Having previously described the misery and "torture" of contemporary schooling, he now argues that the sheer number of conflicting opinions in philosophy and theology is proof that true, certain knowledge has yet to be reached. <page-num>11</page-num>

...driven to the brink by the awareness of their own **imperfection**, they have dared to proclaim that *nothing can be known* <note>original: "Nihil scire posse"</note>—at least, not with certainty and without error. Those very people who mocked this idea as a delusion, claiming for themselves a certain knowledge of things, have almost confirmed the truth of that saying by their own example. For whatever one of the **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophorum"</note> asserted, another soon destroyed; even today, there is no end to these perplexities. The entire world of learning is full of dissensions, contradictions, and conflicts. What is this, if not a testament to a truth that is wavering everywhere? 

Indeed, using this very argument in his work ***Josephus Against Apion*** <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Romano-Jewish historian; "Against Apion" is his defense of Judaism against Greek critiques.</note>, he contrasts the truth of **Hebrew wisdom** with the **vanity of the Greeks**; for the former always remained unified, while the latter splintered into sects. For there can be only one simple way to be right, while there are a thousand forms of error. 

Since, therefore, even in our own age, so many sects are springing up in **Theology** and **Philosophy**, and so many opinions—differing from one another as far as the heavens—are nurtured and most stubbornly defended: what is this but the clearest proof that whatever learning our century boasts of is still submerged in the deep shadows of ignorance? For we must assume, in the judgment of charity <note>The "judgment of charity" is a theological principle of assuming the best intentions in others—here, that scholars are not lying, but are simply lost.</note>, that no one errs knowingly or willingly, or acts as those who are **rebellious against the light**, as **Job** speaks in **chapter 24, verse 13**. Finally, a testament to this are the many things coming into the light <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "venientes" (coming) indicates the sentence continues on the next page.</note> every day...

...the books coming forth—what are they, after all, but sparks struck out from the collision of wits <note>original: "ingeniorum collisione"</note> and scattered here and there, each one hoping that it will kindle a flame? Yet the continued shadows of disagreements and doubts show that, so far, no true torches have been lit, nor has the brightest Sun of truth arisen.

<margin>IV. Because of its scant usefulness for the business of life.</margin>
*We have great witnesses to the fact that learning <note>original: "literas"</note>, as it is commonly taught, is not sufficiently adapted to the uses of common life. **Philosophy** has been confined to the schools, says an illustrious man, and there is no one to recall it to common use. For it bristles with thorns and is entirely occupied with tying insoluble knots and unraveling what it has woven itself; it offers stony bread <note>A reference to Seneca’s critique of logic-chopping that yields no moral "nourishment."</note> that breaks the teeth and tires the mind with prickly trifles. And another says: The **study of Philosophy** is now nothing but a busy and hindered leisure, producing little profit. Squirrels are shut in a round cage that moves in a circle; where, though they throw themselves forward continuously, they do not advance from their place. We do the same when we philosophize: we learn little with much labor; and that little does not make us better, but often worse. **Experience** shows this is most truly said.*

For not only do the <term>literati<note>Literati refers to the educated class, specifically those trained in Latin and the liberal arts.</note></term> rarely excel the <term>illiterati<note>The "unlettered" or those without formal academic schooling.</note></term> in the pursuit of virtues (which are the basis of social life), but in the skillful management of affairs, they are mostly [surpassed] by them...

...[they] show) that by a public edict, he banished all study of **Metaphysics** from his kingdom. He did this so that no bookseller would dare import such books under penalty of confiscation, and no subject would even dare to read them, lest the kingdom be enthralled by a new kind of **Barbarism** <note>In this context, 'Barbarism' refers to a corruption of clear language and thought through overly complex or meaningless academic jargon.</note>, and lest he find, in the management of affairs, vain disputers and **glossators** <term>glossators<note>Scholars who write brief explanations (glosses) in the margins or between the lines of a text, often criticized for obscuring the original meaning of the source.</note></term> instead of practical men of action. This is attested by *Johannes Angelius Werdenhagen* <note>original: "Iohannes Angelius Werdenhagen," a 17th-century German philosopher and diplomat.</note>.

The study of **Logic** and **Rhetoric** should have approached the business of life more closely, since they claim to offer an exposition of reason and speech—the two bonds by which human affairs are held together. But the evidence of reality itself most clearly testifies that the testimony of *Jacobus Acontius* <note>original: "Iacobi Acontii," referring to Giacomo Aconcio (1492–1566), an Italian humanist who advocated for logical method and religious tolerance.</note> is very true. He says: "The number of **Dialecticians** <term>dialecticians<note>Those trained in the art of logical argument and formal debate.</note></term> is frequent everywhere; but if you observe their writings and disputations, you will find nothing less than true dialectics." *Likewise*: "You may see many well-instructed in the rules of **Rhetoric**, whose speech—though it may be proper, ornate, elegant, and abundant—nevertheless lacks the power and strength needed to actually persuade." The same judgment applies to the other **Arts and Sciences**: we are more occupied *by* them than we are prepared by them to skillfully handle the duties of life.

...lead [away from] **GOD**; what need is there for witnesses? The profanity of our **Academies** <term>academies<note>In this era, 'Academies' refers to the universities, which the author views as centers of moral decay rather than true learning.</note></term> is greater than any witness; it is open to all, full of luxury, pride, gluttony, brawls, and every kind of insolence. 

Furthermore, the very **doctrine** gathered there—alas—for how many does it become nothing but a **whetstone** <note>original: "cos," a stone used for sharpening tools; here meaning that education only serves to make their malice "sharper."</note> of malice, an instrument for practicing iniquity throughout their whole lives? Thus, concerning the greater number of our scholars, what God laments is most true: *They are wise in doing evil, but how to do good they know not. Jeremiah 14:22.* <note>In modern Bibles, this quote is found in Jeremiah 4:22.</note> And for such people, their own wisdom is a guide to destruction. For this reason comes that voice of God: — *Not many wise are chosen, because the wisdom of this world is foolishness before God. 1 Corinthians 1.* <note>A paraphrase of 1 Corinthians 1:26 and 3:19.</note>

We, indeed, lest we feel ourselves pressured by that voice of God, confidently interpret it as referring to **pagan** <note>original: "gentili"</note> wisdom; yet, we do not apply ourselves to any other kind of wisdom. For in truth, that very wisdom which the schools have offered until now is drawn from **paganism** <note>original: "gentilismo"</note> and infected with serpentine venom; it affects only the **knowledge** <term>knowledge<note>The author uses 'scientia' here, often contrasted with 'sapientia' (wisdom), implying a factual but spiritually hollow knowledge.</note></term> of good and evil, which *puffs up*, rather than working toward **charity** <term>charity<note>From the Latin *charitas*, referring to the theological concept of divine love for God and neighbor, rather than simple almsgiving.</note></term>, which *builds up*. 

And because, through our inherited perversity, we are always more ready to excuse our sins than to amend them, it happens moreover that we flatter ourselves by using the **corruption** of nature as an excuse: we deem it impossible to be transformed because we are depraved. As if indeed not...

<summary>The author condemns European universities as centers of vice and "pagan" wisdom that puff up the ego without producing moral goodness. He argues that scholars use their learning as a tool for evil and use the concept of "human corruption" as an excuse to avoid spiritual transformation.</summary>

It is true, therefore, that the common <term>study of letters<note>The term *literarum studia* refers to the traditional humanities and classical curriculum of the 17th century.</note></term> is:
*I. An endless preoccupation.*
*II. A most tangled distraction of minds.*
*III. A perpetual training-ground for errors.*
*IV. A hindrance to the business of life.*
*V. Finally, a multifaceted opportunity for wandering away from God, which is most to be deplored.*

<margin>A deliberation on remedies.</margin>
Since certain excellent men long ago began to pity this calamitous state of learning and of scholars, various people have attempted to seek remedies for those diseases in various ways. Some, indeed, urged that the entire apparatus of ancient wisdom borrowed from the **pagans** <note>original: *gentilibus*; referring here to the heavy reliance on pre-Christian Greek and Roman philosophers, specifically Aristotle, in university education.</note> should be cast aside; others insisted that a selection must be applied, and that the **Arts** and **Philosophy** should be used only sparingly; some began to strive for a certain "transfusion" and transformation of all the sciences; some in one way, some in another. And truly, it is more than necessary that whoever can offer any counsel or help in this matter should do so. Firstly, so that such a tiny bit of wisdom, such as common education provides, should not cost so much and such great sweat; or certainly, if labors must be spent,

...so that they may yield a more abundant harvest. Next, it is so that we might rescue ourselves and the pursuit of learning from the scorn of the unlettered masses. For when the common people see those who are supposedly superior to them in the name of <term>erudition<note>original: *eruditio*; here meaning formal academic training or scholarship.</note></term>, yet notice that these scholars do not excel at all in practical skill, moral integrity, or the zeal of piety—and indeed often fail to even follow in the footsteps of simpler folk—they either dissolve into laughter or are moved to contempt for the **world of letters**. Finally, we must act so that we do not incur the wrath of God, who wishes to restore His image within us through the heavenly gift of **wisdom**, but who finds us failing to manage such a divine gift for His glory.

<margin>An investigation into the causes of such great evil.</margin>
But in truth, because any attempt to cure a disease is reckless if the cause of the illness is not yet understood, let us see if we can penetrate to the roots of this great evil weighing down the **world of letters** <note>original: *res literaria*; the collective body of scholarship, literature, and academic life.</note> through a reliable inquiry and expose them. From there, the transition to remedies will be safer. I will therefore state what I believe to be the cause underlying each of the evils we have already listed, and at the same time, I will explain what should be offered by way of a remedy.

<margin>The triple cause of wordiness. 1. An excessive mixture of unnecessary things.</margin>
*The wordiness of studies* <note>original: *Prolixitas studiorum*; referring to the excessive length, complexity, and tedious nature of the academic curriculum.</note>, as they are commonly handled, seems to arise from three sources. First, from the failure to distinguish the necessary from the unnecessary. For as it happens according to the complaint of **Seneca** <note>original: *Senecæ*; Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Roman Stoic philosopher who famously critiqued the education system of his day by saying we learn for the schoolroom rather than for life.</note>: we are ignorant of the necessary things because we learn the unnecessary. Finally, all the roundabout complexities of things and words, which do not bring forth the kernel <note>The "kernel" or "nucleus" refers to the essential truth or core meaning of a subject.</note> but merely grasp at it while it is hidden behind a thousand layers. And yet, most schoolbooks are filled with such things, and for this reason, they distract and stretch out the youth with mostly useless and unnecessary material. Is it then any wonder that rarely anyone reaches a more solid state of scholarship?

The remedy, therefore, will be to construct a *Nursery of Scholarship* <note>original: *Eruditionis Seminarium*; the Latin *seminarium* literally means a seed-plot or nursery where plants are raised from seeds before being transplanted.</note> from the *most solid things and words*, which will provide nothing but the truest utility. For you will gather just as much fruit if you sow an acre of field with one measure of pure wheat as you would if you scattered chaff mixed with wheat over ten acres with tenfold labor, having added ten measures of chaff.

<margin>II. Confusion of the things to be learned.</margin>
Second, academic labors <note>original: *literarii labores*</note> are a kind of inextricable labyrinth in which most people wander without end. This is caused by a confusion of studies: a scattered variety of things and a certain ocean of information in which it is easier to be swallowed up than to drain it dry. This happens because the things that must be learned have not yet been reduced to a sufficiently certain and evident order. It is a necessary truth that things which are scattered appear to be many, while those collected together are compressed into a small space.

The remedy will be found if everything to be learned—from the most important to the smallest detail—is arranged in such a clear sequence that students <note>original: *literarum candidati*; literally "candidates of letters," referring to those aspiring to become scholars.</note> have the material as readily before them as the fingers on their own hands. They should be able to see through the subject from the very beginning, perceiving both the middle and the end, confident that the whole ocean of scholarship will be navigated by this very **Ship** <note>The author uses the metaphor of a ship to represent a systematic method or textbook that carries the student safely through a vast subject.</note> which they board, arriving at last at the harbor of desired perfection.

<margin>III. Excessive pursuit of minor details.</margin>
Third, the monstrously sprawling nature of academic studies is caused by the useless pursuit of every tiny detail <note>original: *minutiarum*; referring to trivialities or overly specific points of grammar or logic that do not contribute to core understanding.</note> by certain people. In matters of both things and words—since there are infinite "playful tricks" <note>original: *lusus*; the author suggests that nature and the human mind produce endless variations that are not always essential to core knowledge.</note> and accidents of both nature and the intellect—there is no end or limit to such details. And should it be a wonder if someone felt sick and grew dizzy? Indeed, nothing is so easy that it does not become difficult if you do it against your will. How, then, can letters be easy to learn when they must be studied amidst trembling? This is not how it happens in the learning of any mechanical art. Severity necessarily leads to fear, and fear necessarily confuses and entangles the mind; so that it knows not where it is, and if the mind is a little weaker, it suffers a kind of vertigo.

Therefore, there is need of an art to capture, entice, and soothe minds. This art will consist partly of the kindness of the teachers and partly of the wisdom of the method; so that the study of letters becomes an enticement for talents and appears to be mere play.

<margin>2. Superficial explanation of things.</margin>

Second, a good part of the difficulty lies in the fact that things are not taught to students through <term>autopsiam; first-hand observation or seeing for oneself</term>, but through roundabout narratives, by which the images of things are impressed upon the intellect with difficulty, and stick weakly in the memory, so that they either vanish again easily or are confused in various ways.

<margin>3. The method is still far too imperfect.</margin>

The third and greatest weight of difficulty comes from the method by which the <term>Sciences and Arts; *Scientiæ & Artes*, the core subjects of a classical education</term> are commonly handed down, which is not sufficiently suited either to the things being studied or to the human intellect. Everyone indeed cries out that we must proceed in the order of nature: from the prior to the posterior, from the general to the more specific, and finally from the known to the unknown. But who delivers on this? As yet we see nothing of the kind: <term>reversals of order; *Hystera protera*, a Greek term for "the last things first"</term> truly darken all the Arts and Sciences, if you except **Mathematics**. 

For if the books we have were to lead minds gently from the known to the unknown in a never-interrupted pace, then readers eager for knowledge would be led to even the most unknown things as certainly as anyone equipped with feet can be led up even the highest cliff, provided that steps are built into it. Since this does not happen, no one can grasp what those books teach—unless they are remarkably gifted, penetrating the subjects with their own sharpness and lifting themselves up by their own strength, or unless the same things are hammered home by a living voice in various ways,

The remedy will be this: a restructuring of all <term>Sciences and Arts; *Scientiæ & Artes*, the core subjects of a classical education</term> so that one begins everywhere with the most familiar things, and a slow, step-by-step progression is made toward the more obscure. In this way, the first elements provide light to the second, the second to the third, and the third to the fourth, carrying that light forward until the very end—exactly as one link in a chain catches and pulls the next. If we can show how this ought to be done in this work, we shall consider our labor well-rewarded.

<margin>The three-fold cause of Truth’s struggle throughout all literature.</margin>
*The truest cause for Truth failing us everywhere throughout the whole circle of the sciences can be identified as three-fold: namely,*

I. *The tearing apart of the sciences.*

III. *An attention to words and style that is partly neglectful and partly excessively luxurious.* <note>The author is critiquing both sloppy writing and "purple prose" that prioritizes fancy words over actual facts.</note>

Much less have I seen the entire apparatus of human intellect so constructed from its own certain principles—principles unmoved by eternal truth—that all things were mutually linked together from the first to the last without any gap in truth. 

For perhaps no one has yet attempted to establish universal principles of things, equalized in every proportion to one another, so as to surround the variety of things flowing in every direction with certain limits of reason. In this way, through a universal harmony proportioned among all things, the hidden yet unchangeable and invincible truth of things might reveal itself. 

This, I say, no one seems to have sought yet: to open some universal path for knowing the truth of things through the most universal principles of knowledge and those methods of properly deducing them all the way to the final conclusions. 

The <term>Metaphysicians; scholars who study the fundamental nature of reality and existence</term> hum to themselves; the <term>Physicists; those who study the natural world and physical laws</term> applaud themselves; the **Astronomers** lead their own dances; the **Ethicists** fix their own laws; the **Politicians** fashion their own foundations; the **Mathematicians** triumph for themselves; the **Theologians** reign by themselves. Indeed, in almost every individual faculty and science, individuals establish particular principles for themselves from which they may build and defend their own opinions; and [regardless of...] <note>original: "súſque déque", a Latin idiom meaning "up and down" or "indifferently." ...foundation, which others derive from their own presuppositions. Yet, it is impossible to unearth truth when it is scattered hidden through all things in such a way, waiting to be gathered from every side. For while each person attends only to himself and his own imagination, dissonance is not avoided—just as in a choir of musicians, if each were to sing for himself, having neglected the common rhythm; or who would believe that a well-ordered republic could exist where one does not live by common laws, but each acts according to his own whim? 

Do we not see that the branches of a tree cannot live unless they all equally draw sap from a common trunk and common roots? And can it be hoped that the branches of wisdom can be torn away while their life—that is, the truth—remains intact? To be sure, can someone be a *Physicist* <note>original: *Physicus*; a student of natural science</note> who is not also a *Metaphysician* <note>one who studies the fundamental nature of reality</note>? Or an *Ethicist* who was not previously a *Physicist* (at least certainly imbued with the knowledge of human nature)? Or a *Logician* who is not skilled in the real sciences? Or a *Theologian*, *Jurist* <note>original: *Jure-consultus*; a legal expert</note>, or *Physician* who was not first a *Philosopher*? Or an *Orator* or a *Poet* who is not all of those things at once? 

He deprives himself of light, of a guiding hand, and of a standard, whoever removes from himself any of the things that can be known. Certainly, *Astronomers* (to add this as an example) would never have dared to introduce and defend such contrary and absurd hypotheses if [they had proceeded] from the common [foundation] of truth...

Indeed, the Philosophy of *Plato* <note>The influential Greek philosopher (c. 427–347 BC) known for his theory of "Forms" or "Ideas."</note> seemed elegant and divine: but how much has the school of the *Peripatetics* <note>The followers of Aristotle, so named because they allegedly taught while walking (*peripatētikos*).</note> convicted it of being mere empty speculation? The Philosophy of *Aristotle* <note>Plato’s student, whose empirical and logical works dominated medieval thought.</note> seemed sufficiently polished to itself: but Christian Philosophers have discovered that it does not sufficiently correspond to the divine scriptures, nor to the truth of things in all respects. Astronomers, with their eccentric spheres and epicycles <note>term: *epicycles*; complex circular paths used in the old geocentric system to explain why planets sometimes appear to move backward.</note>, congratulated themselves for so many centuries, but they were driven out by *Copernicus*. *Copernicus* <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proposed that the Sun, not the Earth, is the center of the universe.</note> plausibly constructed his new Astronomy from optical reasoning, yet the principles of Physics—of immovable truth—admit it in no way <note>In the 17th century, many felt that while Copernicus's math worked, it contradicted the physical "truth" that the heavy Earth should remain stationary.</note>. *Gilbert*, carried away by his study of the *Magnet*, <note>William Gilbert (1544–1603), who discovered that the Earth itself acts as a giant magnet.</note> wished to derive the whole of Philosophy from the magnet: but he did so with a most evident injury to the principles of Physics. *Campanella* <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian friar and philosopher who sought to reform science based on sensation.</note> almost triumphs in his *Natural Philosophy* by taking up again the principles of the ancient philosopher *Parmenides* <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who argued that the world is a single, unchanging substance.</note>: but he is cast down by *Galileo Galilei* with a single optical tube <note>original: *tubo optico*; the telescope.</note>. And why should I review others?

...it might happen that they collapse into contradictions, causing not only an obvious hindrance to the listeners but also the loss of truth itself, which is for the most part lost through arguing. For when useless matters are proposed (not derived from a universal foundation), they cannot help but appear obscure and ambiguous, breeding uncertainty in the minds of the audience. To draw these listeners into agreement, principles are adopted that have been snatched from any available source—principles neither well-known, nor agreed upon by others, nor containing indubitable truth in themselves. In fact, they are subject to various limitations and exceptions (as are most of the rules of common *<term>Philosophy</term>* <note>original: "Philosophiæ"; here referring to the traditional academic study of logic, physics, and ethics</note> and *<term>Theology</term>* <note>original: "Theologiæ"; the study of religious doctrine</note>). What follows, then, except contradiction and the most troublesome contentions? It becomes exhausting to hear such hesitations and disputes over matters that are inherently clear.

Therefore, the entire matter must be organized differently. We must ensure that the truth of things, arriving in a clear light, can escape the pitfalls of hesitation and the arrows of contradiction, and struggle through every error. We believe this is impossible unless the rays of truth scattered throughout all things are gathered into one, so that the same single symmetry may appear in all things—whether they are perceived by the senses, the intellect, or through divine revelation. This symmetry is provided only by the principles of knowledge (for what can they be except three: *<term>Sense</term>*, *<term>Rea-*

<meta>catchword: son (Latin: -tio),</meta>

<summary>The author argues that current academic debates are fruitless because they lack a universal foundation, leading to the loss of truth. He proposes a new system that unifies knowledge from three sources: the senses, the intellect, and divine revelation.</summary>

...understanding, and divine *Revelation*), an equalization and a consolidation into unshakeable strength will be able to reveal [the truth] and, through this, remove controversies concerning things. For once the foundation of things is uncovered, it will necessarily become clear either where one of the opposing parties is in error, or the reason for the diversity in branches of knowledge—namely, that perhaps each side is thinking and speaking correctly from a different perspective <note>original: "diverso respectu"</note>, even though they do not understand one another because the foundation of that differing perspective has not been grasped. Certainly, it is by this way alone that the errors which variously besiege the minds of mortals can be conquered and their minds set in clear light; there is no other way. For it is necessary that once the clear sun of truth has risen, the mists of infinite opinions should vanish of their own accord; indeed, even the very darkness of *Atheism* <note>the denial of God's existence, viewed here as a form of intellectual "fog" or confusion</note> may at last be dispelled with God’s help.

<margin>2. The instability of methods.</margin>

I have called the second cause of truth’s widespread instability the *instability of methods* <note>original: "Methodorum volubilitatem"; refers to the lack of a consistent, objective system for organizing knowledge</note>. This occurs because writers do not bind themselves entirely to the things themselves so as to convey them immutably, exactly as they are structured within themselves. Instead, they twist and pull things in a thousand directions to fit some fictional and self-devised sense of order. This is nothing less than distorting things in the mind’s eye and transfiguring them from their natural form into something else. And in such a case, what can appear in place of the facts but "monsters" <note>intellectual distortions or unnatural conclusions</note>? Furthermore, a method that is entirely parallel to the things themselves...

(29)

...cannot be achieved, it is certain, unless all things are brought back to a harmony within the understanding <note>original: "intellectu"</note> as great as that by which they bind and connect themselves outside the understanding. I have just pointed out that many philosophical systems <note>original: "Philosophemata"</note> have been devised up to now according to personal whim, only to be torn down again by others. Let it be understood that the same applies to the dogmas of Theology, or even entire methodologies: they can be built up and then destroyed because they were fashioned not according to the unchanging standard of things, but according to the **leaden rule** <note>original: "plumbeam regulam"; a "leaden rule" is a classical metaphor for a flexible, unreliable standard that bends to fit the user's bias rather than providing a rigid, objective measurement</note> of this or that person’s fancy.

<margin>The Remedy.</margin>
Would that all these vague methods—those devised at the whim of wandering minds until now, and those yet to be devised—might perish! Then, at last, all things might begin to be taught according to a single, natural order. And it is such a *Christian Philosophy*, or rather a *Pansophy* <term>Universal Wisdom; a system of knowledge that integrates all fields into a single, divinely-ordered whole</term> that we teach must be sought. In this system, everything rises from immovable principles toward immovable truth, and all parts connect in such perpetual harmony that this work of the mind is as indestructible as the very **machine of the world** <note>original: "mundi machina"; a common 17th-century metaphor for the universe as a divinely designed, perfectly functioning mechanism</note> itself. Truly, just as the universe of things is not ruled by our own will but proceeds unchangeably according to its own laws, so its mirror, *Pansophy*, should be presented through a method from which no one can depart—even if they were bursting with the desire to change it for change's sake. This will be achieved if all things are presented demonstratively through...
