(30)

...proximate causes and effects. But for this purpose, it will be absolutely necessary for things to be taught and learned not through external testimonies about things—that is, traditions—but through the things themselves. For authorities can just as easily cast a veil <note>original: "fucum facere"; literally to apply dye or rouge, used here to mean disguise or deception</note> over things as they can illuminate them; at the very least, they certainly distract the learner and call them away from the things toward themselves. The things themselves, however, cannot impress themselves upon the senses in any way other than as they truly are. Or, if the senses should fail in any way, reason must be applied, equipped with certain standards, so that it cannot go astray. Finally, where reason also leaves a gap, divine revelation should come to its aid. These three principles of knowledge must be established as the foundations of **Pansophy** <term>Universal Wisdom; a system of knowledge integrating all fields into a divinely-ordered whole</term>, so that hereafter, whatever might slip from the mouth or pen of any philosopher or theologian is not taken for an oracle. Instead, we should set aside our spectacles <note>original: "perspicillis"; a metaphor for the biased lenses of traditional scholarship</note> and gaze upon the things themselves, seeking out through careful examination what they admit themselves to be or not to be. For indeed, there are some things thought of quite lightly even by great men, which nevertheless those who admire such men are accustomed to worship indiscriminately. It would be easy to show this with many examples, but I refrain, hoping that once a brighter torch has been lit for the truth, infinite instances of this sort will be revealed of their own accord.

The third thing prejudicial to the truth is, as I have said, the carelessness or extravagance of the speech by which things are explained. And extravagance, indeed

By these means, things usually take on an appearance different from their natural one. And then, what is it but a veneer <note>original: "fucus"; literally a seaweed dye or cosmetic rouge, used here to mean a deceptive disguise or artificial coloring</note>? Surely the truth of things wishes to be viewed in pure light, without any added color. It is, however, a neglect of speech if obscure words and expressions are used, or if they are in a language not understood (as **Greek** terms are to Latins and the rest of us), or finally, if opinions of less than certain truth are sold as the standard of truth. Books of Philosophers and Theologians—it is shameful to say, but true if we confess it—teem with such things.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I affirm, therefore, that the cause that creates and preserves error in the realm of learning is that unfortunate **trio** <term>triga; a team of three horses, used here to describe three interconnected problems</term> I have mentioned: a diverse **Fragmentation** <note>original: "Frustillationem"; the act of breaking something into scraps or small bits</note> of things to be known; a diverse **Transformation**; and a diverse **Encrustation** <note>original: "Incrustationem"; like a layer of plaster or coating that hides the true material beneath</note>. For who can know things as they are, when they are only offered in fragments? When they are not in their proper **sequence**? When they are not shown in their own face? For when the symmetry of all things is unknown, it is very easy to go astray in any particular object.

(32)

Is it not too obvious that when the sequence of things is not clearly seen, one finds a labyrinth everywhere? It is more than easy for the eyes to be deceived when any matter presents itself in a color not its own. From this comes that shortage of light in such an enlightened age (as it delights to be called), so that just as *Tantalus* <note>A figure from Greek mythology punished with eternal hunger and thirst; water and fruit would recede whenever he tried to reach them.</note> seeks water amidst water, so we lack light within light, books within books, and true learning among the learned. I pass over in silence that quite fertile mother of errors: the passion for following a specific faction. For not without reason did *Galen* <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose views dominated European medicine for centuries.</note> say that those who devote themselves to a sect become both deaf and blind, so that they not only fail to hear and see what others easily perceive, but they even refuse to speak truer things and resist those teaching them, no differently than the drunken *Lapiths* <note>A people from Greek myth; at a famous wedding feast, they became drunk and started a legendary, violent brawl.</note>, who repel with fists and heels the surgeon preparing to apply a healing hand to their wounds.

<sig>C 5</sig>

...should imbue their minds; however, these subjects are reserved for the Academies <note>Referring to universities or higher institutions of learning.</note>, so that students, possessing a more mature judgment and being now equal to the weight of the subjects, might progress more successfully. But it usually happens that as the heat of early youth fades, everyone rushes toward their chosen professional faculty <term>facultatem; the specific branch of higher study, such as law, medicine, or theology</term>, having already forgotten their preparatory foundations. Indeed, almost all future theologians, politicians, and physicians knowingly and willingly leap over the study of Metaphysics, Physics, and Mathematics as if these were a useless delay to them. This is a great error, since a solid judgment cannot be obtained except from a solid foundation of learning.

The remedy, therefore, will be to present all things to youth in a timely manner throughout their lives, and to establish serious exercises as rehearsals for more serious matters. For just as a craftsman is only made by crafting, a scribe by writing, and a debater by debating, so too must boys become human by practicing human affairs. In this way, nothing can happen in life that they have not already envisioned and practiced in school. Indeed, all of philosophy must be so prepared that it is a living image of reality and a quiet preparation of the soul for the business of life.

<margin>Nor do they lead enough toward God.</margin>

Finally, is it any wonder that learning does not sufficiently lead its pupils toward God? This majesty shows us, by its own brilliance, that all lower things are nothing but the shadow of the true light. There, one may finally rest as if in an immovable center of peace and the goal of all desires, and immerse ourselves in eternal <term>bliss</term> <note>original: "beatitudine"</note> within that fountain of life from which the springs of pleasures flow.

<margin>Why the author attempts this and with what confidence.</margin>
I, the least of all, behold, I come forward! I desire to bring into the world either a new torch, or at least new sparks for lighting a new torch. Although I am all too conscious of my own weakness, yet relying on the grace of God with the deepest humility, I trust that heavenly help will not be lacking for efforts directed toward His glory and the salvation of many.

<margin>It is time to build a splendid Temple of Wisdom.</margin>
The innermost parts of my heart are known to my God; I am not driven to this task by confidence in my own talent, but I am compelled by the stings of **conscience** <note>original: "conscientiae stimulis"</note>. If I can be of use in any way, I truly desire to be so—at least by providing encouragement to those who are capable of greater things. For indeed, after all the past centuries, it is truly time to both hope for and attempt greater things.

Pay attention, my dear Reader: so many investigations into the sciences until now, so many cultivations of the arts, so many searches into hidden secrets, so many clashes of intellects, and so many late-night studies <note>original: "lucubrationes"; specifically refers to work or study done by lamplight late into the night.</note> published on almost every subject; what, I ask, are these and what should they be, if not the raw materials of learning? They are gathered from various forests and quarries of the whole world, worked in different ways by different craftsmen, and brought together in various heaps. Yet, is this material not still scattered here and there? What indeed remains now, except that we finally apply a skillful hand to these piles of material, which must be reduced into their proper form? We must do this, of course, to build a universal **Temple of Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapientiæ Templum"</note>, truly splendid with the light of truth and the ornaments of harmony. Regarding this temple, that famous verse by Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC), a Roman poet and philosopher. This quote is from his work "On the Nature of Things" (De Rerum Natura), Book II.</note> might rightly be sung:

> *But nothing is sweeter than to hold the well-fortified heights,*
> *The temples built by the serene teaching of the wise,* <note>original: "Edita Doctrinâ sapientum Templa serenâ"</note>
> *From where you can look down upon others, and see them everywhere*
> *Wandering, and seeking a path for a straying life.*

Or rather, that saying of **Solomon** <note>A reference to Proverbs 9:1–3.</note> may be used: *Wisdom has built a house for herself; she has carved out seven columns, she has sacrificed her victims, mixed her wine, and prepared her table, and she has sent out her maidservants,* <note>The text ends here, following the catchword.</note>

God, who by this same voice also shows us—as we build *Pansophia* <note>Universal Wisdom</note>, the Temple of Wisdom—an exemplar: *Your Works and Your Voice*. Just as your works and words are true, and a living image of you, so may this work we are undertaking be a true and living image of your works and words.

<margin>But has nothing of this kind existed until now?</margin>
May those most learned men forgive me, for I now presume to pass judgment on their labors. The Encyclopedias <note>original: "Encyclopædias"; at this time, the term referred to the whole circle of learning, often appearing as massive, disjointed volumes</note> I have seen so far, even the most orderly ones, have seemed more like a chain elegantly woven from many links than an Automaton <term>Automato; a self-operating machine or clockwork device</term> skillfully designed with gears for motion and turning itself. They are more like a pile of wood, arranged with great care and elegant order, than a tree rising from its own roots, unfolding into branches and leaves by the power of an innate spirit, and bearing fruit. 

But we desire the living roots of the Sciences <note>original: "Scientiarum"</note> and Arts, a living tree, and living fruits. I speak of *Pansophia*, which should be a living image of the Universe, cohering with itself in every part, nourishing itself everywhere, and filling itself everywhere with fruit. This is to say (looking back at the goals established earlier), we desire a book of Pansophia to be established that would be:

*A solid Compendium <note>original: "Breviarium"; a concise summary or handbook</note> of universal learning:*
*A bright Torch for the human intellect:*
*A stable Standard of the truth of things.*

<vocab>Pansophia (Universal Wisdom), Encyclopædias (General Collections of Knowledge), Automato (Self-moving machine), Scientiarum (Sciences/Branches of Knowledge), Universi imago (Image of the Universe), Breviarium (Compendium/Summary), Intellectus humani (Human Intellect), Veritatis rerum (The Truth of things)</vocab>

<summary>The author defines Pansophia as an organic, self-sustaining system of knowledge, contrasting it with the static and disjointed "encyclopedias" of his contemporaries. He argues that true wisdom should function like a living tree or a clockwork machine rather than a mere collection of facts.</summary>

<page-num>(40)</page-num>

->*A fixed systematic Guide for the affairs of life.*<-
->*A blessed Stairway to God Himself.*<-

<margin>Pansophia <note>Universal Wisdom</note> *must reduce all things to Numbers, Measures, and Weights.*</margin>
Or (to express the desires of my heart in another way) I believe we must labor so that, just as God has arranged all things in *Number*, *Measure*, and *Weight* <note>original: "Numero, Mensura, & Pondere"; a reference to the Wisdom of Solomon 11:20 in the Apocrypha</note>, so for us:

**I.** Everything that exists anywhere—whether it will be or has been—should come under numbers, so that nothing escapes our knowledge.
**II.** That the measured proportion of all things—both in relation to the universe as a whole and to each other—might be clear to our eyes.
**III.** And finally, that the "weights" or importance of causes might be clearly visible, so that we may accurately perceive the truth of all things.

The first will ensure that Learning <note>original: "Eruditio"</note> is Universal: which is our primary aim.
The second: that it be clear and distinct; which we eagerly pursue.
The third: that it be true and solid, which we most greatly desire.

I say, we desire such a book to be constructed, which *on its own* may serve as a truly *Universal storehouse of Learning* <note>original: "Universalis Eruditionis promptuarium"</note>: in which you could rightly desire nothing more, and from the reading of which wisdom may flow into our minds of its own accord. This would happen because of the perpetual, clear, and distinct coherence of things, with everything rising from its own proper roots and veins, so it may appear that each thing is exactly as it is said...

<summary>The author proposes that Universal Wisdom (Pansophia) should mirror God's own creation by organizing all knowledge according to number, measure, and weight. He envisions a single, comprehensive book that allows wisdom to flow naturally into the reader's mind through its perfect internal logic.</summary>

...it is said <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "it may appear that each thing is expressed..."</note>, nor can it be otherwise than it is, because of that very immutable truth of things which connects itself everywhere. Everything must be presented accessibly <note>original: "popularly" or *populariter*; meaning in a way that is understandable to the general public, not just specialists</note>, because light must be brought into minds, not darkness. And everything must be presented solidly through the perpetual connection of causes and effects, because we must seek the foundation of truth, not the fictions of opinions. Namely, so that all things capable of being known—whether they be natural, moral, artificial, or even Metaphysical <note>The branch of philosophy dealing with first principles, including being, knowing, and the nature of the universe</note>—may be handed down with a certainty similar to the demonstrations we see proceeding among Mathematicians, so that not even a place for doubt is left. From which not only may those things that exist be known clearly and without error, but moreover, the sources of infinite thoughts, reasonings, and inventions may be opened up.

<margin>How much it matters that such a Pansophic <note>meaning "Universal Wisdom"</note> book be completed.</margin>

For since books are the instrument for transplanting wisdom—and a truly excellent instrument, like a rule that deviates in no part, prevents the hand of the craftsman from straying—if such an instrument for teaching and learning universal wisdom as we have described existed, not only could school youth be formed most skillfully (which...

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 43. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the "Pansophia" or universal directory of human understanding. This page picks up with the conclusion that such a system will allow humanity to witness God's wisdom and begins listing the foundational reasons why such a perfect method of knowledge is possible.</meta>

(44)

By this means, we are made observers of the Theater of God's Wisdom <note>original: *Theatri Sapientiæ Dei*; the "Theater of the World" or "Theater of Wisdom" was a popular 17th-century metaphor for the universe as a display of divine order</note>, and human minds cannot fail to be filled with joy and gladness, so that some call out to others: *“Come, let us go up to the mountain of the Lord, that he may teach us of his ways.”* <note>A reference to Isaiah 2:3 and Micah 4:2</note> Truly, such a directory (a perfect method of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: from the Greek for "all-wisdom," this was the author's project to harmonize all religious, scientific, and philosophical knowledge into one system</term>) is not to be despaired of; the following points persuade us of this.

---

<margin>
The foundations of this hope.
</margin>

---

<margin>
1. All things are subject to Man.
</margin>

*First*, it is certain that, even if things seem innumerable because of their infinite multitude, incommensurable because of their varied disproportion, and impenetrable because the truth is mostly hidden in the depths: nevertheless, all things are beneath Man and subject to his intellect. For all things were indeed made for his sake, but at a lower level: he himself, as the final crowning touch <term>colophon: originally the final stroke or finishing mark of a manuscript; here used to mean the peak or completion of creation</term> and the most absolute image of his Creator, encompassing the perfection of all things within himself alone—why should he not finally become accustomed to seeing himself and all things clearly? For because God established him as an observer of His wisdom, it is certain that He did not make him unfit for such a great end. He would have done so, however, if He had made things disproportionate to the intellect, or the intellect disproportionate to things. Therefore, let it remain that only One is "too great," of whose greatness there is no searching out: *Psalm 145:3.* All other things were made according to "number, measure, and weight." <note>original: *ad numerum, mensuram, & pondus*; a reference to Wisdom of Solomon 11:20 and Isaiah 40:12, suggesting the universe is mathematically ordered and thus knowable</note> *Wisdom 11:20. Isaiah 40:12.*

<meta>catchword: For indeed (original: Namq.)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that a complete system of universal knowledge (Pansophia) is possible because God created the human intellect specifically to understand the ordered, measurable world. He asserts that while God is infinite, the rest of creation is finite and designed to be mastered by human reason.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, Theater of Wisdom, human intellect, divine order, biblical proportions, microcosm, creation, Comenius</keywords>

(45)

...things. Therefore, they must be numbered, measured, and weighed, until the universal harmony is revealed to our eyes. <note>original: *Numeranda proinde, mensuranda, ponderanda*; this is a reference to the Book of Wisdom 11:20, "thou hast ordered all things in measure and number and weight," a foundational concept for 17th-century science and theology</note>

<margin>
2. The desire implanted in the heart.
</margin>

Secondly, God made all things well, as the Holy Scripture says. But he did so each in its own time, *Ecclesiastes 3:11*, that is, step by step. Would he, therefore, have implanted in the hearts of men a desire to know the world—to understand the things he himself works from the beginning to the end—in vain? <note>original: *mundi desideriū*; referring to the innate human drive for scientific and spiritual discovery</note> Indeed, he would have implanted it in vain if that very desire were never to reach its goal. But it is not fitting for the highest wisdom to do anything in vain.

<margin>
3. Progress is not to be despised.
</margin>

Thirdly, we already possess a grand apparatus <term>apparatus: here meaning a collection of tools, data, or intellectual resources</term> in the many monuments of books <note>The author views books not just as paper, but as enduring "monuments" of human achievement</note> gathered until now with such great labor and diligence. Do we think all those authors have accomplished nothing? I have already shown that this could not be the case because of the supreme governor of things <note>A reference to God as the "Providence" or "Presider" over history</note>, who allows nothing to happen in vain—not even errors. Granted, then, that they may have hallucinated or erred in most things; nevertheless, because God is the eternal and immutable foundation of Truth <note>original: *Veritatis basis*</note>, he cannot fail to direct matters so that even errors themselves must eventually serve the truth, which is to be revealed and strengthened more clearly day by day. Furthermore, it is beautifully evident that many things have already been discovered; why, then, should we not hope for the rest? It is certainly no small thing that *Euclid*, *Archimedes*, and others have discovered the principles of quantity...

<summary>The author argues that human curiosity and the vast history of scholarship are evidence of a divine plan to eventually reveal all knowledge. He asserts that even past intellectual errors serve to refine the ultimate discovery of universal truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the possibility of universal knowledge (Pansophia). (46)

...have led to such clarity that, through numbers, measures, and weights, miracles are practically being performed. It is no small thing that physicians and followers of Hermes <note>Hermeticists: practitioners of a mystical and philosophical tradition often linked to alchemy and the search for the underlying essences of nature</note> have learned, with the help of chemistry <term>Chymia: at this time, the word referred to both practical chemistry and the spiritual/material goals of alchemy</term>, to strip away the outward qualities of natural objects and penetrate almost to their bare essence. It is no small thing that Lord Verulam <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), 1st Viscount St. Albans, whose Latin name was Verulamius; he is celebrated here for his *Novum Organum* (New Tool), which proposed a new system of logic based on induction</note> has uncovered an infallible method for examining the inner nature of things through his wonderful "instrument." And Joachim Jungius of Saxony <note>Joachim Jungius (1587–1657), a pioneering German mathematician and naturalist who sought to apply mathematical certainty to all sciences</note> is now laboring to bring the art of logic to such perfection that the truth of propositions can be established and the deceptions of false arguments <term>sophismatum: logical fallacies or deceptive arguments used to mislead</term> destroyed with the same certainty that any problem of Euclid <note>Euclid of Alexandria, the ancient Greek "Father of Geometry," whose proofs were considered the gold standard for absolute certainty</note> is proven through demonstration. Why should I mention more? Just as one nail drives out another, so one discovery pushes forward another in this century of ours, so fertile with great minds. Should we then despair of finding an "invention of inventions"—one that would make everything achieved individually by so many geniuses common to the whole human race? I say that not only should all the truth already unearthed become common property, but a single method for unearthing the truth of all things should be forged from every available source. And oh, how beautiful that would be!

<margin>
4. From many lights, let there be one.
</margin>

For if individuals had their own sharp insights <term>acumina: literally "points" or "sharpened edges," here meaning keen mental penetration</term> for reaching the heart of a matter, their own rules for acting, and their own weights for re-evaluating things: what might not be possible if all those sharp insights were combined into one single insight, and all those rules into one single rule, and all

(47)

...can weights be reduced to a single weight? More candles mean more light. If only a way can be found by which all lights can merge into one single light: that is, how such diverse and almost infinite inventions, known facts, and thoughts might be reduced into one perpetual, unchanging, and eternal form of thinking, knowing, and discovering. For once this is discovered, we will possess what we seek.

And why shouldn't it be found? We certainly already possess a substantial "forest" <term>sylvam: a Latin metaphor for a vast, unorganized collection of data or raw materials, which the author believes must be shaped into a systematic "garden" of knowledge</term> of known things, and a clear light of method for proceeding toward the rest. Let us therefore attempt to go *further beyond* <note>original: *plus ultrà*; the motto of the Spanish Empire and a rallying cry for the Scientific Revolution, suggesting that humanity should look beyond the traditional limits of ancient knowledge</note> to the very final goal of both things and method. For wherever there is a "further," there is also a "final." Only eternity, for which we are destined, can set limits on our minds; everything that exists in time falls within the mind's reach—provided that their roots—the eternal, constant, and unchanging principles of things, according to which all things exist and happen—are understood.

<margin>5. God's promise concerning the final times.</margin>
And we have an explicit promise concerning the final times, that many shall pass through, and knowledge shall be increased <note>A reference to the Book of Daniel 12:4. In the 17th century, this was frequently cited to argue that the recent explosion in geographical and scientific discovery was a sign of the approaching end of the world or a "Great Instauration" of human power</note>. Many have indeed passed through and examined (in this century more than ever before) the heavens and the earth, the seas and the islands, and the whole...

...the kingdom of nature, as well as the books of divine oracles in one way or another. And what remains then, except that the other part of the prophecy <note>Referring to the biblical prophecy in Daniel 12:4, "many shall run to and fro, and knowledge shall be increased," which early modern scholars viewed as a sign of their own era</note> also receives its fulfillment?

<margin>
*A consultation on the manner of preparing the Pansophic book.*
</margin>
But let us come at last to the matter itself: namely, the way in which this thing, so greatly desired, may be brought into effect. And indeed, from those things which have already been said concerning both the causes and remedies of the confusion in the world of letters <note>original: *rei literariae*; a common term for the collective body of human scholarship and literature</note>, the path may have appeared somewhat; nevertheless, it must be stated more explicitly.

<margin>
*Three things are necessary.*
</margin>
To reach the universal Knowledge, Possession, and Use of all things, I do not think it possible except through a new and universal:

<margin>
1. *A Review, or a new inspection, of everything that currently exists.*
</margin>
I. **Review** of all our assets <note>original: *bonorum*; literally "goods," here used as a metaphor for the collective inheritance of human knowledge</note>, together with all existing inventories.

II. **Comparison** <term>collationem: the scholarly process of comparing different versions of texts or data sets to verify accuracy</term> of those inventories with the things themselves, to see if the objects are truly as our account books or registers <note>original: *rationaria* and *regesta*; administrative terms used here to describe scholarly catalogs</note> report them to be.

III. **Arrangement** <term>dispositionem: the systematic organization of materials into a logical structure</term> of those things which have been found—a new and universal organization for new and universal uses.

This Review of our assets aims to ensure that we are not ignorant of what constitutes our proper inheritance, and what all people without exception ought to be occupied with. For it is only too true: men do not understand their own "goods"; namely...

<summary>The author moves from theoretical prophecy to a practical plan for "Pansophy" (universal wisdom), proposing a three-step process: reviewing all existing knowledge, verifying it against reality, and reorganizing it for universal use.</summary>

(49)
<sig>D</sig>

...that they are possessors of the world; masters of the Creatures, and partakers of eternity itself with God. Because few either know, believe, or consider this, it happens that most people occupy themselves with vain and destructive things, and are at last disinherited as unworthy. Therefore, Christians must be taught to weigh accurately that Apostolic saying: *Whether the world, or life, or death, or things present, or things to come, all are yours; and you are Christ’s; and Christ is God’s.* **1 Cor. 3:22.**

I did not say in vain that the inventories <note>original: *inventaria*; Comenius uses the metaphor of an estate inventory to describe the sum of human knowledge</note> must be reviewed at the same time. For our riches are so vast and varied that few can even remember their names <note>original: *nomenclaturam*; the systematic naming of things</note>, much less do they know what each thing is or what care they require from us. All these things must therefore be described for men, clearly and lucidly, in the manner of a law. **Deut. 27:8.** 

<margin>
Also of the Authors who have already written on any subjects whatsoever.
</margin>
And because they must be accurately described, we must see to it that nothing is overlooked. Consequently, all previous repositories <term>repertoria: catalogs, indexes, or reference works where information is stored</term> must be consulted—universal and particular, old and new, by whomever they were prepared. For although these are very many, it is necessary for one striving for a perfect synopsis <term>synopsin: a comprehensive, condensed overview of all parts of a subject</term> of all things to undergo this labor. And would that more were still extant! But certain famous monuments <note>Here used to mean "great written works"</note> have perished: among which were the books of the wisest man, Solomon, concerning plants and animals <note>A reference to the lost natural history of King Solomon mentioned in 1 Kings 4:33</note>. Since these are desired in vain—

(50)

...should be brought together; those things that are available must be put to use, and before all else, the Holy Book of God himself—the Bible—which is nothing other than God's own commentary on those things he has given us in this life and preserves for us in the future. But because in this book God treats almost exclusively, or certainly primarily, matters concerning the state of the soul, we must not overlook those who have treated the lesser subjects: Philosophers, Physicians, Legal Experts, Engineers <note>original: *mechanici*; in the 17th century, this referred to skilled artisans, inventors, and those who applied mathematical principles to physical crafts</note>, the inventors of various things, Historians, and Cosmographers <term>cosmographi: scholars who studied the structure of the universe, combining geography, astronomy, and natural history</term>. The goal is that from all these specific sciences, one single, universal "Science of Sciences" and "Art of Arts" may finally be forged—that is, Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: from the Greek for 'all-wisdom,' the ideal of a unified, harmonious system of all human and divine knowledge</term>.

However, we do not mean this in the sense that <margin>But to what end?</margin> the various opinions of authors on these matters should simply be gathered into a book, as is done by those who measure scholarship by the sheer volume of their reading. Such people seek only to recite the various viewpoints of different men or to gain a reputation for themselves by publishing "patchwork texts" <note>original: *centonibus*; a 'cento' is a literary work made up of fragments from various sources, like a patchwork quilt</note> stitched together from the works of others. Nor is it simply so that these various opinions might be compared with one another, as those do who debate various questions *pro* and *con* <note>original: *pro & contra*; for and against</note>, exhausting themselves by tearing down the opinions they dislike and filling up entire volumes with their disputes. Rather, our intent is that in the composition of this Philosophical work, all those who have written concerning piety, morals, the sciences, and the arts...

<summary>Comenius argues that a "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia) must combine the spiritual truths of the Bible with the empirical knowledge of various human disciplines. He distinguishes this project from mere "book-learning" or the compilation of conflicting opinions, seeking instead a unified synthesis.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Bible, human sciences, universal knowledge, scholarly method, synthesis, 17th-century education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for an inclusive "inventory of knowledge." Having established that this "Universal Wisdom" should draw from all fields of study (science, history, and scripture), the author now explains why it must also include thinkers from every religion, culture, and philosophical school throughout history, rather than being limited to a single tradition.</meta>

(51)

...[who] have commented, regardless of whether they were Christian or Muslim <note>original: *Mahumetanus*</note>, Jew or Pagan <note>original: *Ethnicus*</note>, and whichever sect they belonged to—be it Pythagorean, Academic <note>Followers of Plato</note>, Peripatetic <note>Followers of Aristotle</note>, Stoic, Essene, Greek, Roman, Ancient or Modern, Doctor or Rabbi, or any Church, Synod, or Council. All, I say, are to be admitted and heard, to see what good they might bring.

<margin>*Why must all authors be admitted here? Because...*</margin>
We urge this because:

<margin>*1. A common task is being undertaken.*</margin>
1. What we are working toward is a treasure of universal wisdom <term>universalis sapientia: also known as Pansophia, the ideal of a unified system of all human and divine knowledge</term> to be held in common by the human race. It is only fair, then, that all minds, all nations, sects, and ages should be contributors here.

<margin>*2. All have been admitted by God.*</margin>
2. We all sit here, placed in the world within the common Amphitheater of God’s Wisdom <note>A metaphor suggesting the world is a stage where God’s works are displayed for all to observe and study</note>. Furthermore, the light of divine revelation shines equally upon us Christians. Why then should even the humblest person not be permitted to alert others—with a modest hand and voice—to whatever remarkable thing they believe they see?

<margin>*3. God distributes gifts in various ways.*</margin>
3. It is not likely that only one person, or a few individuals in one or two centuries, were granted the ability to see everything while everyone else saw nothing. Instead, just as no single land produces every crop, yet every land produces something—and even different things in different years—so God [scatters] the various sparks of His light among different minds...

<sig>D 2</sig>

<vocab>universal wisdom, Christian, Muslim, Jew, Pagan, Pythagorean, Academic, Peripatetic, Stoic, Essene, Amphitheater of God, light of revelation</vocab>

<summary>The author argues for a radical inclusivity in the pursuit of "Universal Wisdom," insisting that insights from all religions, philosophical schools, and historical eras must be considered. He justifies this by stating that the search for truth is a collective human duty and that God distributes wisdom across all people and times rather than concentrating it in a few.</summary>

(52)

...[to] distribute them through various nations and ages. "The wind blows where it wishes," <note>original: *Ventus quò vult spirat*—a reference to John 3:8</note> says Christ, speaking of the Holy Spirit and its operation. And examples are not lacking outside the boundaries of the Church <note>original: *Ecclesiæ Pomeria*; the *pomerium* was the sacred boundary of a city</note> where the Spirit of wisdom was accustomed to inspire one person after another: such as *Job, Eliphaz, Elihu, Hermes Trismegistus, Socrates, Epictetus, Cicero*. Therefore, no one should be looked down upon (for as the saying goes, "often even a gardener has spoken quite opportunely" <note>original: *sæpe etiam olitor est satis oportuna locutus*—a Latin proverb suggesting that wisdom can come from humble sources</note>), especially in those matters that can be understood by the light of nature. *Verulam* <note>Francis Bacon, 1st Viscount St Alban (1561–1626), also known as Baron Verulam, an English philosopher who advocated for the inductive method of scientific inquiry</note> wisely judges that the different opinions of men regarding the *Nature* of things are like different commentaries on the same text: one of which may be more correct in this place, another in that, and each can provide something useful to observe. Let it stand, therefore, that there is no book so bad that something good cannot be found in it. If nothing else, it certainly provides an opportunity to correct some error.

<margin>
4. By comparing opinions, the truth is more easily found.
</margin>

4. It is certain that no one falls into error <note>original: *errare*; literally "to wander"</note> by choice (for to what end would they want that?), but rather they wander because they are deceived by some likeness to the truth. For even for errors, reasons are put forward. Therefore, these reasons must be heard: because we are dealing with men as rational creatures. Thus, by comparing reasons, deception will be more easily uncovered, and the appearance of truth will yield to the truth itself, just as shadow yields to light.

<margin>
5. No one ought to be condemned unheard.
</margin>

5. If anyone is condemned unheard, although <meta>catchword: although (quamvi)</meta>

<page-num>(53)</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>D 3</sig>

Even if a person is condemned justly, justice nevertheless suffers violence <note>original: *justitia tamen vim patitur*</note> because it is possible that the reality of the matter is different from what was heard through hearsay; therefore, all things should rather be investigated first. To say nothing of the fact that it is a mark of excessive arrogance for one man to so prejudge another—based on a preconceived opinion or suspicion—that he is unwilling even to hear his reasons. Is this not the same as saying to one’s brother, *Raca*? <note>original: *Raca*—an Aramaic term of contempt mentioned by Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5:22), roughly meaning "empty-headed" or "fool." The author argues that refusing to listen to an opponent is a form of spiritual insult.</note>

<margin>6. *Errors can even be of use.*</margin>
6. Suppose that this or that person was ignorant of something, or even erred significantly in one matter or another: yet who does not know that the error of another can serve as a warning for a wise man? To the architects of <term>Pansophia: "Universal Wisdom," a 17th-century ideal of a single system of knowledge that would bring peace to the world</term>, the various wanderings of others, and the various occasions that led to those wanderings, can usefully suggest many ways to further polish and perfect the work of Universal Wisdom.

<margin>7. *Dissensions must therefore be removed by a reduction to harmony.*</margin>
7. We desire that humans finally be freed from dissensions, so that sects and hatreds may cease. But these will not cease unless the suspicions that people harbor against one another also cease. Furthermore, suspicions cannot cease unless everyone is satisfied regarding the opinions and doubts <note>original: *sententiis & scrupulis*—literally "opinions and small sharp stones," a metaphor for nagging doubts</note> both of their own and of others. This satisfaction cannot be achieved unless the opinions of all are heard, compared calmly, and measured against the same laws of clear and undeniable truth that both sides have established for themselves.

<margin>8. *The example of the ancient people of God.*</margin>
8. Finally, it is worth remembering that for the construction of the sacred <term>Tabernacle: the portable dwelling place of God used by the Israelites in the wilderness</term> by *Moses*, and

...of the Temple by **Solomon**, the **Israelites** contributed not only from their own stores, but they also offered spoils taken from the enemies of God's people—the **Egyptians**, **Philistines**, and **Ammonites**—and these were accepted by the architects. For gold was sought from everywhere, and gems were offered from places other than the Holy Land, and **Cedar** logs, cut on Mount **Lebanon**, were brought in; all of it was for the honor of the God of **Israel** and the splendor of His house. Just as also in the rebuilding of that same Temple of God by **Zerubbabel** <note>The leader of the first group of Jews who returned from the Babylonian Captivity to rebuild the Temple</note>, God stirred the spirit of King **Cyrus**, so that he not only offered his own royal treasury but also commanded his subjects throughout all his kingdoms to help the **Israelites**—each from his own place—with silver and gold, with goods, and with livestock (see the book of **Ezra 1:2-4**). Why then should the builders of the Temple of Wisdom not also admit anything precious or splendid that can come from anywhere? Indeed, even the **Gibeonites** <note>A group who tricked the Israelites into a treaty; they were spared but forced to become perpetual servants of the Tabernacle</note>, although cursed, could be employed to cut wood and carry water for the house of our God (**Joshua 9:23**). So it happens, because so it is beneficial.

We admit them, therefore, but on this condition: that whatever was truly, wisely, or piously thought, written, or discovered by them shall be taken up for common use; but whatever they said awkwardly, or against truth or piety, or...

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the "cleansing" of knowledge. ...if they are convicted of having done so, let them be ordered to be silent henceforth, so that profanity may not forever struggle against piety, error against truth, and darkness against light.

<margin>In the review of authors, there is a need for integrity and diligence.</margin>
But here, integrity and judgment will be necessary, lest we pronounce judgment too easily. For it happens that we sometimes impose errors on others which they themselves do not recognize, because—preoccupied by suspicion or carried away by emotion—we twist their words against the intention of their authors. Yet everyone is the best interpreter of their own words; **Aristotle** himself does not have the best reputation <note>original: "non optimè audit," literally "does not hear the best," a Latin idiom meaning to have a poor reputation or be criticized by others</note> in this regard, as if, in order to give credit to his own decrees, he refuted the ancient philosophers without having reviewed their dogmas with enough integrity. Thus, things could not help but appear monstrous which, in themselves, might not have been so discordant with the truth, or perhaps even with **Aristotelian** opinion. If this is so (for I pronounce nothing for certain), it will serve as a caution to the builders of <term>Pansophia</term> <note>Pansophia: A "Universal Wisdom" or "All-Knowledge." This was a 17th-century intellectual movement that sought to organize all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system that reconciled science, philosophy, and religion</note>—where not a partial victory, but a universal harmony must be sought—so that, as much as possible, disagreements may be brought back into consonance, even through a truth found in the middle. Of this, more follows.

<margin>How necessary is the comparison between writings and reality.</margin>
These things concern the review of facts and records. I have also said that there is a need for comparison <note>original: "collatio." This refers to the systematic process of checking texts against physical reality or against other authoritative sources to ensure consistency</note> to detect whether everything that exists in reality has been recorded? Or whether everything that is recorded actually exists? And also whether everything remains in the same state as it is handed down? Unless...

D 5

...for unless the things themselves and the explanation of things coincide perfectly <note>original: "ex amussi," a phrase referring to a carpenter's level, meaning with absolute precision or "by the square"</note>, human minds are entangled and enveloped in errors. It is now all too certain that we have far too many items in our catalogs (especially within certain schools of thought where dreams and shadows are mistaken for reality) which do not actually exist in nature. Conversely, there are many things in the **Treasuries of God and Nature** <note>The author views God and Nature as two great storehouses of truth that must be explored together</note> that have not yet been recorded for us. Finally, very many things are quite different from what our little books dictate to us. Thus, it is absolutely necessary that all <term>commentaries</term> <note>Written scholarly records, textbooks, and traditional interpretations of knowledge</note> be compared with the things themselves. 

Only when this is done will the deficiencies, the redundancies, and the errors finally become apparent; only then can they be removed, corrected, or supplemented—and not before. 

But what mortal will be sufficient for such a task? This is due, of course, to the infinite multitude and variety of things: **natural, supernatural, moral, and artificial** <note>In this context, "artificial" refers to things made by human art or skill</note>. For if those earlier <term>investigators of things</term> <note>original: "rerum indagatores"; the scientists and philosophers of previous generations</note> found so many great difficulties in even specific subjects that the diligence of most ended in a complaint—lamenting the insurmountable subtlety of nature, the inscrutable complexity of causes, the relentless hostility of the opposing forces encountered everywhere, and the excessive weakness of the human intellect in the face of it all—what will happen if someone wishes to reweave the entire fabric of knowledge from the beginning? But we must not despond—

<summary>The author argues that true knowledge requires comparing written scholarship against the actual reality of the natural and divine world to correct errors. He acknowledges the staggering difficulty of this task, noting that previous philosophers often gave up in frustration due to the complexity of nature and human limitation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 57 regarding the difficulty of organizing human knowledge. (58)

...the **daunted mind** is. By skill, that which cannot be done by strength is sometimes achieved: a fact which investigators of things seem not to have sufficiently observed until now. These men have struggled to break through by the force of their genius alone and the momentum of their constant diligence; yet, just as a naked hand is of little use, so is the intellect when left to itself <note>The author is quoting Francis Bacon’s *Novum Organum* (1620), which argues that the human mind needs "tools" just as much as the hand does</note>. However, a task may be completed both more easily and more certainly with instruments and aids.

<margin>Not without standards.</margin>Therefore, we shall need **norms** <term>standards or rules of measurement</term> by which we may examine both things themselves and the doctrines concerning them. In this way, the necessary may be quickly distinguished from the unnecessary, the useful from the useless, and the true from the false. 

The most illustrious *Verulamius* <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), the Baron Verulam, an English philosopher and statesman who is often called the father of empiricism</note> seems to have discovered such a norm for investigating nature: a certain specialized **induction** <term>a method of reasoning where general laws are derived from specific observations</term> which is truly an open road for penetrating the hidden secrets of nature. 

But in truth, because this method requires the continued labor of many men and many centuries—and thus appears both laborious and dependent on uncertain success—it happens that this brilliant discovery is despised by most people as if it were useless. 

For us, however, it brings little help toward the construction of **Pansophia** <note>Universal Wisdom; the author’s project to create a unified system of all human knowledge—scientific, religious, and philosophical</note>. This is because (as I have said) it is directed only toward unlocking the secrets of nature, whereas we are concerned with the **totality of all things** <note>original: "rerum universitas"; this refers to the entire scope of existence, including the physical, the moral, and the divine</note>. 

Therefore, some other altogether universal norm will be needed, which perhaps our merciful God—who...

Therefore He hides Himself, so that He may be sought; therefore He wishes to be sought, so that He may be found. <note>original: "ideo se abscondit, ut quaeratur; ideo quaeri vult, ut inveniatur." This theological principle suggests that the mystery of the world is an invitation from God to learn.</note> *Proverbs 25:2. Isaiah 45:15.* "God will in no way allow your search to be cheated of the reward of discovery," (writes the blessed Fulgentius <note>Fulgentius of Ruspe (c. 467–533 AD), an influential North African bishop and theologian</note> to Monimus, Book 1) "since it was He who spiritually inflamed you to seek: while His faithful promise shall by no means be voided when He says; *Ask and you shall receive, seek and you shall find, knock and it shall be opened to you.*" <note>Matthew 7:7</note>

What the Lord our God has revealed and granted us to see as we ask, seek, and knock, I am now about to explain; I pray and entreat those who can judge these matters wisely to attend to them with piety. Indeed, I will set forth the whole matter in several aphorisms <term>short, concise statements of principle</term>.

<margin>
The standard for building Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia; the idea of an all-encompassing system of knowledge</term> is explained through several aphorisms of Universal Harmony <term>Panharmonia; the concept that all parts of the universe are interconnected and in tune with one another</term>.

# 1. There are three things which 
# complete our human knowledge, 
# and even a kind of 
# omniscience: the knowledge 
# of *God*, of *Nature*, and of *Art*.

By **Art**, I mean whatever is produced by human industry—thoughts, speeches, and works. By **Nature**, I mean whatever is born and comes to be by its own power implanted within things. By **God**, I mean whatever of His power, wisdom, and goodness—hidden from eternity—has revealed itself until now through words and works. Whoever knows these three, knows **all...**

<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, where "om-" was likely the prefix. The word "nia" completes "omnia" (all things). It continues the argument that the universe is comprised of three categories: God, Nature, and Art (human industry).</meta>

[...knows] all things; because the whole universe consists of these three kinds of things.

## II. A perfect knowledge of these three must be sought.

Let it not be considered enough to know just *something* about **God**, *something* about **Nature**, and *something* about **Art** (for even the most ignorant and even fools happen to know that much). Instead, we must strive to understand everything that can be understood, both fully and exactly.

## III. Knowledge of things is perfect when it is true.

For if knowledge is not true, it presents us with phantoms <note>original: "spectra"; refers here to false mental images or illusions</note> instead of real things, and the result is nothing but a mockery.

## IV. Knowledge is true when things are known exactly as they are.

For if things are known in a way other than they truly are, it will not be knowledge, but error.

## V. Things are known as they are when they are known just as they were made.

For everything exists exactly as it was made; if a thing is not as it was made, it has degenerated <note>original: "degeneravit"; to fall away from its original nature or purpose</note>.

## VI. Every thing is made according to its own Idea <term>Idea; in this philosophical context, a "pattern" or "blueprint" in the mind of the creator</term>—that is,

<gloss>God, Nature, Art, Knowledge, Idea, The Universe, Error</gloss>

<summary>This page establishes the criteria for "true knowledge," arguing that to understand the world, one must understand things as they were originally created according to their divine or natural blueprints.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, epistemology, truth vs error, the three domains, divine ideas, creation, degeneration</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of an "Idea" from the previous page, completing the sentence: "Every thing is made according to its own Idea—that is,"</meta>

(61)
<meta>catchword: turæ (likely the end of "Naturæ")</meta>

according to a certain definite plan, through which it was able to be what it is. For unless something were able to come into being, it would not be made; and unless it were able to be made in a particular way, it would not be made that way. Therefore, that specific possibility is called an **Idea**, through which a thing is what it is.

### **VII. All things, therefore, that are made, are made according to *Ideas*, whether they be the works of *God*, of *Nature*, or of *Art* <term>Art (Ars); in the 17th century, this term refers to all human industry, skill, and technology, not just the "fine arts"</term>.**

Since an **Idea** is a certain definite pattern for things, it is unthinkable that **God** would do anything without Ideas—that is, without a definite plan—since He Himself is the very Reason behind all reason. Similarly, **Nature**, since it produces works of the highest order, and likewise its rival **Art**, cannot operate without a plan.

### **VIII. *Art* borrows the *Ideas* for its works from *Nature*; *Nature* borrows from *God*; and *God* borrows from Himself.**

That first point—that Art borrows from Nature—is shown to be common knowledge by those well-known proverbs: "Art can do nothing without nature"; "Art is the rival of Nature"; "Art imitates Nature"; "Art is Na-" <note>original: "Ars est Na-"; the word is cut off, likely "Naturæ assecla," meaning a follower or servant of nature</note>

<gloss>Idea, God, Nature, Art, possibility, plan/reason, works, proverbs</gloss>

<summary>This page defines an "Idea" as the necessary blueprint or rational plan that allows a thing to exist as it is. It argues that God, Nature, and Human Industry all work according to such plans, with each level of creation drawing its patterns from the one above it.</summary>
<keywords>Ideas, rationality, divine plan, imitation of nature, Art vs Nature, teleology, Pansophia</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of proverbs regarding the relationship between Art, Nature, and God, completing the final proverb from the previous page: "Art is Nature's daughter."</meta>

(62)
<meta>catchword: non</meta>

-ture's daughter <note>original: "turæ filia"; completing the word "Naturæ" from the previous page</note>. But these sayings are no less true: **Nature can do nothing without God**; **Nature is the rival of God**; **Nature is the daughter of God**; **Nature imitates God**. **God**, finally, imitates no one except Himself, because He neither can nor wishes to do otherwise. He cannot do otherwise because He has nothing besides Himself in His eternity; from where, then, would He take the beginning or the form of His works?

He does not wish to do otherwise because, since He is the best, He cannot wish for anything but the best. And there is nothing "best" except that one, single, eternal Good which He Himself is. If anyone should say that God freely devised the reasons <note>or "plans"</note> for producing things, I ask: for what purpose? If God now does nothing in vain through **Nature**, why would He have done so from the beginning? Namely, so that while having Himself as the most perfect exemplar of all perfection, He might devise something else? Or was it to spare His majesty? Yet He went about expressing this majesty visibly. *Romans 1:20* <note>The text refers to the Apostle Paul's argument that God's invisible attributes are understood through the things He has made.</note> 

Or was it to show the depth of His wisdom by that departure from Himself? But in that way the fullness of His glory would be diminished, as if there were some perfection that did not exist within Himself. But this is impossible. It is certain, therefore, that just as **creatures** <term>Creatures (creaturæ); in this context, all created things, including plants and minerals, not just animals</term> flowed from this source, so too did the **Ideas** of creatures. And because in creatures every agent seeks an assimilation <note>a making-alike</note> of the object to itself, why, in the Creator—by whom that character <term>Character; a Greek-derived word meaning a "stamp" or "imprint," like the mark of a seal on wax</term> was impressed upon creatures—

<summary>This page argues that God is the ultimate source of all blueprints (Ideas), as He imitates nothing but His own eternal perfection. It concludes that Nature imitates God just as Art imitates Nature, forming a chain of creation that stems from God’s own essence.</summary>
<keywords>God, Nature, Ideas, perfection, exemplar, Romans 1:20, creation, divine wisdom</keywords>

### *IX. God, therefore, in fashioning the world, fashions Himself, so that the Creature might be entirely proportional to the Creator.*

For it is necessary that what is formed corresponds to its form: notwithstanding that there are degrees of that proportion, namely in respect to greater or lesser clarity. Thus the **Son of God** is called the "character" <note>original: "character"; used here in the sense of an engraved mark or "express image"</note> (or the express image) of the Father (Hebrews 1:3). And yet, man is also called the image of God (Genesis 1:26, 1 Corinthians 11:6). Furthermore, all other things are compared to a certain image, since "through those things which were made, from the creation of the world, the invisible things of God are clearly seen" (Romans 1:20), and "by the greatness of the beauty <note>original: "speciei"</note> and of the creature, their Creator can be known and seen" (Wisdom of Solomon 13:5). And from this it happened that the pagans <note>original: "gentiles"</note> called **nature** not only the daughter of God, but also God itself. What else is na-

**X.** And because all things participate in the Ideas of the Divine mind, it follows that they also participate among themselves and are mutually proportional to one another. For things that agree in a third thing, agree with each other. <note>This is a fundamental rule of logic: if A=C and B=C, then A=B. Here, because all things (A and B) are modeled on God's ideas (C), they share a fundamental connection.</note>

**XI.** Accordingly, the essential principles <note>original: "rationes"</note> of things are the same, and do not differ except in their form of existence: because they are in *God* as in the *Archetype* <term>Archetype (archetypo); the original blueprint or master pattern in the mind of God</term>, in *Nature* as in the *Ectype* <term>Ectype (ectypo); a physical copy or reproduction of an original pattern</term>, and in *Art* <note>In the 17th century, "Art" (Ars) referred to any human skill, craft, or technology.</note> as in the *Antitype* <term>Antitype (antitypo); a representation or something that corresponds to the original type</term>.

Just as, for example, the same form exists in a seal. First, it is conceived in the mind of the one who shapes it or orders it to be shaped; next, it is engraved in metal; finally, it is impressed upon wax. For although the form is threefold, it is nevertheless one: because the second is formed from the first, and the third from the second, each subsequent one according to the image of the prior. Thus the Ideas conceived in the [Divine] mind impress their likeness upon the **creatures**; and the **creatures**, in turn, acting with reason, [impress that likeness] upon the things produced by them.

### ***XII.* Therefore, the foundation of all things, both in their creation and in their understanding, is *Harmony*.**

Musicians define Harmony as a pleasant sounding together of many voices. In truth, there exists such a concordant song <note>original: "concentus"; a "singing together" or choir-like agreement</note> of the eternal virtues in God, the created virtues in Nature, and the expressed virtues in Art, harmonizing through all things. This is because each of these is a Harmony in itself, and they are also harmonic toward one another, since Nature is the image of divine Harmony, and Art is the image of Nature.

### ***XIII.* The first requirement of Harmony is that nothing should be dissonant.**

Musical Harmony is forged from voices that are very different, and even contrary to one another, and yet this contrariety is brought back into consonance. In the same way, the entire world is composed of contraries (because without contraries, neither truth, nor order, nor indeed the very essence of the world would stand), just as Scripture contains many things that appear to conflict. Nevertheless, all things return to a concord within themselves, and must be brought back to it within our own minds, if that Universal Harmony <term>Panarmonia; from the Greek, meaning a state where all parts of the universe are in perfect accord</term> is to emerge—which, just as it belongs to those divine works, so too must it belong to our own works and
opinions <note>catchword: "sententiis"</note>

<summary>This page defines "Harmony" as the structural link between God, the natural world, and human craft, arguing that even conflicting elements (contraries) are necessary to achieve a universal consonance.</summary>
<keywords>Harmony, Panarmonia, Nature and Art, Consonance, Contraries, Divine Order</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the final sentence of page 65: "...so too must it belong to our own works and opinions."</meta>

(66)

opinions and words, they win universal agreement, with any dissonance that seemed to exist disappearing. Because this mystery is not commonly understood, Philosophers seize it from nature and Theologians from scripture—some taking this, others taking that—and they set nature against nature and scriptures against scriptures. They extract contrary meanings and stir up quarrels and battles; these must necessarily vanish when the light of universal harmony appears. For Truth is consistent with itself on every side.

### ***XIV.* The second requirement of Harmony is that nothing should be discordant.**

Regarding things of Nature and things of Art, it is clear that everything is made according to harmony. For in an Animal, a Tree, a Musical Instrument, a Ship, a Chariot, a Book, or a House, and so on, it is necessary that all parts be proportionate, both to the whole and to each other. 

But regarding divine things, someone might doubt whether they enter into any proportion with natural and artificial things. For one might think it belongs to the divine majesty to share nothing in common with created things. But in truth, it must be known that whatever is found in the <term>Ectype; a copy, reflection, or representation of an original pattern</term> is necessarily found first—and indeed in a more perfect form—in the <term>Archetype; the original model or divine idea from which all things are derived</term>. This is because a river from a spring, a shadow from a body, or a mirror from an image, derives whatever it possesses from its source. <meta>catchword: possesses (habet)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that religious and philosophical conflicts arise from a lack of "universal harmony," which, once understood, reveals that all truth is consistent. It further explains that the created world (the ectype) is a direct, proportional reflection of the divine original (the archetype).</summary>

variety of proportions <note>Completing the word "proportionum" from the previous page.</note>, yet it arises from a few principles and certain modes of differentiation.

For all the differences in Harmonies <term>Harmoniarum: Musical harmonies or the mathematical ratios of sound</term>, however many have been devised or can be devised, arise from seven notes and three consonances <term>consonantiis: Intervals perceived as harmonious, such as the octave, fifth, and fourth</term>. All bodies, as many as the world contains, arise from those very few elements and some variations in qualities. Thus it is in all other things: so that the multitude and variety of things is nothing but the varied repetition of similar things. *For example*, in a tree, though there be many thousands of leaves, they are all of the same shape, color, and power <note>original: "virtutis"; here meaning the inherent quality or medicinal property of the plant</note>. Likewise, however many trees of the same kind there are throughout the whole world, they all exist in the same way, and act and are acted upon <note>original: "agunt, & patiuntur"; a classical philosophical distinction between an object's active influence and its passive reception of external forces</note> in the same manner. So too, trees of different kinds have certain common proportions <note>original: "rationes"; which can mean reasons, ratios, or logical principles</note>.

### *XVI.* Once the principles of things and the modes of their differences are known, all things will be known.

*For example*: Whoever has learned what is beautiful in itself, or what requirements constitute beauty, will now—without any teacher—know what makes a beautiful Angel, a beautiful soul, a beautiful body, a beautiful color, beautiful speech, beautiful morals, or anything else. Oh, how swift a path this is to the knowledge of many things: to have the springs of things thus laid open!

### XVII. And such common principles of things must be abstracted from the things themselves, through a certain induction, and set down as the norms of things.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the principles of Universal Wisdom (Pansophia). ...the moon. And is it not a waste of time to say: "Every pig is a pig, every horse is a horse"? For who would deny this? To whom would it be necessary to urge such a point? Therefore, the precepts of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: A system of "universal wisdom" popularized by Jan Amos Comenius, aiming to organize all human knowledge into a single, harmonious structure.</term> should contain nothing but the substance of serious science.

<margin>5. General Theorems should be pure axioms.</margin>
**V.** Let the general precepts of Universal Wisdom be nothing but real and practical axioms: that is, statements worthy of belief in themselves <note>original: *per se fide dignæ*</note>, not needing to be demonstrated by prior principles, but only illustrated with examples. These are truths which every human intellect, as soon as it perceives them, accepts as a standard of truth <note>original: *pro varietatis norma*</note>. For such notions, divinely imprinted on our minds, will be torches of understanding to be lit concerning all particular things, and they will be the keys to our operations. But one must take the utmost care not to admit as an axiom anything that is not truly such. Indeed, it is not without reason that Verulamius <note>Francis Bacon, Lord Verulam (1561–1626), an English philosopher and statesman who famously criticized traditional logic in favor of the inductive method.</note>, Streso <note>Caspar Streso (1603–1664), a Dutch theologian known for his work on logic and rhetoric.</note>, and others complain that the common rules of Logic rashly affirm or deny anything; the result is that they rarely serve for a true demonstration, but mostly only for dispute—which is carried out by making exceptions, limiting terms, insisting, and retorting; that is to say, by bickering.

<margin>6. Particulars are pure Demonstrations.</margin>
**VI.** Let all particulars throughout the whole of Universal Wisdom bring nothing new; but let them only be a more specific deduction of the general principles that went before, just as we see happening in the growth of a tree or an animal. In these, new parts do not sprout <note>original: *enascantur* (completed from the catchword on the following page)</note> every year...

<summary>This page argues that Universal Wisdom must avoid trivial tautologies and instead rely on "axioms"—self-evident, divinely imprinted truths that serve as the foundation for all practical knowledge. It also critiques traditional logic for being more suited to argumentative bickering than to actual scientific demonstration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the pedagogical and logical structure of Pansophia (Universal Wisdom). It picks up from the previous page's discussion on axioms, moving toward the benefits of a demonstrative method where knowledge is built sequentially rather than by relying on the reputation of ancient authors.</meta>

(74)
<meta>catchword: dere,</meta>

...members or branches spring forth; but rather, that the prior parts are first completed. Arranged in this manner, **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: The author's project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</term> will be:

1. **Easy to perceive.** For one thing will flow naturally from another.
2. **Solid in truth.** For all subsequent points will be founded upon those that came before.
3. **Distinguished in use.** For the underlying reasons of all conceivable things are remarkably revealed.

And such a book will truly be not only a nursery <note>original: *seminarium*</note> of every kind of science, but also a kind of irrigation system, making everything take deep root: so that students are not merely enticed by persuasive words, but are compelled to give their assent by demonstrations; this is what finally makes learning firm.

Just as, therefore, among *Mathematicians*, no one says: "*Pythagoras* teaches that three times three is nine," or "*Euclid* defines three species of continuous quantity: the line, the surface, and the solid body." All *Geometricians* agree that in a triangle, the three angles are equal to two right angles. (Anyone who would foolishly rattle off authorities <note>The author is criticizing the medieval habit of proving a point simply by quoting a famous ancient author like Aristotle or Galen, rather than proving it through logic or observation.</note> in this way would be laughed at.) Instead, they demonstrate that it is so, and that it could not be otherwise, even if no *Pythagoras* or *Euclid* had ever been born. In the same way, hereafter in *Metaphysics*, *Physics*, *Ethics*, and *Politics*, it should be considered a shame to cite authorities where the matter can be dealt with through reason.

But we also intend by such a method that the whole work of *Universal Wisdom* may be able to proceed without the repetition of any single thing...

<summary>The author argues that Universal Wisdom should be taught through logical demonstration rather than an appeal to historical authorities, making it easier to learn and more resistant to doubt. He compares this method to geometry, where truths are self-evident through reasoning regardless of who first discovered them.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, pedagogy, mathematics, Pythagoras, Euclid, demonstration, logic vs authority, Metaphysics, Physics, Ethics, Politics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the structural methodology of Universal Wisdom (Pansophia). It follows from the previous page's argument that knowledge should be built upon foundations of logic and demonstration rather than simply citing historical authorities.</meta>

(75)
<sig>E 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: then</meta>

[procee]d, <note>This completes the word from the previous page, likely 'procedere' (to proceed).</note> and that brevity—even though the entire work is packed with a dense solidity of subject matter—will bring no obscurity. This is because, as one moves forward step-by-step, everything that follows will naturally receive light from those things already known. Thus, we will successfully begin to apply even in the writing of books that rule used in **military architecture** <term>Architectonica militaris: The science of designing and building fortifications.</term>: *No place defends itself, but is defended by another.* This principle holds if each and every point being taught gains its clarity and strength from what has already been established.

<margin>Note Well</margin>
However, in more serious matters, the authority of **Holy Scripture** must be added as the testimony of God's own mouth. We must also include the testimony of the senses through physical demonstration <note>original: *ostensionem*</note>, so that the truth of the matter may be proven by reason through experiments. Specifically, just as among *mathematicians*, once a theorem is demonstrated, certain knowledge follows, and once a problem is solved, a certain result follows; so too, from the principles of **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: The author’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</term>, the understanding and practical application of things should follow without fail.

<margin>7. If something cannot be demonstrated, it should be proposed as a problem.</margin>
**VII.** If, however, some point cannot be brought to that level of demonstrative certainty but is nevertheless useful to know, it should be placed among things for further deliberation. Alternatively, it should be posed as a "problem" <note>In the classical sense, a 'problem' is a question proposed for academic debate where the answer is not yet settled.</note>, with arguments provided for both sides of the issue. This allows everyone the freedom to judge the weight of the arguments and decide which way they are more inclined to lean:

<summary>The author argues that a book of universal knowledge should be structured like a fortress, where every point is supported by the ones preceding it. He emphasizes that while logical demonstration is the goal, weightier matters require the support of Scripture and sensory evidence.</summary>
<keywords>logic, military architecture, Holy Scripture, scientific demonstration, Pansophia, methodology, experimental proof</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methodology of Pansophia. It picks up from the previous page's point that if a concept cannot be fully demonstrated yet, it should be presented as a challenge for future scholars to solve, rather than being ignored.</meta>

[demonstrability] <note>Completing the thought from the previous page.</note>, then so that an opportunity may be left for others to eventually solve such problems and to extract the infallible evidence of truth even in such matters. For God is accustomed to communicate His light to men step-by-step; and wherever one proceeds step-by-step, it is only natural to be carried forward even further by degrees. Meanwhile, those things which shall have been handed down systematically <note>original: *scientifice*</note>, although they may be fewer in number, will nevertheless be more effective in practice. For it is better to hold a few things—provided they are certain, complete, and correct—than to be tossed about through infinite details; for the latter brings only a vague and conjectural knowledge, while the former confers a constant and firm erudition.

<margin>Praise for this method.</margin>
Between such a book of **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: The author’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</term>, if it were fully completed, and the books we use now, there would be the same difference as that between a musical organ <term>Musicum organum</term> most aptly tuned for full harmony, and many imperfect organs of that kind which are mutilated in their principal parts, with pipes disturbed here and there and discordant. Or, it is like the difference between an accurate musical score <term>Tabellatura: A form of musical notation using letters or numbers common for organ or lute players.</term>, by the sight of which alone the organist <term>Organarius</term> or the lutenist <term>Citharoedus: A player of the lute or cithara.</term> easily plays any melodies, and those scattered books of ensemble parts <term>Symphonia: Here referring to multi-part musical compositions where performers have separate booklets.</term> which cannot be used except by many people together, and which perhaps do not always and everywhere agree.

<margin>Why the author attempts this.</margin>
How great the [value] of such a book would be for wis[dom]... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with the word *sapientiae* (of wisdom).</note>
