(77)
E 3

...studies of wisdom <note>Completing the word *sapientiae* or *scientiae* from the previous page.</note>, having considered these things so many times within myself, I have suppliantly worshipped God, and I do not cease to plead with Him in my prayers that He might rouse **heroic minds** <note>original: *Heroica ingenia*</note>, through whom such a great light might be kindled for the world. And because He has granted me to be among the number of those who recognize the **imperfection of human affairs** and seriously desire their improvement for the better, I did not think it would be out of place if I were to attempt it myself. I wished to discover, you see, whether **divine kindness** might wish to provide some new light through me—for that **heavenly wind** <note>A reference to the Holy Spirit; see John 3:8: "The wind blows where it wishes."</note> blows where it wills—or at least that my work might serve as a stimulus to others. These are people to whom **divine providence** has granted more talent, learning, and leisure to achieve greater things in this field than I ever could. And who would count it a fault if I should vehemently desire to be of use to **Christian youth**, whether through my own efforts or those of others?

However, I completely feared that a matter of such great necessity might remain only as a wish if nothing but prayers were set before the public. Therefore, I have done what God has enabled me to do, so that I may be better understood now that a sample <note>original: *specimine*</note> has been provided, and so that others whom God will rouse may have an example to imitate more easily.

I do not think I should pass over in silence the occasion that led me to dare such a great undertaking, and the order and method I used in composing it, and finally...

<summary>The author explains his motivation for publishing a "sample" of his work: he hopes to inspire more talented scholars to complete the task of organizing human knowledge, fearing that otherwise the project would remain a mere wish.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's autobiographical reflection on the origins of his educational reforms. It follows the previous page's discussion on seeking a "new light" for education and explains how his own late start in Latin motivated his work.</meta>

(78)

...and with what success, in my judgment? All these things I mention for this end alone: because I consider it necessary, in order to elicit a true judgment, to show above all what I have proposed. What I have actually achieved, however, will be found in the body of the work—namely, our little **Pansophic book** <note>original: *Panſophicus noſter libellus*. "Pansophia" or "Universal Wisdom" was the author's system for organizing all human knowledge.</note> itself. From this, a conclusion will spontaneously arise in the minds of those who judge fairly.

<margin>The occasion for this work explained more deeply.</margin>
It is now the twentieth year since I first felt the desire and the drive to seek remedies for the **difficulties of academic labors** <note>original: *literariorum laborum*</note>. This arose from the occasion of my own misfortune, which—alas!—robbed me of almost all the years of my youth. For as a young boy, having been orphaned of both parents, I was so neglected by the laziness of my guardians that it was only in my sixteenth year of age that I happened to get my first taste of the **Latin elements** <note>The basics of the Latin language, which was the foundation of all higher education at the time.</note>.

Yet it was through God’s kindness that this taste so inflamed the thirst of my natural desire that, from that time on, I did not cease to act and strive to find ways to repair the losses of my missed years—not only for myself, but for others as well. For I simultaneously felt pity for the lot of others, especially among my own people <note>The author, Jan Amos Comenius, is referring to his fellow Moravians/Czechs.</note> who were somewhat sluggish in cultivating letters. Therefore, I pondered many things deeply: how as many people as possible might be stirred to love **liberal studies** <note>The "liberal arts," or the traditional subjects of a well-rounded education.</note>, and by what shortcuts of expense and labor schools might be opened...

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>The author recounts his personal history, explaining how his own delayed education and status as an orphan drove him to find more efficient ways to teach Latin and the liberal arts. He presents his "Pansophic" work as a solution to these academic hardships.</summary>

(79)

<sig>E 4</sig>

...and in them, the youth might be promoted to some notable progress in learning by an easier method. But because I was soon (in the 24th year of my age) called to the ministry of the Church, and that divine command, "**DO THIS**" <note>original: *HOC AGE*. A common Latin exhortation to focus entirely on the task at hand; for Comenius, this meant prioritizing his religious duties over his educational interests.</note>, was before my eyes, those scholastic cares had to be set aside. But when five years later, by God's permission, I was exiled from my homeland along with others, and for the sake of sustaining life in exile I returned to the **scholastic dust** <note>original: *scholasticos pulveres*. A classical metaphor for the toil and "dusty" atmosphere of the schoolroom and academic study.</note>, I applied my mind to the reading of various authors. I came across several who, in these times, have begun to labor over improving the method of studies: Ratichius, Helvicus, Rhenius, Ritterus, Glaumium, Caecilius <note>These were prominent 17th-century educational reformers, such as Wolfgang Ratke and Christoph Helwig, who sought more efficient ways to teach languages and sciences.</note>, and him whom it was especially fitting to name, *Johann Valentin Andreae*, a man of fervent spirit and a purified mind, as well as *Campanella* and *Verulamius* <note>Francis Bacon, known as Lord Verulam, whose works on the advancement of learning profoundly influenced Comenius.</note>, the glorious restorers of Philosophy. Raised to great hope by reading them—that at last so many and such varied sparks might burst into a flame—I nevertheless seemed to notice certain defects and gaps here and there. I could not restrain myself from attempting something resting on unmovable foundations, which, if it could be devised, would allow no wavering. Therefore, after many agitations of the mind, by measuring everything against the unmovable laws and norms of nature...

<meta>This page continues Comenius's narrative of his pedagogical development. Following his study of earlier reformers, he describes the birth of his own major works: the "Great Didactic" and the "Gate of Tongues." He transitions from focusing on language instruction (words) to the broader goal of "Pansophy" or universal wisdom (things), driven by his belief in the underlying harmony of the universe.</meta>

(80)

...from under my hand was born the **GREAT DIDACTIC**, presenting the art of teaching all things to all people quickly and thoroughly.

<margin>The *Gate of Tongues* provided the opportunity for the *Gate of Things*.</margin>

Following the rules established there, I attempted a shortcut for teaching languages. I published it under the title *The Gate of Tongues Unlocked* <note>original: *Ianua linguarum reserata*. This was Comenius’s breakthrough textbook (1631), which taught Latin by describing the natural world rather than through abstract grammar rules.</note> for the purpose of evaluation. It happened that the work was received with such applause and approved by the nearly unanimous consensus of learned men as a true and genuine method for teaching languages. From this, I saw a new opportunity arise to attempt a **GATE OF THINGS** <note>original: *Rerum Ianua*. Having simplified how to learn words, Comenius now sought to simplify how to learn the actual subjects of science, nature, and divinity.</note> as well—or, if you prefer, a **Key to the Human Intellect**—which refers everything universally back to the senses. 

From this small effort, if it succeeded, I believed much more profit was to be hoped for; for it is far better to possess true wisdom than to merely be able to stammer out a few Latin words. Indeed, there were those who simply doubted that such a "Gate" or "Key" to Things could be constructed—for I had shared my plan with friends. However, I was remarkably encouraged by my own deep-seated conviction regarding a universal and constant **Harmony of Things** <note>original: *Harmonia rerum*. The belief that the macrocosm (the universe) and the microcosm (the human mind) are perfectly synchronized according to a divine plan.</note>. I believed that everything man needs to know could be reduced to certain rules—rules that are finite in number, yet infinite in their application. For I reasoned thus with myself: If language, that most agile interpreter of the mind, delights in pouring itself out into infinite abundance...

<summary>Comenius describes how the success of his language textbook, the Gate of Tongues, inspired him to create a "Gate of Things" to teach universal knowledge. He argues that understanding the world through the senses is more valuable than rote language learning, guided by his belief in a universal harmony.</summary>

<keywords>Great Didactic, Gate of Tongues, Gate of Things, pedagogy, universal harmony, sense perception, Latin instruction, pansophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument for "Pansophy" (universal wisdom). (81)

<sig>E 5</sig>

...[in a certain] way, nevertheless, it could be enclosed within such boundaries that, with a finite number of words, it must necessarily express all the principal concepts of the mind; why should the concepts of our wandering mind not likewise be capable of being restrained by the barriers of **reality** itself? For although things outside the intellect seem to be something infinite, they are not; for even the world itself, that stupendous work of God, consists of only a few <term>elements</term> and a few differences of forms, and all that has been devised in the arts can be reduced to certain categories and specific totals. 

Since, therefore, **THINGS**, **CONCEPTS of Things**, and the images of those concepts—**WORDS**—exist in parallel, and since in each of these there are certain fundamentals from which everything else results, I began to think that these very fundamentals could be taught equally regarding things and concepts as they are in speech. 

It also occurred to me that **Chemists** <note>original: *Chymicos*. In this era, "chemistry" included alchemy and the pharmaceutical extraction of "essences" from raw materials.</note> had found a way to free the essences or "spirits" of things from the excess of matter, so that a vast power of minerals or plants might be concentrated into a tiny drop, proving more effective in medicine than when administered in their bulky, natural state. And I thought that nothing better could be devised than a means by which the <term>precepts of wisdom</term>, now scattered across the vast territories <note>original: *pomoeria*. Traditionally the sacred boundary of a city; here used to describe the limits of various scientific disciplines.</note> of the sciences—and indeed dispersed almost to infinity beyond their proper bounds—might be gathered together...

<summary>The author argues that just as a language uses a limited vocabulary to express all thoughts, the infinite variety of the world can be reduced to a few fundamental principles. He compares this systematization of knowledge to chemical distillation, where a powerful essence is extracted from a large amount of raw material.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophy, universal wisdom, elements of nature, distillation, logic and reality, systematic education, Comenius, philosophy of science</keywords>

(82)

...in a certain way, and be able to be concentrated? 

Far be it from us to despair: all despair is an insult to God, who promised help to those who ask, seek, and knock. I therefore determined that some ***Universal Seminary of Learning*** <note>original: *Universale aliquod Eruditionis Seminarium*. Comenius envisioned a school or system that would cultivate all branches of knowledge in every student.</note> could be established.

<margin>What the author proposed in the weaving together of the *Gate of Things* <note>original: *Janua Rerum*. This refers to his projected work on metaphysics and science, following the success of his *Janua Linguarum* (Gate of Tongues).</note>.</margin>
Therefore, in the name of God, I began this web, proposing to observe the same method here that I used in the weaving together of the *Gate of Tongues*. 

**First**, just as all the words of the Latin language were gathered there, so here all **THINGS** worthy of human knowledge should be brought together, as if into a Treasury. 

**Second**, that nothing should be placed more than once, except for connecting words and those without the mention of which others cannot be explained. 

**Third**, that nothing should be placed except in its own place and proper sense—that is, in the most natural order of things and the clearest sense of words—so that whatever noteworthy thing the universe of things possesses, and all that books and libraries provide, may all be learned here in summary, briefly and lucidly. 

If we should achieve this—so that through this "Gate" of ours, a passage might be prepared into all things and all books, to be understood even without a teacher—we would surely gain a share of that praise which **Timothy Bright** <note>Timothy Bright (c. 1550–1615) was an English physician who invented an early system of shorthand. Comenius admires him here for his focus on "brevity and clarity."</note> attributes to the inventors of brevity and clarity. From various, in—

<summary>Comenius proposes applying the systematic method of his famous language textbook to all human knowledge, creating a "Gate of Things" (Janua Rerum). He aims to organize every notable concept in the universe into a single, clear, and non-repetitive treasury of learning.</summary>

<keywords>Janua Rerum, Universal Seminary of Learning, Timothy Bright, educational method, brevity, organization of knowledge, Janua Linguarum</keywords>

(83)
<meta>catchword: true</meta>

> ...[he] says, regarding those parts of **Philosophy** in which lovers of truth and students of the best things have labored: none is either more useful for life, or feeds the mind with a more honorable pleasure, than that by which other arts are shaped toward both **brevity** and **clarity**. Therefore, the study of those who so apply their effort that learners are relieved of the boredom of wordiness and freed from the thorns and labyrinths of difficulty is to be celebrated with grateful memory.
> — (on Scribonius’s <note>Wilhelm Adolf Scribonius (1550–1600) was a German philosopher and educator whose works on logic and natural philosophy were widely used in the 17th century.</note> *Physics*, chapter 1).

And so it is; this was our sole intention when we first set our hand to this work: to embrace everything that must be known, briefly and clearly. But by the gift of God it happened that, as the work progressed, a more sublime thought and care came upon us: namely, to establish everything from its deepest **foundations** <term>foundations: the "principles" or "roots" (*fundamenta*) upon which all other knowledge rests</term> as truer, better, and—for us **Christians**—more suited for the use of this life and the life to come. I have already discussed at greater length why this must necessarily be our aim.

Therefore, so that this little work might truly be a **Gate** <note>original: *Janua*. A reference to Comenius's famous "Gate of Tongues" and "Gate of Things," designed as introductory textbooks to provide a structured entrance into a subject.</note>, not only to the works of Authors but even more to the universe of things itself, I have brought here everything that I noticed exists in both divine and human works as well as books. I have done this not by weaving together catalogs of each and every thing with a superstitious and hardly useful diligence, but rather by laying down the foundations...

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his unique method for organizing knowledge. He explains that by following a logical, universal order, he has discovered new insights that were previously overlooked. He now shifts focus to how this system—which he calls the "Gate of Things"—serves as an introduction to the Bible, integrating theology and scripture into his educational framework.</meta>

(84)

...by revealing the truth of all things and explaining the most important principles within the most important subjects, so that the logic of the rest follows easily. In this way, you will find here new things—not only things already discovered, but things yet to be discovered—which perhaps are found nowhere else. Indeed, this new and more complete ordering of subjects has often led us to places where no one else seems to have traveled yet.

<margin>This Gate of Things is also a Gate to the Divine Scriptures</margin>
To ensure this work also serves as a gateway to **Holy Scripture**, I have attempted, first: to organize all the doctrines of God’s sacred book into the rules of **Pansophia** <term>Pansophia: "Universal Wisdom," the 17th-century ideal of an all-encompassing system of knowledge that harmonizes science, philosophy, and religion</term>, placing each in its proper context, and including nearly all the biblical histories as illustrative examples. I have done this so that young people might come to know "the exceeding great and precious promises" given to us, through which we are made "partakers of the divine nature" <note>original: *2 Pet. 1:4*. A reference to the Second Epistle of Peter in the New Testament.</note>. This way, they are not only prepared to safely study secular <note>original: *prophanos*. Referring to non-religious classical authors like Cicero or Virgil, who were standard in schools but sometimes viewed as spiritually risky.</note> authors later (when the need arises), but also possess a fortification against the obstacles of their entire lives. 

Second: I have worked to cite the most difficult passages of the Bible in their natural contexts. By placing these citations exactly where they belong, the context itself sheds light on their meaning, serving almost as a commentary. (A special index of these biblical passages could also be added later.) Third: I have made an effort to use biblical phrasing and sharp, memorable sayings throughout the text. My goal is for young students to become thoroughly familiar with both the subject matter and the very style of the **Holy Spirit**.

<meta>catchword: nor</meta>

<summary>The author explains how his "Gate of Things" applies a new logical order to discover hidden truths and integrates Holy Scripture into his educational system. He aims to familiarize students with both divine doctrine and biblical style to prepare them for both secular studies and spiritual life.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Holy Scripture, biblical education, educational method, spiritual fortification, Holy Spirit, 2 Peter 1:4</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that his pedagogical system (the "Gate of Things") acts as a comprehensive introduction to the Bible. (85)

...nor can anything easily occur in the body of **Holy Scripture** <note>original: *Bibliorum corpore*</note> itself that has not been previously recognized here. Nor do we hand down this or that system of **Theology** as they are currently divided into sects, but rather the universal and catholic truth <note>original: *universalem & Catholicam veritatem*. Here, "catholic" is used in its original sense of "universal" or "general," rather than referring specifically to the Roman Catholic Church.</note>. I do not descend into those matters which lead to a dangerous crossroads <note>original: *periculosum bivium*. A metaphor for a difficult choice or a theological dilemma where two paths seem equally valid or dangerous.</note>, or where a clear path to level ground cannot be shown. For we consider it better to be ignorant of certain things than to know them wrongly, as the Apostle suggests in *Philippians 3:15, 16*.

Nevertheless, through this universal balancing of principles, I hope the prudent reader will easily observe that hardly any occasion remains for such dangerous crossroads—and the errors that follow—since most stumbling blocks have been removed. 

For just as it is impossible for a man who devotes himself simply and without reconciliation to one of two contradictory statements (such as occur in **Scripture** even in its literal sense) not to be pressured by the other, and often even violently tormented by it; so it is impossible—once a middle sense is found and both extremes are brought into combination—for whatever difficulty, scandal, or terror was present not to vanish simultaneously, to the great joy of the soul. For in this way, whatever truth each side possesses comes together excellently into one; whatever of vanity was mixed in here or there disappears, [showing] how all truth is one... <meta>catchword: truth-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that his "Pansophic" method reconciles seemingly contradictory passages in the Bible by finding a "middle sense." By focusing on universal truths rather than sectarian disputes, he aims to provide a clear, joy-filled path to theological understanding.</summary>
<keywords>Theology, Scripture, Pansophia, Sectarianism, Reconciliation of Contradictions, Universal Truth, Philippians 3:15-16, Biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's description of his pedagogical system. Following the previous page’s discussion on reconciling contradictory biblical passages, the author now transitions to describing the "Method" of his work, specifically how he has structured "Metaphysics" to be accessible even to children.</meta>

(86)

<margin>
The Method of this book.
</margin>

We have already spoken of the way to bring truth into the center of **Harmony** <note>original: *Harmoniae*. A central concept in this period, referring to the cosmic and intellectual reconciliation of all knowledge and faith.</note> and to remove controversies from the world. Regarding the order of handing down the **Sciences** <note>original: *Scientias*. In the 17th century, this term referred broadly to any organized body of knowledge or academic discipline.</note>, we hope that by God’s gift we have achieved this: that a sample may be provided of a method that correctly carves matters exactly as they are and as they happen, spreading them out clearly before our eyes. 

For I am confident that the truest "veins" of things have been discovered by us in **Metaphysics** <term>Metaphysics: The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter, and the nature of being.</term>. By following these veins, one arrives most clearly at individual things and even the very smallest reasons for concepts and words, so that a new, true, and hitherto unseen **Anatomy of the Universe** <note>original: *universitatis Anatome*. This suggests a structural dissection of all existence to reveal its inner workings.</note> might be established. 

Yet this is not achieved through some thorny subtlety that can only be understood by sharp minds or those already expert in such matters. Instead, it is presented with a plain reason so accessible to any sound mind that even eight-year-old boys could grasp our entire Metaphysics—and through its help, all the lower sciences and arts—with no labor and, indeed, with great delight. 

Whatever constitutes the "nerves" <note>original: *nervos*. Referring to the essential strength, core logic, or vital connections.</note> of science, we include in **Aphorisms** <term>Aphorisms (or Axioms): Short, concise statements of a principle or truth, intended to be self-evident and foundational.</term>. These are true (I hope) and not trivial; they are solid and not exposed to the arrows of exceptions or objections, which would otherwise need to be defended by the weak shields of constant qualifications. Instead, they stand unshaken by the light of their own universality and the power of truth. Yet we hand down nothing of these things, however much they possess evident truth in themselves...

<summary>The author describes his new "Method," claiming to have discovered the fundamental "veins" of knowledge in Metaphysics. He asserts that his system is so clear and logically structured that it can be understood by children, replacing complex scholastic debates with universal "Aphorisms."</summary>
<keywords>Method, Harmony, Metaphysics, Universal Anatomy, Aphorisms, Axioms, Education of children, Universal Truth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of his pedagogical method, moving from the accessibility of the material (even for children) to the rigorous, logical structure of the system and his discovery of "trichotomy" (division into three) as a fundamental law of reality.</meta>

(87)

...we deliver, not by presupposing anything as if we were begging for your agreement, but by demonstrating it beforehand. That is, we labor to deduce one thing from another, proceeding continuously from the first principles of **Metaphysics** <term>Metaphysics: The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including existence and the relationships between things.</term> down to the final and smallest differences between things. We do this indeed with the same clarity with which the propositions of Mathematicians are usually demonstrated. Consequently, it is necessary to place as much trust in the final conclusions as in the first principles, because the demonstration of truth is continuous and never interrupted. In the books of **pre-cognitions** <note>original: *præcognitorum*. This refers to introductory or preliminary studies that must be mastered before the main science.</note> alone, this rigor could not be fully observed because the underlying causes of things had not yet been handed down. Therefore, in those books, we deal with the **human intellect** as trainers usually do with young horses. They first use softer bits to delight rather than press upon them; and they exercise them in running through flat and pleasant places before they force them to run in tight circles.

<margin>
Three-way divisions
</margin>
This peculiar and even wonderful thing occurs in our method: all the more formal divisions of things are made through **Trichotomy** <term>Trichotomy: The division or classification of something into three parts or categories.</term>. I swear that I did not seek this out through some affected superstition. Rather, in all the principal matters of greatest importance—from the very first attributes of things, namely the **one, the true, and the good** <note>original: *uno, vero, bono*. In medieval and early modern philosophy, these were known as the "Transcendentals," properties that apply to everything that exists.</note>—it offered itself so spontaneously that I stood astonished for a while, stunned by the novelty of the matter. And since the same elsewhere...

<summary>The author explains that his method uses mathematical-like rigor to deduce truths from first principles and introduces his "discovery" that all reality is naturally structured in groups of three (trichotomy). He compares his gentle introductory teaching style to the way a trainer carefully breaks in a young horse.</summary>
<keywords>deductive reasoning, mathematical logic, pedagogy, horse training metaphor, Trichotomy, Transcendentals, first principles</keywords>

...now encouraged by these examples, I tried [this method], and I saw it succeed everywhere. Therefore, I did not dare to resist the truth of things, which so often presents itself in a **triple mystery**. Rather, joyfully embracing such harmony of the **sacred triad**, I eagerly followed it in the remaining parts as well. Nor was any force brought upon the things (I firmly believe), but they split themselves of their own accord <note>The author is arguing that his "rule of three" is not a forced system he invented, but a natural structure he discovered in the world.</note>. I also believed this would be very profitable for learners. First, as an aid to memory, which delights in proceeding through structured paths <note>original: *cancellos*. Literally "lattices" or "railings," implying a guided framework.</note>, both in finding and remembering. Second, for the understanding of the things themselves; the nature of things thus remarkably revealing itself. In this matter, I appeal to the judgments of all who will weigh these things piously and attentively in the fear of the Lord: I am secure in the knowledge that they will discover not the vanity of fantasies, but the truth of the things themselves.

Let this **Christian Pansophia** <term>Pansophia: From the Greek for "all-wisdom," referring to a system of universal knowledge that harmonizes all branches of learning with Christian theology.</term>, revealing **ternary mysteries**, be sacred to the eternal, **Triune Jehovah**, the only Powerful, Wise, Good, and eternally adorable God.

<margin>
*Defense of the name Pansophia.*
</margin>

The author includes the Greek word for "only" or "alone" to emphasize the biblical text.</note>. (*Romans 16:27*). We profess a human Pansophia, namely the knowledge of those things which God wants us to know, along with a wise ignorance of those things which He wished [us to be ignorant of]...

<summary>The author justifies his use of "trichotomy" (three-fold divisions) as a natural reflection of divine order that aids human memory and understanding. ...as the Greatest Master willed. <note>original: *luit magister maximus*. We do this so that men may understand how, through Him—that eternal wisdom and power of God—all things were made and are being made **"until the end comes; when He shall have delivered up the kingdom to the Father, and shall have abolished all rule and authority,"** (1 Corinthians 15:24). As they allow themselves to be ruled by His spirit, why should we not boast that we hand down, with Christ, the true and saving knowledge of all things? It certainly befits us Christians, and no others, to profess **Pansophia**; since outside of Christianity there is none, nor can there be. This is what **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose work *Against the Academics* argued against skepticism and for the certainty of Christian truth.</note> asserted long ago (*Against the Academics*, Book 3, Chapter 19), demonstrating that **Christianity alone is the true philosophy**. ...race, through wisdom gathered by reason; which feeds the soul for a few days with certain painted delights, only to send it away empty afterward? He therefore is wise, who is wise for eternity. For it alone can teach us how all our knowledge is nothing but a shadow when compared to the brightness of that eternal wisdom which is in God. 

Therefore, we maintain that **Pansophia**, or universal wisdom, should be the name for such a study as we recommend and attempt, for three reasons. First, with respect to the things themselves, whose "fragmentation" <note>original: *frustillationem*; the act of breaking knowledge into small, disconnected scraps rather than seeing the whole.</note> we discourage; and we teach that the powers of the human mind must be unfolded to master all things. Second, with respect to the sciences, which we commend <unclear>mu</unclear>s <note>The OCR suggests "commendamus" (we commend).</note>, not as separate disciplines, but as a single science embracing all things within its scope. Third, with respect to those for whose use it is prepared: namely, all who have taken the name of Christ. Thus, a triple fruit may be hoped for from this modest work: for the learned; for the youth in schools; and for all the Christian people.

<summary>The author defines Pansophia as a universal, God-centered wisdom that integrates all knowledge into a single system, contrasting it with the "fragmented" and "nonsensical" learning of those outside the faith. Because, however, we have undertaken to write not the **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: A 17th-century movement, led by figures like Comenius, aiming to organize all human knowledge into a single Christian framework.</term> itself, but the *Gate to Universal Wisdom*, we do not pursue every single thing entirely (which would be a task of infinite labor, and far beyond the strength of one man), but only the hinges and foundations of all things. [...let] all come to this Little Theater <note>original: *Theatriolum*. This is a diminutive form of "theater," a common metaphor in the Renaissance and Baroque periods for a book or collection that puts the whole world on display for the viewer.</note>: not to judge things according to what they have heard before, but according to the things themselves, just as we offer them to be seen and handled. For unless they observe this, they will deceive not the things, but themselves; just as he who looks through colored glass does not color and deceive the things he sees, but rather his own sight. We must take care, therefore, lest anyone stand in their own way by attending more to their own opinions than to the **truth of things**. I hope it will be a greater pleasure to all wise people if, relying on the privilege with which we are all equally endowed, we might contemplate things freely without intermediaries <note>original: *sine arbitris*. Literally "without judges" or "arbitrators," suggesting the author wants the reader to engage with nature directly rather than through the "authority" of ancient scholars.</note>, and offer to those learning not opinions about things, but the things themselves to be seen, handled, mastered, and possessed.

Nor let anyone suspect that we remove the views of others so that room might be made for our own. Far be that vanity from us. We do and shall do nothing else but what we transcribe onto our own tablet from the **Book of God, Nature, and Scripture** <note>original: *Codice Dei, Naturæ, & Scripturæ*. Early modern thinkers often spoke of "Two Books"—Nature and Scripture—through which God is revealed. If it is not done aptly enough, it will be a fault, not a fraud. We are too insignificant to seek to make **Disciples** for ourselves; but we are bold enough to make them for nature and for God. To that very end, we establish this new **Anatomy** of the universe <note>In this context, "Anatomy" refers to a systematic "cutting open" or detailed analysis of the structure of the world to reveal its inner workings.</note>, so that they might be led away from human books to the Great Book, whoever [lovers] of things...

<page-num>(95)</page-num>

...seek not opinions, but the truth. It is not that we reject all authority, but since we know that authority is often used harmfully to the prejudice of free judgment, we consider it only fair to suspend it for a while, as long as the things themselves are being thoroughly investigated. In the first and second books of our **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: *Pansophia*. This was a 17th-century concept, championed by figures like Comenius, aiming to organize all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system.</note>, we do cite certain things from the **Philosophers** <note>This refers to the established academic authorities of the era, such as Aristotle and the Scholastics.</note> to establish the foundations of this system. We do this not for the sake of the philosophers themselves, but for the sake of those readers who are so preoccupied with old opinions that they must be stripped of them—using as guides the very same teachers they have followed until now. After that, we cite others very rarely, and only in serious matters, when they have long ago noted the same truth. Even then, we use them not as judges, but as witnesses. Their testimony shows that the rational thoughts of all people necessarily coincide with those things that are drawn directly from the foundation of truth.

To rouse these people, God ordered the Prophet to address them with a word of rebuke. This people says:

> *The time has not yet come for the House of the Lord to be built. But is it indeed a time for you to dwell in your paneled houses <note>original: *domibus laqueatis*; referring to luxurious, ornate ceilings, contrasting private wealth with the neglected state of God's house.</note> while this house lies in ruins? Now, thus says the Lord of Hosts: Set your hearts upon your ways. You have sown much, yet brought in little; you have eaten, yet are not satisfied; you have drunk, yet are not refreshed; you have clothed yourselves, yet are not warm; and he who gathered wages put them into a purse with holes. Thus says the Lord of hosts: Set your hearts now upon your ways.*

Haggai 1. Hear these things, you who are also the leaders <note>original: *Antistites*; literally "overseers" or "high priests," here used to address the intellectual and spiritual elite.</note> of letters and wisdom among the holy Christian people! For most of you truly act and suffer in similar ways. Many say, "It is not yet the time to strive for some higher wisdom here; that is reserved for the life to come." Flattering themselves with such thoughts, a great many people rest content with that fragmented <note>original: *frustillatâ*; literally "broken into small bits or crumbs."</note> and obscure knowledge of things, and with their other private pleasures. But what profit comes from that? "You sow much," says the voice of God...

<summary>The author calls upon the learned elite to stop neglecting the pursuit of Universal Wisdom, comparing their excuses to the Israelites who delayed rebuilding the Temple in Jerusalem. He argues that current intellectual efforts are fragmented and unfulfilling because scholars have prioritized private interests over a unified, higher understanding.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Haggai 1, Temple of Jerusalem, intellectual duty, fragmentation of knowledge, exhortation, Christian scholars</keywords>

...in a similar zeal for cultivating intellects, indeed, you expend much effort, yet you bring little into your barns <note>Continuing the agricultural metaphor from Haggai: scholars work hard but store up little lasting substance.</note>. You eat—indeed, you devour—books, and yet you are not satisfied. You drink from any stream you come across, and yet you are not refreshed. You cover yourselves entirely with a covering, seeking the cloaks of authorities <note>original: *authoritatum pallia*; referring to the practice of relying on the prestige of ancient authors rather than direct truth.</note> from every side, yet you never grow warm enough with the light of truth. 

O, therefore, at last set your hearts upon your ways! Take courage so that a more splendid Temple of Wisdom, destined to dwell with us, may be built with whatever labor and expense is required. A Temple of Wisdom, I say, so that by divine art we may elegantly arrange a **Theater** <note>original: *Theatrum*; in the 17th century, this referred to a book or system that provides a comprehensive, visual, or systematic overview of a subject.</note> according to the form of the **Archetype** <note>original: *Archetypus*; the original divine pattern or perfect idea in the mind of God.</note>. From this Theater—as is fitting in divine matters—all useless, vain, and confused things should be absent, while nothing useful, true, or pleasant should be lacking. Thus, people might be called away from the frivolous study of transitory things and invited to the very fountains of the true and the good, led from the customary smoke of vanities to the possession of solid goods.

This will happen if such a **Door of Wisdom** <note>original: *Sapientiae Janua*; a reference to the author’s famous method of using "gateways" or introductory textbooks to lead students into complex subjects.</note> is opened in Christian schools to Christian youth, and the most precious Treasures of Wisdom are shown to them. 

<margin>This little work, when brought to its perfection, can primarily serve the youth.</margin>And this will be the second use of our little work <note>original: *opellæ*; a humble diminutive common in scholarly prefaces.</note>—for the education of youth—and this use is by far the most beneficial. For just as *new wine requires new wineskins* (as Christ warns in **Matthew 9:17**)...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the construction of a "Temple of Wisdom," transitioning from the critique of old scholarly habits to the specific potential of young minds and the universal scope of this new educational vision.</meta>

<page-num>(100)</page-num>

...are most fitting; so too the *minds* <note>original: *ingenia*; the natural talents or intellectual capacities of children</note> of children—like new and pure vessels not yet filled with the empty notions of hollow science—are most suitable for drinking in these new, purer concepts of reality. In this way, they may become accustomed to drinking in a wisdom that is not merely speculative, superficial, or noisy, but one that is real, fundamental, and solid. I speak of a Wisdom that is not designed for the empty display of intellectual vanity, nor for the "wrestling arena" of academic debates <note>original: *concertaminum palæstram*; referring to the competitive and often aggressive style of scholastic disputation common in the 17th century</note>, but rather for acquiring a sound judgment about things, for increasing the comforts of the human race through new inventions, and finally, to serve in unfailingly reaching the ultimate goal of life: eternal happiness <note>original: *beatitudinem æternam*</note>.

<margin>It is also remarkably beneficial for the entire Christian people.</margin>
Thirdly, we also intend that this **Little Amphitheater** <note>original: *Amphitheatriolum*; a diminutive of 'amphitheater,' used here as a metaphor for a systematic, visual, and accessible display of divine and natural knowledge</note> of the wisdom of God, constructed in such a way, should become common to all of humanity. This is to be done by inviting and admitting the whole Christian people—regardless of their rank, age, gender, or language—to observe and contemplate those marvelous "plays" which the adorable wisdom of God performs always and everywhere in the sight of all. For all who are born human must be guided toward the same end of God's glory and their own blessedness; no one is to be excluded—neither man nor woman, neither child nor elder, nor...

<summary>The author argues that children's minds are ideally suited for a "solid" education over empty academic posturing and asserts that divine wisdom should be accessible to all people regardless of status.</summary>

...[whether] noble or commoner; not just the artisan or the peasant, and so on. For we are all equally the offspring of God <note>Acts 17:28</note>; and to all of us, having been sent equally into the **theater of the world** <note>original: *mundi theatrum*; a popular 17th-century metaphor viewing the universe as a stage where God’s wisdom is performed for humanity to observe</note>, it has been said: *Come, and see the works of the Lord* <note>Psalm 46:9</note>. Finally, for everyone, this wretched age must be passed through with troubles and *affliction of spirit* (as **Solomon** testifies <note>A reference to the Book of Ecclesiastes, traditionally attributed to King Solomon, which reflects on the vanity and toil of life</note>), so that all people everywhere require an antidote to vanities and a relief for their weariness. These can be obtained from no other source than the aids of **true wisdom**.

We desire, therefore, and plead that the **studies of wisdom** <term>sapientia; the pursuit of deep, divine, and practical knowledge</term> no longer be handed down in Latin letters alone, so that they are held imprisoned in the schools. This has been done until now with the greatest contempt and injury toward the common people and the common languages. Instead, we desire that everything be handed down to each nation in its own mother tongue <note>original: *suomet sermone*; literally "in its very own speech"</note>. In this way, an opportunity may be provided to all who are human to occupy themselves with these **liberal pursuits**, rather than wearing themselves out with the cares of this life, or with ambitions, or drinking-bouts, or other vanities—as constantly happens—miserably losing both their lives and their souls at the same time.

It would also happen in this way that, along with the **Sciences** and **Arts**, the languages themselves would be elegantly polished. Therefore, we have also decided to set these works of ours out into the light in both **Latin** and the **vernacular** <note>original: *vernaculè*; the local, everyday language of the people</note>, if God permits. For *no one lights a lamp and hides it under a bu...* <note>original: *sub mo...*; referring to the "bushel" (modio) from Matthew 5:15. This is a catchword for the next page</note>

<summary>The author argues that wisdom should be accessible to all social classes in their own languages, rather than being "imprisoned" in Latin-only schooling. This universal education aims to provide a spiritual antidote to the hardships of life and to refine local languages alongside the sciences.</summary>

<margin>The reason for the title.</margin>
And for that purpose, we have changed the title somewhat from our previous little work, substituting **GATE** for **DOOR**. <note>The author is referring to his famous earlier textbook, *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Door of Tongues Unlocked). He is signaling that this new work is even broader in scope.</note> For it was sufficient to call the work on the Latin language a **DOOR**; here, the designation of **GATE** seemed better suited to the goal. For individuals enter through a door; but through a gate, entire crowds pass. A door is usually closed after individuals enter; but gates, in a peaceful city, remain open. 

In the same way, the study of the Latin language—which previously was the focus to be unlocked—is specific to certain people; but the desire for **Wisdom** is common to all of mankind. In the former case [Latin], those enter who wish to, or who have need of it; in the latter [Wisdom], all who are born human ought to enter, as we have already shown. Let there be, therefore, an open gate that leads to wisdom. Grant, O God, that we may see its image even here under heaven, which

<summary>The author explains why he has titled this work a "Gate" rather than a "Door," arguing that while Latin is for specialists, wisdom must be accessible to all of humanity like an open city gate. He uses biblical metaphors of lamps and treasures to emphasize that knowledge must be shared publicly rather than hidden.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to the civic and educational leaders of Wrocław. ## *Most Distinguished* LORDS,

THAT the undertaking of those who labor to bring back the long, complex, and arduous journey of **childhood studies** <note>original: "puerilium studiorum." Comenius is specifically addressing the education of young boys in the Latin schools of the 17th century.</note> to brevity, ease, and pleasantness by some art of **method** <note>original: "methodi." For Comenius, "Method" was a scientific and systematic approach to teaching that mirrored the laws of nature.</note> is truly praiseworthy; no one among all those who wish for the greatest welfare of human affairs would deny this. 

For since the **Life of men** <note>original: "Vita hominum."</note> should be spent not merely in **learning**, but in **acting and making use of things**: and yet only he is able to act and use who has learned; and only he has truly learned who has been rightly taught; it is clear that they employ their labor with total utility who, with some happy success, strive to provide teachers and students with the best and most **beneficial**— <note>original: "saluberrima." The text breaks off here at the end of the page; the word literally means "most health-giving," implying that a good education is vital for the health of the mind and the state.</note>

<summary>In this opening of the dedication's text, Comenius argues that because life is meant for action rather than endless study, education must be made efficient and pleasant through systematic methods. He posits that proper teaching is the necessary foundation for a productive and useful life.</summary>

<keywords>pedagogy, educational reform, method, active life, childhood education, Wrocław, human welfare</keywords>

—beneficial things are suggested, and that both <note>Refers to both the teacher and the student mentioned on the previous page.</note> be liberated from nausea and boredom (which arise all too abundantly from a poorly ordered handling of these labors). I will say even more, because it must be said: that those who achieve this ensure that *teachers teach less* than they do with the common *lack of method* <note>original Greek: "ἀμεθοδίᾳ" (amethodia). Comenius argues that teaching without a systematic "Method" is the root of educational failure.</note>; while *students learn more* than they do in the usual *butchery of intellects* <note>original: "carnicinâ," which refers to a rack, a torture chamber, or a place of execution. Since we are pursuing this very thing in this present little work—or at least seeking out with a steady investigation how it ought to be done in **Schools**, and demonstrating certain methods and paths—I dedicate this to your **HONOR**, Most Distinguished Lords, and for the **USE** of your studious youth (who are the **seedbed of your most flourishing Republic** <note>original: "Reipublicæ vestræ seminarium." In the 17th century, "Republic" referred to the commonwealth or the city-state of Wrocław. <summary>Comenius defines his pedagogical goal: to replace the "intellectual butchery" of traditional schools with a method where teachers teach less and students learn more. # TO THE MOST DISTINGUISHED MAN:

## OF LATIN ELEGANCE

->Finally, working on<-
->the work of **UNIVERSAL WISDOM**<- <note>original: "**PANSOPHIÆ**." Comenius's lifelong project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</note>

*With only a vestibule, without the refinement of later rooms,*

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Comenius's own theoretical explanation of his method. It follows the commendatory poem by Georg Vechner on page 111, transitioning from external praise to the author's personal justification for his pedagogical reforms.</meta>

(112)

<meta>catchword: guarum</meta>

<image-desc>Decorative headpiece consisting of four horizontal rows of repeating typographic ornaments including fleurons and scroll-work motifs.</image-desc>

## A Didactic Dissertation on Perfectly Establishing the Study of the Latin Language. <note>original: "De Latinæ Linguæ studio perfectè instituendo Dissertatio Didactica."</note>

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'A' featuring architectural motifs and foliate designs within a square frame.</image-desc>

**A**PELLES, Pliny is the authority <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author who recorded the history of ancient art in his *Natural History*.</note> who relates, was accustomed to display his painted works on a balcony <note>original: "pergula." A covered walkway or gallery where artists showed their work.</note> and, hiding behind the panel, would listen to the judgments passed on them. He did this not so much to tickle himself with his own praises, if he heard any, but rather so that he might correct any faults that he noticed were being pointed out by the common people, who are inquisitive censors of things. <note>Apelles was a renowned painter of ancient Greece; this anecdote illustrates Comenius's desire for constructive criticism of his new teaching methods.</note>

2. Since it is permissible for anyone attempting something of artistic merit to imitate this example, so that they may turn public criticism toward the advancement of their craft, I believed I should also be allowed to do the same regarding a matter of public benefit I have recently conceived and developed: namely, that new method for teaching languages more concisely <note>original: "compendiosiùs." Referring to his "short-cut" or efficient method of teaching.</note> which (following the example of others who attempted the same before us) [I have called] the *Gate* of Languages <note>original: "Linguarum." This likely refers to his famous *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Languages Unlocked), which revolutionized language learning by teaching vocabulary through sentences describing the natural world.</note>

<summary>Comenius begins a formal essay justifying his new educational methods, using the anecdote of the Greek painter Apelles to invite public feedback on his "Gate of Languages."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Didactic Dissertation," picking up from the end of page 112. The catchword "guarum" from the previous page completes the word "Linguarum" (Languages) at the start of this page, referring to the title of Comenius's famous textbook.</meta>

(113)

<meta>catchword: pum</meta>

...languages, which happened to be called the *Gate* <note>original: "Januam." This refers to Comenius's most famous work, *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Tongues Unlocked), first published in 1631.</note>.

3. Therefore, I exposed my little work to the light, and I hid myself, for six whole years exploring the various judgments of various people: *which fell into three categories in total.*

4. *For some, and indeed the majority, praised the invention*, thinking this method of teaching and learning languages to be such that a better one could not be given: specifically, because it was adapted to the pattern of Things themselves <note>A core principle of Comenius’s philosophy: "Realism." He believed students should learn about the world and language simultaneously, rather than memorizing abstract grammar rules.</note>.

5. Hence it happened that it was not only adorned with translations into various languages and printed in various places, but it was also received everywhere into schools—not only the common ones, but even the illustrious ones.

6. *One or two people were found who began to point out defects and to warn publicly that it should not be so praised:* they said the work was begun, not perfected, and that the public welfare of letters required it to be completed and improved.

7. *Finally, there were those who could neither bring themselves to praise nor to blame:* only whispering that they did not see how this little book could provide the promised use—namely, that by its benefit, a beginner <note>original: "tyro." Originally a military term for a raw recruit, used here for a student just starting their Latin studies.</note> in Latin could be introduced more easily, pleasantly, and quickly to the reading of Authors <note>Refers to the "Classical Authors" like Cicero, Virgil, and Ovid, which were the ultimate goal of Renaissance and Baroque education.</note>.

8. Hence it happened that they received it more hesitantly: waiting, clearly, for something more finished and better suited to the goal...

<summary>Comenius reflects on the six years following the publication of his "Gate of Tongues," categorizing public reaction into three groups: enthusiastic adopters, constructive critics, and skeptical observers. 9. *What then do I say to all this?* First, I allow it to become common property, and indeed I rejoice that it has been made so—whatever useful thing He who gives all things <note>A reference to God as the source of all intellect and invention.</note> has allowed me to discover.

<meta>This page continues from page 114, where Comenius was addressing critics or those who might improve upon his work. The text transitions from acknowledging potential improvements to outlining his formal defense and explanation of his educational system.</meta>

13. --- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 115, finishing the sentence regarding the systematic approach to education. Comenius is shifting from the "what" of his textbooks to the "why" and "how" of his broader didactic philosophy.</meta>

(116)

...by reason and method, having provided the means of dispensing instruction in such a way that it is impossible to wander from the goal.

17. *The goal of this didactic investigation is*, that the study of the Latin Language (which, after it ceased to be the property of a single nation, became the common *Mercury* <note>original: "Mercurius." In Roman mythology, Mercury was the messenger of the gods; here Comenius uses the name to describe Latin as the shared messenger or medium of communication for all Europe.</note> of the European nations, and once included in the schools, became the bond of the learned and the vehicle of learning) *may be rendered more expeditious: that is, easier, faster, and more fruitful than it has been until now.*

18. Specifically, that the use of this language might be reached without such great trouble, boredom, and disgust for teachers and students; nor with such a great waste of time and years; nor finally with such hindrance and loss of <term>real sciences</term> <note>original: "scientiarum realium." A core principle for Comenius was "Realism"—the idea that students should learn about concrete things and the laws of nature (reals) rather than just abstract grammar rules.</note>, as is currently the case: but rather that <term>Christian Youth</term> <note>original: "Christiana Iuventus."</note> may be led *quickly and safely, easily and pleasantly* <note>original: "citò & tutò, facilè & jucundè." This was Comenius's famous pedagogical motto, suggesting that education should be efficient and enjoyable rather than a burden.</note> into the best authors, and through their help into the knowledge of divine and human things.

19. For we do not undertake to train *parrots*, for whom it would be enough to chirp sounds without meaning; but rather a *rational creature, the mistress of things, the spectator, witness, and interpreter of the wisdom of God <note>original: "sapientiæ DEI."</note> spread throughout all things.* To distend this creature with words, rather than to fill it with <term>things</term> <note>original: "Rebus." Comenius argues that words are merely the husks or vehicles for the "things" they represent; teaching words without the underlying objects or concepts is an educational failure.</note>, is to wander from the goal of the divine plan, to diminish the majesty of man, and to shamefully deceive minds by instilling images and shadows in place of the knowledge of things.

20. Therefore, this is what must be done: that the
*study of Languages (especially Latin)*
*should focus on Things: so that it results*
*not in vain loquacity, but in wise Eloquence.* <note>original: "loquacitatem... Eloquentiam." Comenius contrasts "loquacity" (empty chatter) with "eloquence" (the ability to speak meaningfully and wisely about reality).</note>

21. We shall obtain this by no other means than
if *words are perpetually taught in connection*
*with Things: so that the Mind and the Tongue*
*are always educated by one and the same effort.*

22. *Words without Things* are shells
without a kernel, a scabbard without a sword,
a shadow without a body, a body without
a soul.

23. Therefore, he who measures learning
by the abundance of words, whenever he speaks,
says nothing but speech—that is, he prattles.

24. *Wisdom consists in Things, not in*
*Words: and a wise and truly learned man*
*thinks of things, speaks of things, and acts on things.*

25. From this it is concluded that *the divorce*
*of Words from Things must be avoided:* and
that Books, which are occupied only with
*Vocabulary* and occupy the youth, are useless
for this sacred intention. Understand this to mean

<meta>This page continues Comenius's argument for a "realist" education. The previous page ended mid-word with "com-", which this page completes as "compendio" (shortcut/abridgment), discussing how studying "things" alongside "words" creates a more efficient path to learning.</meta>

...[com]pendium, and through the most solid use, we can simultaneously acquire for ourselves both the knowledge of Things and the faculty of discoursing about things wisely, variously, and promptly: otherwise, never.

27. Let this therefore remain fixed: that the *Latin language, in order to be a real instrument for drawing out real learning, must be learned from Authors: and accordingly, the exposition of good Authors is everything* <note>original: "τὸ πᾶν." 28. This is a common Latin idiom meaning to make something an internal, vital part of oneself, much like how we say we "digest" information.</note> of the mind: so that young people leaving School may be found equally ready in mind, hand, and tongue, and fit both for judging wisely about things and for happily beginning their state of life.

29. --- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues Comenius’s argument that learning should be a social and gradual process. The previous page ended with the syllable "th-", which this page completes as "the light" (lumine), part of a metaphor about how minds stimulate one another.</meta>

(119)

[as the light] is kindled by light, and the virtue of a magnet is stirred by the application of iron, so by the rubbing together of minds, minds are sparked and bring forth their own power.

31. Let it therefore remain fixed: *the Latin language must be taught and learned only with Things and through Things: namely, from authors who treat actual Subjects.*

32. But truly, since whatever is done is done step by step (as the examples of Nature and Art show), and so even what is learned can only be learned gradually; it is evident that a beginner in Latinity cannot be thrust into that vast sea of authors without certain preparations and safeguards.

33. Otherwise, he will either be dashed against the rocks of difficulties (which he has not yet learned to overcome), or he will be swallowed up by the waves of variety, as if by a gaping abyss to which he is not yet accustomed.

34. Therefore, let him learn to play in a small boat on a little lake before he commits himself to the deep sea.

35. Let the young boy be allowed to ride first upon a long reed <note>original: "arundine longa." This refers to a hobby-horse, a common toy used by children to mimic riding before they are old enough for a real horse.</note> before he mounts caparisoned steeds that champ at the bit.

36. Let the infant learn to babble before he speaks; and the boy to speak before he practices oratory <note>original: "dicere." In the context of Roman education, *loqui* refers to everyday speaking, while *dicere* refers to the formal art of oratory or public declamation.</note>; for even Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman whose writings were the primary model for "perfect" Latin in the Renaissance.</note> (otherwise so great a master of the art of teaching) says he cannot teach a man how to deliver an oration if he does not already know how to speak.

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for a gradual, step-by-step approach to education. Having established on the previous page that a beginner should not be thrust into the "vast sea" of classical authors immediately, the text now provides natural and mechanical analogies to prove why learning must proceed by degrees.</meta>

(120)

<meta>catchword: 48.</meta>

37. And how could it be otherwise? All things that go upward do so only step by step.

38. From a plant's seed, or a kernel, nothing can first be born but a tiny sprig; from the sprig, finally, through slow growth, comes a little bush; and from this, a tree.

39. And in human life, no one attains manly years, stature, or strength, unless he was first an embryo, then an infant, a boy, and a youth.

40. Nor do buildings rise in any other way than that, in the first place, a rough but solid foundation is laid; then, walls are erected upon this; to the walls are added the vaulting of beams or arches; and in the last place, all those things are secured by a roof.

41. Even he who climbs to the summit of a tower, a cliff, or some mountain can only climb by raising himself step by step: so much so that ***Nature and Art*** <note>original: "Natura & Ars." In this context, "Art" refers to human skill, craft, or systematic method, following the classical idea that human ingenuity should imitate the natural order.</note> nowhere make a leap, nor ever allow one.

42. From this it becomes manifest that ***the study of the Latin language should not begin with whole and complete works—namely, the Authors—but from certain rudiments and beginner-tasks, which may rightly predispose and prepare minds for those greater things.***

43. But it is also clear that these ***Preparatory Lessons*** <note>original: "Præparamenta." This refers to the graded textbooks Comenius designed to bridge the gap between basic literacy and advanced literature.</note> cannot be of only one kind: because things that are very far apart can hardly be joined together by the intervention of only a single thing.

44. Certainly, while a tall building is [being] e- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; "e-" is likely the start of "erigitur" (raised or built).</note>

<note>Vocabulary from the original: Nature, Art, study of the Latin language, step-by-step, buildings, rudiments, beginner-tasks, Preparations.</note>

<summary>Comenius uses analogies from nature, human growth, and architecture to argue that learning Latin must be a gradual process. He concludes that students need graded preparatory materials rather than jumping straight into complex classical authors.</summary>
<keywords>gradualism, pedagogy, Latin instruction, natural order, architecture analogy, educational theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analogies of growth and construction to justify a graded curriculum. It completes the building metaphor and introduces the specific four-stage model of education that Comenius proposes for Latin studies.</meta>

...is erected, workers have work with several different storeys <note>original: "contignationibus," referring to the timber framing of floors or levels in a building.</note> before they place the roof-peak.

45. And just as there are certain steps or ages of a person's life that are clearly distinct, so too in the cultivation of **Minds** and **Language**, it is necessary that certain steps be established and so coordinated that one rises from the other.

46. For just as the dawn of the day is not sent ahead so that it might *remain* dawn, but so that by brightening little by little it might change darkness into light and night into day; and as a Painter does not draw the first outlines so that they may *remain* outlines, but so that, being more and more developed, they may take on the distinct form of all the limbs, and thereby represent the whole thing perfectly as it is: So the formation of **Language** begins from rudiments, so that it may be perfected by further progress, and at last find its end in eloquence.

47. It seems most convenient, then, to establish four steps or ages of Latin study: namely, as follows:

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the curriculum outline started on page 121, moving from the metaphorical justification of "steps" in learning to the specific definitions of each of the four levels of Latin mastery.</meta>

<page-num>(122)</page-num>

48. The first and lowest step, therefore, will be *to understand something of Latin, and to be able to utter it in whatever manner.* (For that truth must remain: *Whatever begins is unrefined.* Also: *He who never does a thing poorly, never does it well*; and: *No one avoids error except him who has erred as often as possible.*) <note>These pedagogical proverbs encourage the student to embrace the "stammering" phase of learning without fear of mistakes.</note>

49. The second step will be higher: *to be able to express any obvious things, and all the more common concepts of the mind, in a true, proper, and authentic sense:* without any harmful error in the meanings of the words, or fault in their formation <note>original: "formationibus," referring here to the grammatical construction and morphology of words.</note>.

50. The third, even more sublime step will teach *to utter one and the same thing and concept of a thing in different ways, and to paint it, as it were, with various colors.* <note>This refers to the rhetorical concept of "copia," or the ability to vary one's expression to suit different contexts.</note>

51. The fourth and final highest step will be to have learned how speech ought not to spread itself out and dilute into luxury; but rather *to contract itself through a careful choice of words and sentences, and to solidify around the **THINGS** themselves, so that it may powerfully impress itself, and the **THINGS** with it, upon the minds of others.*

52. This is the peak of Eloquence: to be able to depict each thing to the life in its own natural colors, not in those that are counterfeit or disguised, and thereby to captivate minds under the empire of the Things themselves. Hence that saying of Solomon: *The words of the wise are like goads, and like nails deeply driven.* (Ecclesiastes 12:11.)

<meta>vocabulary: step, of Latin, of Eloquence, of Solomon, Ecclesiastes 12:11.</meta>

<summary>This page defines Comenius’s four stages of Latin proficiency, progressing from basic comprehension and "clumsy" speech to a refined, powerful eloquence where language perfectly represents reality.</summary>
<keywords>Latin pedagogy, levels of learning, eloquence, rhetoric, Comenius, Solomon, linguistics, educational theory</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the abstract stages of language mastery defined on the previous page to the practical organizational structure of the school curriculum. It introduces Comenius’s famous four-tiered textbook system.</meta>

<page-num>(123)</page-num>

53. *Thus, there will be four distinct "Schools" or "Classes" of Latinity, as it were: the first for **stammering** <note>original: "balbutie," referring to the earliest stage of learning where one struggles to form words</note>, the second for **speech**, the third for **Fluency**, and the fourth to be attained for **Latin Eloquence**.*

54. *To each of these, its own portions and goals must be assigned—where each ought to begin and how far it should progress—and a certain and exquisite method must be described, so that the goal is necessarily reached everywhere within the given time.*

55. *Let these be distinct, and let each have its own specific goals as well as its means, and a method of applying those means; yet they should follow and correspond to one another in harmony.*

56. *For we desire this to be common to all those classes: that they be equipped with the same aids in a proportionate way—namely, with books that present everything to be known step by step, always with greater fullness, and through almost identical types of exercises to be practiced.*

57. To the book for the first Class, we have given the name **THE VESTIBULE** <note>original: "VESTIBVLI"</note>; for the second, **THE GATE** <note>original: "JANVÆ"</note>; for the third, **THE PALACE** <note>original: "PALATII"</note>; and for the fourth, **THE TREASURY** <note>original: "THESAVRI"</note> of Latinity.

58. It will be fitting for the structure of each of these books to be tripartite <note>divided into three parts</note>.

59. The first and fundamental part should contain the material <note>original: "τὸ materiale," using the Greek article for "the [essential] matter"</note> or the portion of things...

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...of things [rerum]</meta>

<summary>The author outlines a four-tiered Latin curriculum consisting of the Vestibule, Gate, Palace, and Treasury, each designed to lead the student from basic "stammering" to full eloquence. He specifies that while each level is distinct, they must all follow a harmonious, step-by-step methodology using structured textbooks.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, Latin curriculum, Vestibulum, Janua Linguarum, levels of learning, language acquisition, tripartite structure</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the tripartite (three-part) structure of Comenius’s language textbooks, detailing how each book should be organized to facilitate natural learning.</meta>

<page-num>(124)</page-num>

...and of words assigned to that Class—
that is, the *Text or Author*. The second
part should explain the formal aspect <note>original: "τὸ formale," the structural or grammatical framework</note>, or the measure
and *directory of words*, that is, the instructions
and *Rules*, through which the logic of the con-
structed speech in the aforementioned Author may be
uncovered, for an easier and truer
understanding of it. Finally, let an *Index*
*of contents* be added, to serve as a *Repertory* <note>a quick-reference storehouse</note>

swift use and practice.

60. The primary reason <note>original: "Ratio"</note> for this method
is this: that we teach *more gently, better, and more*
*effectively by examples than by rules: (for*
*Examples form the understanding through the senses, which*
*is natural; Rules form the senses through the understanding,*
*which is against nature.)* Therefore, let the
*finished Speech* <note>original: "Oratio facta," a completed piece of literature or text</note> come first; and finally, let the *Rules*
follow, illustrating how it was made,
and how another might be made similarly in imita-
tion of it. (Note: I say "illustrating":
for these must be such as to bring
light to this study, not darkness; and they
should be a help to it, not a hindrance;
nor should they present to young minds a new
struggle, more heavy than the text itself,
but rather resolve the struggle brought
about by the words.)

61. Then, because not everything
can be impressed upon the memory all at once
in such a way that it can be readily
brought forth for use, we must assist this
weakness by attaching an *Inventory*,
so that whatever needs to be taken up
again is close at hand.

<summary>Comenius argues that language should be taught using a "natural" sequence: providing a complete text first (examples) before introducing the grammatical rules that govern it. He emphasizes that rules should clarify the text rather than become an additional burden for the student.</summary>
<keywords>natural method, examples vs rules, textbook structure, pedagogy, memory aids, linguistic practice, sensory learning</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Comenius’s graded language learning system, specifically defining the content and purpose of the first two levels of his textbooks: the Vestibule (the introductory book) and the Gate (the intermediate book).</meta>

<page-num>(125)</page-num>

62. Thus, the **VESTIBULE** <note>original: "VESTIBVLVM"; the first and simplest of Comenius’s three language textbooks, acting as an "entrance hall" to learning</note> will contain in the first place the material for *Latin for beginners* <note>original: "Balbutici Latinæ," literally "Latin babbling" or early speech</note>, consisting of several hundred *vocabulary words* included in *short sentences*. Then, in place of the formal part, it will contain *tables of Declensions and Conjugations*, along with other first *rudiments of Grammar*. Finally, an *Index of the words included in the native language*: and nothing besides. For with the help of these, we shall reach the goal beautifully; nor is there any need for the book to be increased by useless additions that only add bulk to the volume, labor to the mind, and difficulty to the purses of the poor who must buy them.

63. **THE GATE** <note>original: "JANVA"; referring to Comenius's famous *Janua linguarum reserata* or "The Gate of Tongues Unlocked"</note> *should similarly propose*, first, the material necessary for *speech*: namely, *all the more common words of the Language*, roughly ten thousand in number, arranged into sentences easy in structure and sense; by which *the things themselves* <note>original: "Res ipsæ"</note> are expressed in summary, in their own order, and with a simple and natural appearance.

64. Therefore, nothing *metaphorical, figurative, unusual, or too specific* should be mixed in here. For the aim here is nothing else but to open the *Gate*, so that at the proper time and place, things may be known and spoken of more distinctly. Some, unmindful or careless of this plan, have burdened this *Gate* with useless additions: wishing to cram in I know not what, to the certain delay and disgust of the learners. This must be avoided hereafter.

<meta>catchword: 65.</meta>

<summary>Comenius outlines the specific contents of the "Vestibule" and the "Gate," emphasizing that the first should focus on basic sentences and grammar tables, while the second should cover common vocabulary without overly complex or figurative language.</summary>

65. Rather, we must work toward this: that we may have a true **GATE** <note>original: "JANVA"; Comenius's famous textbook *Janua linguarum reserata* (The Gate of Tongues Unlocked), which aimed to teach both language and worldly knowledge simultaneously</note> (as I had promised in its first preface) *well-secured by the solid doorposts of* **THINGS** *themselves; easily opened on the swinging hinges of the* **DICTIONARY**; *and quickly unlocked by the ready key of* **GRAMMAR**: that is <note>original: "h. e." for *hoc est*</note>, that we add grammatical rules and that promised dictionary to the text of the *Gate*, so that whatever the use of this level <note>original: "classis," referring to a specific stage or grade in the school system</note> requires may be held all at once.

66. To be sure, *very brief and very clear grammatical rules must be established, showing most plainly the true, genuine, and simple method of writing, pronouncing, forming, and constructing Latin speech*; but nonetheless touching upon nothing unusual, figurative, or highly irregular. For they must be illustrated with examples from the text of the **GATE** alone (where nothing so irregular or figurative is contained).

67. Finally, in the place of an index, let there be added an **ETYMOLOGICAL DICTIONARY**, *laying out before one's eyes the roots, or primary words, of the Latin terms contained in the Gate, along with their series of derivatives and compounds, and their meanings*.

68. For the third grade, there will be the **PALACE** <note>original: "PALATIVM"; the third and most advanced book in Comenius’s pedagogical series, following the *Vestibule* and the *Gate*, intended to lead the student into eloquent and complex mastery of the language</note> of Latinity, exhibiting a further development of more specific things, words, phrases, and rules, with all...
