with the principal techniques for decorating a speech, and any subject matter, through various modes of eloquent expression.

69. Therefore, various *discourses on Things* <note>original: "Rebus discursus"; thematic essays or discussions on the nature of the world</note> must be established: and indeed according to the Method of the things themselves, which the *Gate* <note>original: "Janua"; Comenius's intermediate textbook</note> designated with those hundred titles of Things.

70. Then, there must be added (*in the place of the formal part*) *whatever remains of the Grammatical instructions, concerning Anomalies* <note>irregular grammatical forms</note>, *figured Syntax, Hellenisms* <note>original: "Hellenismis"; Greek linguistic patterns used in Latin</note>, *etc., as well as the rules of Rhetoric, concerning the decoration of Speech, and the first rudiments of Logic, to be arranged in a popular method* <note>a simplified, accessible teaching style</note> (*such as* GRAMMAR *itself can partly supply, and part of which* RHETORIC *necessarily requires before itself*). *Regarding the excellent execution of this matter for the benefit of Youth, I would even dare to become a guarantor for a certain person here* <note>Comenius likely refers to a colleague or perhaps himself as the one capable of producing this work</note>.

71. *Moreover, it seems convenient that this* PALACE *of pure Latinity be constructed in a fourfold way according to the grade of style.*

72. *Namely, so that the Candidates of Latinity may learn, in the first* PALACE, *the Epistolary style* <note>the style used for writing letters</note>: *one that is indeed rapid and more casual, yet pure and flowing. In the second, the Historical style, rising a little higher and worked out with more care. In the third, the oratorical style, plainly accurate, grave, and sublime. Finally, the Poetic style, with meters* <note>original: "numeris"; the text ends mid-sentence, leading into a discussion of poetic rhythm</note>

...bound by poetic rhythms <note>original: "meris adstrictum"; literally "restricted to meters"</note>: then a simpler and more level style (such as is found in **Elegiac** poetry), then a grand and pompous one (such as is found in **Heroic, Lyric, and Tragic** poems).

74. Thus, led slowly step by step, as if guided by railings <note>original: "per cancellos"; referring to the practice of guiding a student's hand or path with physical boundaries</note>, they will more easily learn the character of the style, and how to distinguish and imitate it with distinctness and uniformity.

75. **The Epistolary Palace** <note>original: "Palatium Epistolicum"; the curriculum stage dedicated to letter writing</note> *should therefore contain one hundred letters, each corresponding to the individual titles of the* **Gate** <note>original: "Janua"; referring to Comenius's *Janua Linguarum Reserata*, his foundational textbook</note>: *through which may be distributed all the more elegant formulas for greeting, returning a greeting, narrating, requesting, giving thanks, etc.: so that the entire art of letter-writing may be present here, as it were, with very brief instructions on the art of writing letters added.*

76. **The Historical Palace** *should contain more distinguished narratives regarding the same subjects: with citations assigned to the margin indicating the **Authors** and the locations from which each passage was taken.*

77. *Moreover, four reasons persuade us that these such **Discourses on Things** should be conceived in a dialogistic form <note>the form of a dialogue or conversation</note>.*

78. <meta>This page continues the argument for using a conversational or dialogic format in education, specifically concluding the first reason (started on the previous page) and detailing three further reasons why student interaction is superior to passive recitation.</meta>

(129)

to youthful <note>original: "puerilibus"; literally "boyish" or "child-like"</note> minds, lest they imagine this handling of serious matters to be either impossible or too arduous and difficult for themselves.

79. Secondly, **Conversations excite**, animate, and foster attention. This is due to the variety of questions and answers, and their various occasions and forms, with delightful elements mixed in from time to time. Therefore, the students' minds are to be enticed with these lures, so that they may progress through these tasks without boredom <note>original: "fastidio"; a sense of weariness, distaste, or loathing for study</note>.

80. Thirdly, **Dialogues** serve both to create a firmer impression of the subject matter and to make review easier (even among the students themselves in private). For just as we more easily remember an event in which we were personally involved than one we only heard about, so things learned through the mode of a Play <note>original: "Comœdiæ"; referring here to the dramatic or performative aspect of dialogue rather than "comedy" in the modern sense</note> or Conversation stick more tenaciously in the learners' minds. In these, we seem to ourselves not merely to hear about things, but to see, speak, and act them out ourselves, especially when compared to things absorbed through bare recitation.

81. Finally, because the greater part of our life consists of social interaction <note>original: "conversatione"; the way we live, move, and speak among others</note>, youth are guided toward it in an elegant and efficient way if they become accustomed not only to understanding subjects but also to discussing them fluently.

82. *We desire, therefore, that the whole art of learned conversation and of discoursing about things...*

84. **THE ORATORY PALACE** <note>original: "PALATIVM ORATORIVM"; likely referring to a specific textbook or section of the curriculum</note> should repeat the same materials, using the same series of titles, but in the form of Declamations <note>formal oratorical speeches</note>. *...materials, in verse <note>original: "stylo ligato"; literally "bound style," referring to the constraints of poetic meter</note>: with brief instructions on composing poetry added again.*

86. It seems, moreover, the best thing to do is not to forge new poems, but to take pithy <note>original: "sententiosos"; full of meaning or maxims</note> verses from the ancient Poets and reduce them into one continuous text: in the same manner by which *Ross the Englishman* <note>Alexander Ross (1591–1654), author of the *Virgilius Evangelisans*, a "cento" poem that told the story of the Bible using only Virgil's lines</note> most elegantly wove together the entire history of Christ’s Gospel out of nothing but *Virgilian* verses.

87. The use of this **POETIC PALACE** will be: First, to make students familiar with the <term>quantity of syllables<note>In Latin poetry, "quantity" refers to the duration (long or short) of vowel sounds, which determines the rhythm or meter of the verse.</note></term>: so that for those studying Poetry, this work might suffice instead of *Smetius* <note>Henricus Smetius, author of a widely used dictionary of prosody and syllable quantities</note> and similar books on <term>prosody<note>the theory and practice of poetic meter</note></term>. (For we intend, and urge others to intend, that students of every class carry all their necessary materials with them, without the mental distraction that comes from a multitude and variety of books.) Second, it will become clearer to them how the poetic character differs from the oratorical: so they may more easily discern the styles of speaking in the Authors to whom they will now be sent. Finally, they will here prepare for themselves a notable supply of the most elegant maxims, which are of very frequent use throughout all Scholarship <note>original: "Eruditionem"</note>.

simple names of things (arranged in alphabetical order) are exchanged for more elegant ones: that is, it should be shown how the proper names of things may be expressed through <term>synonyms<note>words with similar meanings</note></term>, through <term>periphrasis<note>using many words where fewer would do; "circling" a topic</note></term>, through <term>tropes<note>metaphorical or figurative use of a word</note></term>, and in altogether varied and ornate ways. I am in a dilemma <note>original: "In ancipiti sum"; literally, "I am on a two-edged [sword]"</note>. It is not clear to me. I am in suspense of mind <note>original: "Animi pendeo"</note>. 91. And so this would be the **PALACE** of the third class of Latin study.

92. Finally, the **TREASURY** of the Latin language itself will follow, the substance <note>original Greek: "τὸ materiale"</note> of which will be the Latin Authors, explaining every variety of things and speech: Natural Scientists <note>original: "Physici"</note>, Geographers, Moral Philosophers <note>original: "Ethici"</note>, Physicians, **POLITICIANS**, Theologians, Historians, Orators, Poets, and so on. Both ancient and modern, those already born or those yet to be born—whoever brings forward (or will bring forward) anything remarkably useful, and who uses a polished and pure style of speaking.

93. For the logic of the method we have begun requires that all kinds of authors not only be admitted here, but even summoned.

94. Indeed, we have set wide boundaries for this study: the total fullness of both Things and Words—that is, of <term>Wisdom<note>original: "sapientia"</note></term> and <term>Eloquence<note>original: "Eloquentia"</note></term>—as much as can possibly exist. It is therefore necessary that the means of study correspond to this goal in their own capacity.

95. --- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the final stage of language education, the "Treasury," which follows the "Vestibule," the "Gate," and the "Palace." The author is explaining why a wide variety of classical authors must be studied to achieve true mastery of both language and knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>(134)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: 99.</meta>

---

...just as the ***Gate*** <note>original: *Janua*; the intermediate stage of the author's language system</note> is nothing but a supplement to the ***Vestibule*** <note>original: *Vestibulum*; the introductory stage</note>; and the ***Palace*** <note>original: *Palatium*; the stage focused on refined style</note> is a supplement to the ***GATE***: so the ***Authors*** <note>The "Treasury" stage where students read original classical texts</note> must necessarily provide supplements to all those that came before.

96. However, they will not provide these supplements if we only admit ***Historians*** along with ***Orators*** and ***Poets*** (as has almost been the custom in schools until now). This is because those writers do not pursue everything that pertains to the full breadth of ***Learning*** <note>original: *Eruditionis*</note>; instead, each of them delights in some certain, specific subject matter.

97. If our method intended only the single and naked elegance of style, even one suitable Author would suffice—provided he was made so familiar to us that we could express his diction to the life. For example, ***Terence*** <note>Publius Terentius Afer, a Roman playwright famous for his pure and refined Latin</note>: everyone knows him to be pure and concise in his words and phrases, and so pleasant that he was even a favorite of ***Cicero*** <note>The most famous Roman orator and statesman</note>. Yet, anyone who reads with judgment can see that he has very little substance <note>original: *materiæ*</note>. Hence, someone reports that ***Scaliger*** <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a renowned Renaissance scholar and critic</note> used to say that the "poverty of the Latin language made Terence so famous" <note>original: *inopia Latinae Linguae Terentium adeò celebrem fecit*</note>.

98. But because a complete method proposes a complete ***Eloquence***; and since this can in no way be obtained without a full knowledge of ***Things***; it is clear that those who aspire to full Eloquence must travel through the entire ***circle of things*** <note>original: *Rerum circulum*; the author refers to a comprehensive education or encyclopedia of knowledge</note>.

<summary>The author argues that language mastery requires reading a wide range of specialized authors across all fields of knowledge, rather than just poets and orators, because true eloquence depends on a complete understanding of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Janua, Vestibulum, Palatium, curriculum, Terence, Cicero, Scaliger, Eloquence, Encyclopedia, Latin instruction</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for a broad education in various subjects to achieve true eloquence. The author explains that stylistic devices like metaphors and proverbs are hollow without a deep understanding of the scientific, mathematical, or historical realities they are based on.</meta>

<page-num>(135)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

99. For from a whole and perfect thing, nothing can be excluded. To wish to speak eloquently about what you do not understand—how foolish is that!

100. Neither **metaphors**, **adages**, **maxims** <note>original: *gnomæ*; pithy sayings that impart a moral lesson</note>, **witty sayings** <note>original: *apophthegmata*</note>, **hieroglyphs** <note>symbolic images or emblems used in Renaissance rhetoric to convey hidden meanings</note>, nor various **allusions** (in each of which resides great power of **Eloquence**) can be truly understood, properly used, or sharply applied without a proper **prior knowledge** <note>original: *præcognitione*</note> of the subjects from which they are drawn.

101. These subjects are drawn from natural, artificial, mathematical, and logical things, and so on, and from events that have occurred regarding them. From where can you learn these better than from their own **Authors**? <note>Referring to the specialists or original classical writers on those specific topics</note>

102. If anyone thinks that a sufficient knowledge of things can be drawn from **commonplace books** <note>original: *locis communibus*; popular reference books of the era that compiled excerpts and quotes under topical headings for easy use by students</note>—such as ***Lang’s Polyanthea*** <note>A famous and massive encyclopedic reference work compiled by Josephus Langius, used widely in the 17th century</note> and similar works—truly, he has little considered the goal of these counsels! Here, we seek a **solid**, **mature** <note>original: *virilem*; literally "manly," signifying a serious, adult level of comprehension</note>, and internally coherent **knowledge of things**, as well as the power to interpret those things; not that childish, fragmented, torn, confused, and dull kind.

103. **Commonplace books**, and all similar supplementary books, have their own merit and use (especially if they are correctly structured): namely, to serve as general **Indexes** of the **Authors**, so that one may find which Author said what about a specific...

<note>Vocabulary: Eloquence, metaphors, adages, maxims, witty sayings, hieroglyphs, prior knowledge, Lang’s Polyanthea, commonplace books, Indexes</note>

<summary>The author argues that true eloquence requires a deep understanding of the subject matter, asserting that stylistic devices are useless without factual knowledge. He critiques the over-reliance on "commonplace books" or encyclopedic snippets, advocating for a coherent and mature study of original authors instead.</summary>
<keywords>Eloquence, rhetoric, metaphors, Josephus Langius, commonplace books, classical education, prior knowledge, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while reference books and "commonplace books" are useful for organization, they cannot replace the study of original sources. The author warns that relying solely on summaries leads to a superficial education.</meta>

<page-num>(136)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: ipsa</meta>

...or wrote on that matter; or as **Handbooks** <note>original: *Promptuaria*; literally "storehouses" or "ready-rooms" of information</note> of knowledge already acquired, which briefly suggest various things on various topics. This use is certainly distinguished.

104. But if they [these reference books] thrust themselves forward as a replacement for the **Authors** themselves—a deception that is as common as it is pleasing, for it presents the appearance of a shortcut to learning—then they do more harm than good. This is because they are a most immediate obstacle to a truer and fuller education, becoming what they call ***Shortcuts that lead to loss*** <note>original: *Compendia Dispendia*; a Latin wordplay contrasting a *compendium* (summary or shortcut) with a *dispendium* (expense or loss)</note>. Hence also the saying: *He is hardly wise who is not wise beyond his commonplace books.* <note>original: *parum sapit, qui ultra locos communes non sapit*</note>

105. Unless, perhaps, **Lang’s Polyanthea**, as it has been expanded by **Gruter** <note>Jan Gruter (1560–1627), a Flemish-German scholar who edited and significantly enlarged the *Polyanthea*, a massive encyclopedic reference work</note> into several volumes, might seem to deserve a different evaluation. Those who have put them to the test will be able to judge and decide whether those volumes really provide for every subject in the way their title pages promise.

106. Meanwhile, who would not think a theologian was out of his mind if, believing himself sufficiently provided for by **Biblical Concordances** <note>an alphabetical index of words found in the Bible</note> or some collections or other, he should neglect the sacred book of the Bible itself? Or what heir of earthly property is so foolish that, having been assigned certain small portions of income according to another’s judgment, he neglects to look at the total income along with the lands from which they come? Not goods...

<summary>The author warns against the over-reliance on encyclopedic summaries and "commonplace books," arguing that they should serve as aids rather than replacements for original texts. He uses the analogy of a theologian neglecting the Bible for a concordance to illustrate the danger of superficial learning.</summary>

<keywords>commonplace books, Lang's Polyanthea, Jan Gruter, shortcuts to learning, Biblical concordances, original sources, scholarship, educational theory</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 136, where the author warned that relying on summaries and reference books (commonplace books) instead of original sources leads to superficial knowledge. The text concludes a rhetorical question about whether a theologian can truly know the Bible only through concordances.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>

...claim its own things in their entirety?

107. It is certainly better to make use of **Wholes** than of fragments; to gather the harvest than to go gleaning; to pick from flowering gardens than to collect little flowers that have been plucked and scattered—which are mostly withered and drained of their life-blood.

108. It follows that, according to our method in **Classical Schools** or **Academies** <note>original: *Gymnasiis*; referring to secondary schools for the liberal arts</note>, everything that provides the foundations for **Universal Learning** <note>original: *universali Eruditioni*</note> must be completed:

> *nothing should be reserved for Universities or for study-travels except for each person's own specialized **Field** <note>original: *Facultatem*</note>, in which he has decided to spend his life and serve GOD and human society.*

Specifically, this is so that no one lacks the proper time or the necessary preparations to make great progress in their own field and to excel in it afterward.

109. Since, therefore, **Authors** provide all kinds of material for that **Universal Learning** <note>original: *Catholicæ Eruditioni*; here "catholic" is used in its original sense of "universal" or "comprehensive"</note>, they must be summoned and brought into order. Each author should contribute to the common store whatever useful or beautiful thing they were granted to discover or think. In this way, from all the individual parts brought together into one, a universal **Treasure of Wisdom** <note>original: *Sapientiæ Thesaurus*</note> may be gathered and stored within the hidden depths of the mind.

110. Someone will say that this is laborious, or simply impossible. Certainly, if the matter...

<summary>The author argues that students should study complete original works rather than summaries and that schools must provide a total foundational education so that university years can be focused entirely on a chosen profession.</summary>

<keywords>universal education, academic specialization, original sources, educational reform, school vs university, Treasure of Wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the proper way to approach classical and academic authors. The previous page (137) argued that students should focus on original "Wholes" rather than just fragments, and that general education should be completed before specializing at the University level.</meta>

<page-num>(138)</page-num>

...lest we approach so great a task with mere impulse, rather than with careful planning and skill.

111. However, first of all, we do not mean that we should understand every single **Author** <note>original: *Autores*</note> currently in existence. Instead, we should select certain ones from every field—specifically the best. From any or all of them, we should take only the best, or that which might be missing even in the works of the best; for indeed, not even the greatest author has always explained everything in the best possible way.

112. Secondly, we do not mean that all those selected works must be read in their entirety. Some should be studied only through indexes, or rather through **summaries** <note>original: *compendia*; handbooks or condensed versions of larger works</note> which we hope will be constructed for this purpose. The goal is that *the youth preparing for **University** <note>original: *Academiae*</note> should not be ignorant of the names, books, number, order, and contents of all the best authors who have written on individual subjects. Rather, they should already know the mindset and the method of treatment used by all those authors regarding all the principal matters.*

113. *Once they have reached University, future **Philosophers** will be able to study the Philosophers; **Physicians**, the Physicians; **Politicians**, the Politicians; and **Theologians**, the Theologians. At last, they may fully approach and "eviscerate" <note>original: *eviscerare*; a vivid term meaning to thoroughly dissect or extract the very core of a text</note> these works, in order to acquire for themselves a specific and solid knowledge and **practice** <note>original: *praxin*</note> of everything belonging to their own profession.*

114. Thirdly, we do not believe that even those authors who are presented in full deserve equal care; rather, among the **Masters of style** <note>original: *styli Magistros*</note>, we must select... 
<meta>The word "deli-" at the end of the page and the catchword "gendos" combine to form "deligendos," meaning "to be selected" or "chosen."</meta>

<summary>The author explains a tiered system of education: general students learn from the best parts of selected authors via summaries, while specialized University students perform deep, exhaustive "dissections" of the primary texts in their specific fields.</summary>
<keywords>curriculum, University preparation, summaries, specialized study, academic method, textbooks, professional training</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 138 regarding the selection of classical authors. The previous page ended with students needing to acquire a "specific and solid" foundation; this page begins by finishing the thought that they should gather this foundation from only the very best authors.</meta>

<page-num>(139)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: things [original: rerum]</meta>

...to be gathered from the best, and only the best. For example: for the first and most perfect Author of the **Asiatic style** <term>Asiatic style: A rhetorical style known for its ornamentation, richness, and rhythmic flow, typified by Cicero.</term>, let **Cicero** be chosen. Let the students' minds become so accustomed to him that they develop the habit of imitating and expressing his composition in all things (even if they do not adopt his specific phrasing in every detail, for phrasing must be sought from many sources). On the other hand, for the leading Author of the **Laconic style** <term>Laconic style: A concise, brief, and pithy style of writing and speaking, named after the inhabitants of Laconia (Sparta).</term>, let **Seneca** be held as the model (or, in the opinion of some, **Lipsius** <note>Justus Lipsius (1547–1606) was a famous Flemish scholar who advocated for a return to the concise prose of the Roman Empire.</note>). Students must be taught to imitate him perfectly <note>original: *ad unguem*; literally "to the fingernail," a metaphor for precision</note> through frequent reading and rereading, and by translating his work back and forth between languages. Likewise in Poetry: let **Virgil** be the primary model for the heroic style, and **Horace** for the Lyric style, and so on.

115. By this or a similar method, it will be neither impossible nor difficult for all the best classical Authors to be thoroughly absorbed <note>original: *exhauriri*; literally "drained" or "exhausted"</note> by our classical students within a short span of years, and converted into their own "juice and blood" <note>A common Renaissance metaphor meaning to internalize knowledge so deeply that it becomes a natural part of oneself.</note>.

116. And this will be the *material* part of the treasures of **Scholarly Learning** to be collected: **GOOD AUTHORS**.

117. As for the *formal* part <note>original: *τὸ formale*; using the Greek article 'the' with the Latin word for 'form'</note>, which brings everything here into order, we desire that a **KEY TO THE HUMAN UNDERSTANDING** be constructed; *containing certain general rules, which bring all things that ever are and happen, are said and thought (or even can be and happen, be said and thought), into a certain general proportion (by which Things, [Words, and Thoughts] harmoniously correspond to one another...)*

<summary>The author argues for a curriculum based on imitating the very best classical models—such as Cicero for flow and Seneca for brevity—to internalize their styles. He proposes creating a "Key to Human Understanding" to provide a systematic framework for all knowledge and expression.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, Seneca, Virgil, Horace, Asiatic style, Laconic style, imitation, pedagogy, Scholastic Erudition, Key to Human Understanding</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how students should synthesize their reading. The previous page (139) ended by suggesting that students' extensive reading of classical authors should be "converted into..."</meta>

<page-num>(140)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: from [original: à]</meta>

...concepts of things and speech), leading them back so that in this way, from all the particulars heard, seen, and discussed thus far—and later to be read and observed in the Authors—a certain universal light of Wisdom may arise in their minds.

118. *In place of a table of contents, there is here a* **UNIVERSAL LEXICON** <note>original: *Lexicon Catholicon*; in this context, "catholic" means universal or all-encompassing</note>, *reviewing all the most memorable points from all the Authors: but in an elegant* order of **Words and Things**, as they follow one another.

119. For example, under the word **OLYMPUS**, let whatever descends from it in terms of Words and Things be briefly recorded: perhaps in this manner. (1.) **OLYMPUS** *is a very high mountain in Thessaly, climbing above the clouds with its peak.* (Named as if <term>holos lampros</term> <note>original Greek: ὅλος λαμπρὸς</note>, namely: "entirely shining": either from the Sun, or from the snows.) (2.) From this, the Poets use the word for heaven; and they call *Olympian Jove* <note>The Roman name for the Greek god Zeus</note>, the Governor of heaven and the supreme GOD. (3.) From this, the *City of Olympia* received its name, in which the Temple of that Jove was located. And here, in honor of Jove, they had established equestrian games or contests of Heroes. (4.) These were called the *Olympics*, to be repeated at the end of every fourth year, or at the start of the fifth. Hence, therefore: (5.) An *Olympiad* was the name given to that four-year space of time, adopted as an epoch <note>A starting point in time used to fix the dates of historical events</note> for counting the successions of Kings and the history of the Greeks; beginning, of course, from...

<summary>This page describes the "Universal Lexicon," a pedagogical tool designed to help students synthesize their reading into a "universal light of wisdom." It uses the entry for Olympus to demonstrate how a single term can link geography, mythology, and the chronological system of the ancient Greeks.</summary>

...from the time of the first founder of the *Olympiads*, *Iphitus*, in the year of the World 3174. This era is most commonly used among Greek and Latin writers. (6.) There was also a mother of Alexander the Great named Olympias, etc.

120. In this way, regarding everything knowable, the most important points can be recorded very briefly, so that there may be a truly universal <note>original: *catholicum*; in the 17th century, this referred to a "universal" or all-encompassing system of knowledge</note> Repository of all Things and Words.

121. If things are arranged in this way, who does not see that the whole of school-age youth can be advanced toward true literacy (namely, the knowledge of various things and the purest Latinity) in great numbers and by the easiest methods? Provided that the aforementioned books—the best instruments for successfully spreading this *scholastic learning*—are prepared with sufficient accuracy.

122. As far as the **VESTIBULE** and the **GATE** <note>original: *VESTIBVLVM* and *JANVA*; these refer to Jan Amos Comenius’s famous graded Latin textbooks. The *Vestibulum* was the introductory primer, and the *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Languages Unlocked) was the intermediate text</note> (the foundations of this study) are concerned, we have labored to the best of our meager ability to lay them solidly. 123. Perhaps also the **KEY OF THE human UNDERSTANDING** <note>original: *CLAVIS INTELLECTVS*; likely referring to a planned advanced volume in his educational series</note>, which for directing the universal reading of authors [we have intended] to fashion...

125. And because several most learned men from various Kingdoms, in their private letters sent to you for this reason, have urged you to beautifully complete the work so beautifully begun: and they also advise me to apply the torch and to urge you on, not...

<meta>This page continues the address to David Vechner begun on page 142. The author is urging Vechner to complete his educational work, moving from the metaphor of a "Temple" to a "Palace" for his Latin textbook.</meta>

<page-num>(143)</page-num>

I will not stop; you will bear it patiently that I remind you of your pledge before the Republic of Letters <note>original: *Reipublicæ literariæ*; the international community of scholars and intellectuals</note>, so that it might not be a burden to compose with focused effort <note>original: *admorsis unguibus*; literally "with bitten nails," a classical idiom for intense concentration or hard work</note> the work that can serve the benefit of so many.

126. 127. 128. Why should we not comply with the friend's counsel, which is not lacking in reason? *Metaphors are more safely drawn from secular matters,* he says: *sacred things should be spared as much as possible.*

129. 130. Consider also this: *Rome, when it was in its bloom, took pride in its Palaces*, to the amazement of those coming from elsewhere who marveled at the unusual splendor of the City. Modern *Rome*, revived from the ruins of antiquity, emulates that glory—even if from a distance—and magnificently shows itself off with its Palaces.

131. *If you, therefore, construct for us a splendid Palace of Roman eloquence from the remains of Roman books, believe that you will perform a greater work than those who restore those massive stone structures.* <note>original: *facundiæ Romanæ Palatium*; a "Palace of Eloquence" suggests a comprehensive guide to mastering the Latin language.</note>

132. 134. May GOD likewise rouse someone who, by unlocking the more hidden Treasures of studies, may perform useful work for the Schools! 140. *In this way at last, something remarkable can be achieved, if—tasks being divided—we do not all do the same thing (one doing it one way, another differently, yet repeating the same task and confusing one another), but rather we each handle different matters while all directing our minds and expending our effort toward the same common goal.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the educational path for students, moving from the foundational "Gate" to the higher levels of study. It outlines the structure of the Comenian school system, transitioning from basic language acquisition to deep knowledge and eloquence, while reflecting on the dire state of education in war-torn Germany.</meta>

(147)

141. It must be kept in mind, however, that one does not pass immediately from the <term>Gate</term> <note>original: *Janua*; referring to the textbook *Janua linguarum reserata*</note> to the <term>Authors</term> <note>original: *Autores*; referring to the study of classical literature</note>, but rather—by quite deliberate planning—the <term>Halls</term> <note>original: *Atria*</note> or <term>Palaces</term> <note>original: *Palatia*</note> are placed in between. Only from these will there be a final passage into those *treasury-storehouses* <note>original: *thesaurarios illos carceres*</note> (as Coelius, following Herodes the Sophist, calls them).

142. Remembering this fact will serve to let you know that the minute care regarding the basics of style has already been passed by you. Whatever remains to be done now concerns a more solid knowledge of Things themselves and a more mature Eloquence; thus, the very summit of this Scholastic Erudition is now committed to you.

143. **One must not forget**, truly, the *Universal Directory* <note>original: *Repertorii Catholici*</note>, 그that most beautiful and precious Treasury of Universal Erudition.

144. May God, the thrice-greatest and best, grant that whether you take up this <term>task to be adorned</term> <note>original: *ornandam Spartam*; a classical proverb meaning "You have been assigned this lot, now do it credit."</note> or another does, it may be done to the praise of the divine name and for the public benefit of the Schools. Through this, may there be a certain hope of restoring to a better state those human affairs which have slipped into decay (and in our Germany, have now plainly collapsed)!
> -> *For a multitude of the wise is the salvation of the world* (as the Wise Man says in the Book of Wisdom, chapter 6, verse 26) <-

145. When these Books have been prepared, certain time-limits must be designated within which each Class must complete its assigned period. Furthermore, there must be written for those teaching—

## General warnings for teachers.

151. *A teacher should not teach as much as he himself is able to teach, but as much as the learner is able to grasp.* For I cannot pour into a vessel as much liquid as I wish, but only as much as the *Vessel* can hold; nor should it be poured in all at once according to the internal capacity, but rather trickled in as the narrowness of the opening permits. And when I desire to lift something lying on the ground with my hand, I must necessarily stoop down, for that object cannot raise itself to my hand. Clearly, therefore, the Teacher <note>original: *Praeceptor*</note> should descend to the level of the student in all things; and he should conduct himself actively, and the student passively. A tablet can paint nothing on itself, but the *Painter* can; it performs its part well enough if it remains still and receives the pigments <note>original: *pigmenta*</note> skill-

<meta>catchword: fully.</meta>

(150)

152. *Whatever difficulties and troubles school studies <note>original: Scholastica studia</note> bring, the Teachers <note>original: Praeceptores</note> should bear them on their own shoulders; leaving nothing to the Pupils <note>original: Discipulis</note> except the eagerness and ease of imitation.* Indeed, they should act in the same way that Nurses are accustomed to: they provide their infants with nothing but food that has been chewed beforehand, so that only the gentle task of digestion remains for the children.

153. *Whatever (therefore) they want the students to know, they should show it to them with the greatest possible clarity <note>original: perspicuitate</note>, and demonstrate it to the eye as if it were painted in vivid colors; so that the student cannot fail to see both the thing itself and the manner in which it exists.* In this way, they cannot help but rejoice in the new light, and always extend their desire <note>original: appetitum; referring to a natural intellectual hunger</note> toward further things.

154. *Whatever (however) they want to be done by the students, they themselves should show the way by doing it.* For it is most natural that everyone says to their leader (even silently), "Go before me, I will follow" <note>original: I prae, sequar; a classical Latin expression for following a guide</note>: because it is easy to follow one who goes ahead, but being pushed forward without a pre-marked path is a hard thing.

155. Therefore, let nothing ever seem so easy that the *Teacher does not labor to make it even more clear and easier to imitate in various ways*. For a matter is not as easy for the learner <note>original: discenti</note> as it is for the one who already knows <note>original: scienti</note>. And even if it were, the learner does not yet trust himself enough...

(151)

...ate. <note>completing "imitate" from the previous page</note> In this way, the mind is strengthened as to whether it conceives or forms correctly what it has been ordered to do; but when it sees its leader going ahead by repeating the task so many times, it follows with spirit. And this will truly be to lead a child <note>original Greek: παιδαγωγϵῖν (paidagōgein)</note>: to guide the little ones by the hand as gently as possible.

156. *Students should never be weighed down by the sheer mass of things to be learned*: otherwise, they will gain nothing except nausea and a rawness of the intellect <note>original: ingenii cruditatem; the author uses a medical metaphor comparing learning to digestion</note>. For just as those who devour many things are not healthier than those who eat only as much as they can digest; so too, those who read, hear, and commit many things to memory are not necessarily learned. Instead, the truly learned are those who rightly drink in, concoct, and digest what they read, hear, and memorize through their understanding, and then promptly produce it for every use.

157. *Three things must always be formed in the student: the Mind, the Hand, and the Tongue.* The Mind, so that they understand the matter they are learning. The Hand, so that they learn to express in writing what they understand. The Tongue, so that they may promptly declare what they understand and know.

158. *These things proceed step by step: so that understanding something is the easiest; imitating it with the hand is more difficult; and expressing it with the tongue is the most difficult, yet closest to perfection.* For what is done by the hand allows for delay, and permits deliberation and assistance, if...

159. Thus, regarding the Latin language (in any of those stages indicated in § 47), it is possible to understand before one can either write or speak. And when the practice of what has been understood is to be attempted, it should first be attempted with the pen <note>original: calamo (a reed pen)</note> rather than with the tongue. For while writing, there is no lack of some amount of time for deliberation and the power to consult: whether by prodding <note>original: vellicando (literally "plucking" or "twitching")</note> one's own memory, or by asking someone else sitting nearby, or finally, by searching through books. And to those who have practiced in this way for a while, there finally comes that extemporaneous faculty of expressing the thoughts of the mind, which is done by the Tongue.

160. *The same must be observed in learning the Arts and sciences. For everywhere this same progression holds: namely, that before all else, the Understanding <term>Intellectus</term> of things is formed in us through the senses; and through the Understanding, Memory. Then, with Memory directing the operation, the Hand <term>Manus</term> attempts and completes its work. But to engage in discourse about things belongs only to one for whom the Understanding and Memory, already strengthened regarding the use of things, suggest the matters and words as promptly as the Tongue <term>Lingua</term> is able to speak them. Therefore, the highest level and the peak of progress is altogether to [understand] things well...* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page; the catchword indicates the next word is "understood" (intellectas).</note>

161. *Therefore, let **Teachers** <term>Præceptores: instructors or masters</term> take heed that whatever they wish to teach their students successfully, they proceed through those steps without confusion: otherwise, they will produce confusion for both themselves and their students.*

162. Everywhere, however, let *Examples* precede as the *matter*; and let *Precepts and Rules* follow as the *form*: just as was said above (§ 60). For Precepts and Rules are certain guides for the mind regarding things and speech: but how can that which does not yet exist be ruled and guided? And who is not forced to admit that every Rule receives its light from Examples? Why, therefore, is the example not put first?

163. *Whatever task is begun, let it not be set aside unless it is finished.* For if one returns to a business at intervals, the labor is not diminished, but is increased by the repetition: because the same things, left in the same state, will repeat the same difficulties. Make a trial <note>original: Fac periculum (literally "make a danger/trial")</note> of how easily you might fill a vessel with water if you only put in one or two drops daily; whereas you could easily fill it if you poured continuously.

...except by repeating. This repetition, however, will take place later while doing other things, and with a new utility: namely, when subsequent things are built upon these already established foundations. For this true method (which we intend) demands that we proceed in order, and with a step that is never interrupted: so that preceding things always prepare a way for the following, and following things always add strength to the preceding.

165. *The first foundations of things must be laid down with absolute accuracy, unless you wish for the whole thing you have built upon them to wobble.* Physicians <term>Medici: practitioners of medicine following classical traditions</term> know that an error in the first *digestion* <term>concoctionis: "concoction," the physiological process of transforming food into nutrients</term> is not corrected in the second, nor the second in the third. And Mathematicians know that an error admitted at the beginning of a calculation increases infinitely as one progresses: nor can it be removed, unless the whole operation is destroyed and one returns to the principles.

166. Therefore, whatever a Teacher <term>Præceptor: instructor or master</term> begins to teach, he shall take care that it be accurate, and thus so firm that subsequent things can be built upon it safely. *One must first learn Correctly, then Quickly. He hastens enough who moves toward the goal by a straight path.* It is better for learners to be slow at first, provided they are accurate: Speed will come later, once the foundations are firm. This is certainly the nature of all growing things: that they perform their first movements and first increases imperceptibly; at length, in their progress, they take on force and momentum, if they are well root-

167. *If anything has been wrongly grasped or adopted, care must be taken that it does not take root, but is soon pulled up.* For the Mind <note>original: *Mens*</note> is accustomed to entrust whatever it seizes to the Memory <note>original: *Memoria*</note>, and it becomes an idol <note>In this context, an "idol" refers to a false mental image or a stubborn misconception that distorts the truth, a term popularized by early modern philosophers like Francis Bacon.</note>, which only with difficulty allows itself to be uprooted.

168. *Therefore, whatever is taught should be taught accurately, so that it cannot be dragged into error.* Then the Teacher <term>Magister: the master or primary instructor</term> should immediately examine how it was perceived, lest any error perhaps has crept in. And if he notices anything, let him correct it at once: having both detected and destroyed the occasion for falling into that error (which a wise shaper of minds <note>original: *ingeniorum plasmator*</note> can easily sense from the circumstances): namely, by taking precautions so that a similar mistake is not made later on.

169. *The same diligence must be applied in forming and correcting writing exercises <term>styli exercitiis: specifically refers to exercises in prose composition or penmanship</term>, so that nothing used incorrectly by the student <term>discipulus</term> becomes an example or a rule for him.*

171. *Indeed, by far the greatest shortcut to ease* (for the entire company of teachers and learners) *is if the Preceptor* <term>Praeceptor: a teacher or instructor who guides others</term> *teaches no single person alone, whatever he teaches, but all together! Or if he teaches one, when the occasion arises, let it be in the sight of all: so that whatever is said to one may benefit everyone.*

172. For just as a Printer <term>Typographus: a master of the printing press</term> fills a thousand sheets with writing from one and the same arrangement of type, and all of them most accurately: so a Preceptor can illuminate the minds of a thousand students <term>discipuli: students or learners</term> with one and the same explanation of a matter, and can refine the styles of all through one and the same exercise.

173. *For this to happen, two things are necessary. First, that students of unequal progress or from different times are not admitted into the same class: but that all of similar progress* <term>profectûs: advancement or proficiency level</term> *be received together and led together: in the same way that seeds in the fields, which are prepared for the same harvest* <term>messi: the crop or harvest time</term> <note>original: *semina, quæ eidem messi parantur*</note>, *are scattered at the same time, watered at the same time, aired at the same time, sprout at the same time, grow at the same time, and ripen at the same time.*

175. This kind of attention can be excited and retained in eight ways.

176. *First*, if the Preceptor <term>Praeceptor: a teacher or instructor</term> makes an effort always to bring something that both delights and benefits; for in this way their minds will be enticed <note>original: *inescabuntur*. This word literally means "to be caught with bait," suggesting that the teacher should lure the students' interest like a fisherman.</note>, so that they always come eagerly and with prepared attention.

177. *Second*, if at the start of any task, their minds are either soothed by a recommendation of the subject matter to be presented; or stimulated <note>original: *vellicentur*. This suggests a sharp "pinching" or "plucking" of the mind to wake it up.</note> by raising questions—either about what has already been proposed, so that by a logical connection they arrive at the current material; or about what is yet to be proposed, so that having noticed their ignorance on that point, they are fired up to receive the explanation of the matter more greedily.

178. *Third*, if the Master <term>Magister: the schoolmaster</term>, standing in a higher place <note>original: *editiore consistens loco*. The author likely refers to a "cathedra" or teacher's raised platform, common in early modern classrooms to ensure visibility and authority.</note>, casts his eyes around and permits no one to do anything else but fix their eyes on him in return.

179. *Fourth*, if he assists attention by presenting any matter to as many Senses as he can: namely, not to Hearing <term>Auditui: the sense of hearing</term> alone, through spoken words, but also to Sight <term>Visui: the sense of sight</term>, through pictures; and to Touch <term>Tactui: the sense of touch</term>, by placing the object itself before them to be handled; and to Smell <term>Olfactui: the sense of smell</term>, etc., whenever such objects are to be explained. For such an application of Things to the Senses, and of the senses to Things, continually stimulates the imagination <term>imaginativam: the faculty of the mind that forms images</term>,

<summary>This page outlines the first four of eight methods for a teacher to capture and hold the attention of a classroom, emphasizing engagement through delight, curiosity, eye contact, and multisensory learning.</summary>

180. *Fifth*, the Preceptor <term>Praeceptor: a teacher or instructor</term> will sharpen the students' attention if, during the lesson, he occasionally interrupts his speech and says: "*You, or You, what did I just say? Repeat this sentence. You, tell me how we came to this point?*" and similar things, according to the progress of each Class <term>Classis: the grade level or specific group of students</term>. If anyone is caught not paying attention, let them be rebuked or punished on the spot: in this way, the desire for attention will be thoroughly sharpened.

181. *Likewise*, if you have questioned a particular student and they hesitate, skip to the second, third, tenth, or even the thirtieth student and demand the answer without repeating the question: all this is for the purpose that everyone strives to pay attention to whatever is said to one person, and to put it to use.

182. *Seventh*, it can also happen that if one or two do not know something, it may be asked of the whole crowd: and then, whoever responds first or best should be praised in everyone's sight, so that the example may serve to spark competition <note>original: *aemulationi*. In early modern education, "emulation" was a vital tool where students were encouraged to compete for honor and academic standing.</note>. If someone makes a mistake, let them be corrected, with the cause of the error being detected and rooted out (as was said before). It is incredible how much this will be a shortcut <note>original: *compendium*. This term refers to an efficient path or a "short way" to achieve a goal.</note> to the swiftest progress.

183. *Finally*, when the lesson is finished, an opportunity should be given...

184. *Such daily exercise of attention will benefit young people not only for the present, but throughout their entire lives. For having become accustomed through several years of continuous use to always focus on the task at hand <note>original: "id semper agere quod agitur," a common Latin maxim meaning "always do what you are doing" or "focus on the present task."</note>, they will always perform all things with a present mind, without waiting for the warnings or proddings of others. And if Schools are like this, why should there not be a most abundant harvest of the most skillful men?*

# Instruction on the Use
# of the VESTIBULE of the Latin Language.

186. The goal of the first Latin class is that the boy [be skilled] in reading and writing in his mother tongue <note>original: *vernaculè*. Comenius famously argued that children should master their native language before tackling Latin.</note>
<meta>catchword: bendi</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a series of rules for maintaining student focus, advocating for public Q&A sessions to benefit the whole class. It then transitions into the specific curriculum for the "Vestibulum," the introductory level of Latin studies.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, student attention, public questioning, Vestibulum, Janua, Latin instruction, mother tongue</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the curriculum for the "Vestibulum" (the introductory stage of Latin learning). The catchword from the previous page was "monitis" (warnings/reminders), leading into the conclusion of the general principles and the start of specific instructions for the first level of language study.</meta>

...skilled in [writing] (for we presuppose this), let him complete his first and therefore most difficult struggle (because every beginning is heavy <note>original: *omne principium grave*; a common Latin proverb suggesting that the start of any endeavor is the most challenging part</note>) with Latin letters: that is, let him learn to:
(1.) **Pronounce** the Latin language correctly.
(2.) **Read** fluently.
(3.) **Write** accurately.
(4.) **Understand and interpret** in some way or another.
(5.) **Distinguish** the parts of speech moderately well.
(6.) **Decline and conjugate** regular forms <note>original: *analoga*, referring to words that follow standard grammatical rules rather than exceptions</note> exactly.
(7.) **Express** childish thoughts of the mind, etc.
(8.) **Imitate** models already provided in his own style, in some manner.

187. To achieve this, several hundred short sentences have already been conceived, called the **VESTIBULE of the Latin Language** <note>original: *Latinitatis VESTIBULUM*; this was the first of Comenius's graded Latin textbooks, intended for beginners</note>: to be perfected hereafter in such a way that:
(1.) **Accents** are expressly placed above the words.
(2.) **Observations** regarding correct pronunciation are added.
(3.) **Other copies** of these same sentences are printed with the same type but in some diluted color (perhaps green would suit best of all): from which boys may learn the foundations of Latin writing, tracing over the strokes <note>original: *ductus*, the movement or direction of the pen</note> of the letters expressed there with ink, and by that method training the hand for forming similar letters.
(4.) A **vernacular version** <note>a translation into the student’s native language</note> should be placed before each sentence.
(5.) **Nouns** should be expressed in one typeface <note>original: *charactere*</note>, **verbs** in another, and other small words in yet another.
(6.) **Tables** of Declensions and Conjugations, with

### Note Well

188. This structure of the <term>Vestibule|the entry-level textbook</term> will conveniently serve to achieve the goal: if the booklet is gone through ten times within the assigned six-month period <note>original: semestre</note>: on each occasion, the beginners' understanding, memory, hand, and tongue should always be advanced to a higher level.
189. It is proper at this point to advise that the morning hours should be devoted to exercising the understanding and memory (as elsewhere, so in this <term>introductory training|tyrocinium</term>); the afternoon hours should be devoted to the pen and the tongue <note>meaning writing and speaking practice</note>.

### I.

190. *First, therefore, they should go through the booklet for the sake of reading and correct pronunciation alone: in this manner.* Let the <term>Teacher|Præceptor</term> read one or two pages, clearly, distinctly, and slowly, so that the students, looking at their own booklets, may be able to follow along with their ears, eyes, and attention. When the Teacher has finished the reading, let the students begin the same: but each one reading individual short sentences in a circle.
191. While this is happening, the Teacher will pay diligent attention to pronunciation, so that...

This care for elegantly "painting" the letters will continue throughout this entire semester (and even afterward), so that everyone might acquire for themselves a hand as beautiful as possible.

### II.

196. Once the little book has been finished in this way through mere *reading and writing*, they shall return to its beginning so that it may be read and written out again, even more diligently than before. This time, however, an <term>interpretation|interpretatione</term> is added: a more accurate explanation of the Words, and even an initial explanation of the <term>Things|Rerum</term>. <note>Comenius emphasized "Realism" in education—the idea that students should learn about the objects and concepts themselves (Things) alongside the vocabulary used to describe them (Words).</note>

197. And to address the latter point first (the explanation of Things), the Teacher—by leading the students in *reading and interpretation*—may pause here and there to interpose something pleasant and useful to hear. This should be done in the <term>vernacular tongue|linguâ vernaculâ</term>, since the students do not yet understand Latin. For example, at the first sentence, *Come, children* <note>original Latin: "Venite pueri"</note>, he will advise them that languages are best learned in childhood. For a child can learn any language within the span of a year, which a grown man can hardly do in a whole lifetime. Therefore, let them rejoice to come to School, where Latin is learned—that language which is beautiful and elegant above all others, etc. At the phrase *God will help you* <note>original Latin: "Deus vos juvabit"</note>, he will say: "My dearest little sons, we can [do] nothing..."

...that we can do nothing without God’s help. God, however, promptly aids those who fear and call upon Him, and so on. 198. In the meantime, let the students continue to read and interpret the sentences, again in order, but with the <term>vernacular text|vernaculo textu</term> <note>The "vernacular" refers to the students' native language, such as German or Czech.</note> coming first—for we recommend that the little book be printed in this way, so that the vernacular precedes the Latin—for two reasons. First, because they are not learning the vernacular by way of Latin, but Latin by way of the vernacular: therefore, let the guide go first. For this is an eternal and unchangeable law of method: that for the <term>Intellect|Intellectui</term>, when it is about to penetrate the darkness of an unknown thing, it is necessary to hold out the light of something already known; (*For whatever is learned is learned from things previously known.*) <note>original Latin: "Quidquid enim discitur, è præcognitis discitur." This was a fundamental principle for Comenius: teaching must always build upon what the student already understands.</note>

199. Second, this method benefits the <term>Memory|Memoriæ</term>, whose nature is to move forward, not backward. For if I ask a boy, *In the <term>Lord’s Prayer|Oratione Dominica</term>*, what follows after the word **LET [IT] BE DONE**? <note>original Latin: "FIAT", from the phrase "Fiat voluntas tua" (Thy will be done).</note> he will answer immediately (as Memory promptly suggests) *Thy will be done*, etc. But ask, *What precedes that word*, **LET [IT] BE DONE**? even any of us will hesitate: because, naturally, Memory does not know how to go backward, or at least is not accustomed to it.

(165)

...diately; just as <term>Memory|Memoria</term> stretches itself from the known to the unknown (from native words to foreign ones), it is of absolute necessity that the known be placed first, so that the unknown follows naturally. **Come here, you boys** <note>original German: "Komt her ihr Knaben"</note>, *Come, boys* <note>original Latin: "Venite pueri"</note>. **Learn the Latin language** <note>original German: "Lernet die Lateinische sprach"</note>, *learn the Latin language* <note>original Latin: "discite Latinam linguam"</note>, etc.

201. But this should also be observed for the sake of better progress: that as they learn to render sentence for sentence, they should also be commanded to render word for word. In this way: **Come here, you boys**: *Come, boys*: **Come here**, *Come*: **You boys**, *Boys*. And so in every instance.

202. The reason for this advice is: (1.) So that the tongue may linger longer on the same sound (for correct <term>pronunciation|pronunciatio</term> and fluent reading must still be strengthened), and so that the <term>Imaginative faculty|Imaginativa facultas</term> <note>The "imaginative faculty" refers to the student's ability to form a mental picture of the object or action being described.</note> may linger on the same thing that the word designates, so that a better impression may be made. (2.) So that the same impression and concept may be more distinct, and free from the danger of confusion. For because sometimes a Latin word is translated by two German words, or there occurs a transposition of words in German, the boy might not sufficiently consider where each word belongs if he were allowed only to pronounce whole sentences in a general way.

They will strengthen their **penmanship** <note>original Latin: "Calligraphiam"</note> and (2.) their understanding of the words. (3.) Furthermore, they will begin to discern the <term>Construction|Constructio</term> <note>The arrangement and grammatical relationship of words in a sentence.</note> of the words while focused on another task.

### III.

205. Thirdly, let the *Vestibulum* <note>The "Vestibulum" (Porch) was Comenius's introductory textbook, designed to follow the "Janua" or gate to the language.</note> be run through again by repeating the translation from **Latin** into the **native tongue** <note>original Latin: "vernaculum"</note>, in the reverse order from before, but with a similar number of sentences for each turn: likewise rendering first sentence for sentence, then word for word.

206. *The reason why we now advise a return from Latin to the native tongue is so that, by this method, the power of the Latin words and their <term>analogy|analogia</term> <note>The structural or logical similarity between two languages.</note> to the native ones may be more clearly revealed. For just as a traveler learns a path better by going and returning, and the structure of some work becomes better known by weaving and unweaving, so foreign*

208. In the afternoon, let them write those same sentences into their **Exercise book** <note>original Latin: "libellum Exercitiorum"</note> in the same order and manner as they were explained in the morning. In this way, the whole little book can be run through within eight or ten days.

214. Let them be taught that there are six terminations—variable according to the difference in number <note>Meaning singular and plural forms.</note>—into which any noun can be turned. For a word ends one way when we simply name a thing; another way, usually, when we call out to it; another when we attribute something to it, and so on. Therefore, in place of the technical names of the <term>Cases</term> <note>The different forms a noun takes to show its role in a sentence, such as subject or object.</note> (for this first time), let them use either questions: *Who? O you! Whose? To whom? Whom? With whom?* <note>original German: "Wer? O du! Weſſen? Wem? Wen? Mit wem?"</note> or those general formulas which naturally suggest to the mind what ought to follow. Namely: (1) Behold (2) O You (3) Part of (4) Give to (5) See (6) Take away from.

216. Once the use of the <term>Declension Table</term> <note>A chart showing the different endings of nouns.</note> has been shown through examples taken from the *Introduction* (how individual nouns like *Boy*, *Tongue*, *Thing*, *Wisdom*, *God*, *Teacher*, etc. <note>original Latin: *Puer*, *Lingua*, *Res*, *Sapientia*, *Deus*, *Præceptor*</note> are declined): both can then be declined together in pairs according to the following chapter (where *Adjectives are joined to Substantives*). However, first (for the sake of ease) use only those which belong to the same <term>Declension</term> <note>A group of nouns that follow the same pattern of endings.</note>. As in the first declension: *Latin Tongue*, *Beautiful tongue*. *Lowest earth*. *Near cloud*. *Broad space*, etc. <note>original Latin: *Latina Lingua*, *Lingua pulchra*, *Terra infima*, *Nebula propinqua*, *Area lata*</note>. Then in the second: *Eternal God*. *Temporary world*. *Highest heaven* <note>original Latin: *Deus æternus*, *Mundus temporarius*, *Cœlum ſupremum*</note>. After that the third: *Mortal man*. *Heavy weight*, etc. <note>original Latin: *Homo mortalis*, *Pondus grave*</note>.

217. Finally, when they have made progress in those "pure" forms <note>Examples where both the noun and adjective belong to the same declension group.</note>, let them come to mixed declensions: *Elegant tongue*. *Varying thing*. *Easy means*. *Pleasant end*. *Immortal angel*, etc. <note>original Latin: *Lingua elegans*, *Res varia*, *Medium facile*, *Finis jucundus*, *Angelus immortalis*</note>. Always vary these using the native language at the same time, so that they thoroughly learn the purpose and use of declining.

218. As for the <term>Pronoun</term> *this* <note>original Latin: *hic*, *hæc*, *hoc*</note>, others before us have proved that placing it before Nouns while declining is of no use and is a certain torment to tender minds; therefore, we pass over it in silence.

219. *Writing exercises will be held here (during all afternoon hours), consisting of the most accurate transcription of those same* <term>Declensions</term> <note>The patterns of endings for nouns, adjectives, and pronouns.</note>, *with* Masters *always present, observing, directing, and correcting.* *For the presence of the Master on duty* <note>original: *Turni*</note> *demands it.*

### VI.

220. *In the same way, the* text of the Vestibulum <note>The *Vestibulum* (or "Entrance") was an introductory Latin textbook used to teach basic vocabulary and grammar.</note> *will be gone through for a sixth time, for the sake of learning the* <term>Conjugations</term> <note>The patterns of endings for verbs, showing person, tense, and mood.</note>: *with the lesson and explanation again being presented from the fourth chapter of the elementary* Grammar. *But on this occasion alone, the verbs of the first form—namely* <term>Active</term> <note>Verbs where the subject performs the action, like "I carry."</note> *along with* <term>Neuter</term> <note>Verbs that show a state or action that doesn't take a direct object, like "I stand."</note>—*will be taught to be conjugated; with* <term>Passive</term> <note>Verbs where the subject receives the action, like "I am carried."</note> *and* <term>Deponent</term> <note>Verbs that have a passive form but an active meaning, like "I encourage."</note> *verbs being disguised and passed over in silence. For one must proceed step by step, and young minds should never be burdened by learning many and diverse things at once.*

### VII.

221. *Finally, for the seventh time, by repeating the* Vestibulum, *let them proceed in order through the* Verbs *as they occur. There they should be generally informed regarding* Passives, *what meaning and use they have, according to the attached* Elementary Grammar, etc.

222. In the afternoon, let those same Conjugations be transcribed into exercise books in the manner already designated.

223. And here the practice of the Language <note>Speaking and using the language actively, rather than just reciting grammar.</note> can now also be conveniently begun. Not that it was forbidden before, but

### VIII.

224. *Eighth, let the little book be repeated, so that they may recite from memory even entire pages, to run through them as quickly as possible. And let the exercise of declining and conjugating be added, likewise now from memory, without looking at the tables; so that they may now become accustomed to "swimming without corks" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning to perform a task without assistance or training aids.</note>. An exception is made, however, for verbs of the second form recently begun, not yet mastered, but eventually to be mastered. Here, therefore, it remains permissible to use the Table like splints <note>original: *serperastrorum*; wooden supports used to straighten the legs of children, used here as a metaphor for grammar tables that support a student's "wobbly" memory.</note>.*

225. *The writing exercises (in the afternoon) during these days will be the Variation of sentences by number, changing the singular into the plural, and vice versa. For example: let them transcribe the entrance to the* Vestibulum <note>The "Entrance" or introductory section of the textbook.</note> *as follows: "Come, boy, learn beautiful and elegant languages: and grasp according to your capacity various things, the seed of wisdom. God will help you, the Teacher will love you, another (whoever..."* <note>original: *Veni puer, disce linguas pulchras & elegantes: & comprehende pro tuo captu rem variam, sapientiæ semen. Do this through the whole little book.

...to mix in a word in the mother tongue <note>original: "Vernaculam Vocem"</note> that is not found in the Index of the *Vestibulum*, and show the corresponding Latin word within the *Vestibulum* itself. (Or if he does include such a word, he should immediately teach how it is to be expressed in Latin.) Nor should he form sentences with two or three clauses, but rather simple ones. For it must be remembered that the "infant-speech" <note>original: "balbutiem"</note> of Latin is only now being shaped, and one must "babble" <note>Comenius suggests that teachers should match the simple, foundational language level of their students rather than using complex rhetoric too early.</note> along with those who are just beginning to speak.

233. *I advise, and not without reason, that the Index of Words be only in the mother tongue* (without adding the Latin words, but instead providing the sentence number where that word is translated into Latin). First, because "entities are not to be multiplied without necessity" <note>original: "entia non sunt multiplicanda sine necessitate"; this is a famous philosophical principle known as Occam’s Razor, suggesting that the simplest method is usually the best.</note>: why then should the translation of words, which has already been done in the main text, be repeated in the Index? Second, it is useful for the students to look at the text as often as possible and become very familiar with it; they should therefore be directed back to it at every opportunity. Finally, the little index <note>original: "Indecula"</note> will be shorter because of this, making the booklet more affordable and portable.

234. While examining a student's composition, the Teacher <note>original: "Præceptor"</note> should also inquire into the reasons behind it (so that they may become accustomed to writing and speaking with logic): *“Why did you put it this way? Why not that way?”* In this exercise, the teacher may even deliberately suggest an incorrect word ending. For example: "Why do you write *Venite* <note>English: "Come" (plural command)</note>? Why not *Venāte*, like *Amāte* <note>English: "Love" (plural command)</note>, or *Venēte*, like *Docēte* <note>English: "Teach" (plural command)</note>?" Let the student respond, and be taught how to respond: because

**235.** This will involve shaping the understanding of precepts and rules through Practice, so that they may bear the fruit of Practice: and thus precepts will truly be precepts, and norms truly norms and guides. Without this, no matter how much care is taken in studying grammar books, they will serve no purpose other than to weary, torture, and dull young minds.

**236.** In this way, having been thoroughly polished <note>original: "decies dedolatus"; literally "hewn ten times," a metaphor for refining a piece of wood or stone until it is smooth.</note> at the <term>Vestibulum</term> <note>The "Vestibulum" or "Porch" was Comenius's introductory textbook for absolute beginners.</note> of Latinity, the Latin <term>tyro</term> <note>A "tyro" is a beginner or a recruit in training.</note> will be judged fit to enter the <term>Janua</term> <note>The "Janua Linguarum Reserata" or "The Gate of Tongues Unlocked" was Comenius's famous intermediate textbook.</note> itself.

# THE DIDACTICS OF THE GATE
## of the Latin Language

**237.** We set the goal for the second class of Latinity so that the boy may be educated to the point where: (1.) *He understands all common words of the Latin speech*, and grasps their true and genuine sense—that is, their proper meaning—precisely <note>original: "examussim"; a term from masonry meaning "according to the level" or perfectly straight.</note>. (2.) *He knows how to give reasons for the meanings* (as far as can be done through usage) by virtue of their origin <note>meaning the etymology or the root of the word.</note>. (3.) Finally, *that he may write and speak correctly* (that is, grammatically, without faults) whatever matters he already understands.

**238.** If these three things, within the span of a year (which we designated before, § 146.)

239. For these three things will clearly pave the way

240. Regarding the first point, *Ravenius* <note>Likely referring to a contemporary educational reformer or rhetorician critiquing the state of German schools.</note>

they strive for the highest style of speaking 
right from the very beginning, surrendering 
themselves immediately to the imitation of Cicero 
and other grandiloquent authors by an insane plan.

241. He advises, therefore, to proceed step-by-step, 
from the humble style of speaking to the middle;

(which must be properly observed), one should 
not even begin with the humble style, but first 
by training Youth in the common style, 
which is suitable for every character.

(179)

...[training for the next] step of eloquence, but also the foundation of all *Eloquence*. Indeed, he proves—through the examples and testimonies of *Cicero* and *Quintilian* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE) and Quintilian (c. 35–100 CE) were the most influential Roman authorities on oratory and education.</note>, as well as *Lysias* and *Isocrates* <note>Lysias (c. 445–380 BCE) and Isocrates (436–338 BCE) were celebrated Greek orators known for their clarity and stylistic precision.</note>—that the primary power of an already flowing and pressing *Eloquence* lies not in those final ornaments of tropes and figures <note>Tropes and figures are rhetorical devices, like metaphors or irony, used to embellish speech.</note>, but in those common and proper expressions of things. Finally, he shows that without this foundation, every effort toward *Eloquence* is ridiculous and dissolves into trifles.

243. This is something we experience in ourselves—those of us who were not led by this happy and easy path, but were dragged through all sorts of detours. This applies not only to those of us who at last recognize and deplore our own thinness, dryness, and roughness of style; but also to those who are "sublime" in their own opinion. Swollen with wordy loquacity, whenever they bring forth and publicly display the strength of their eloquence, they offer nothing but either the rhapsodies <note>original: "rhapsodias"; here meaning disconnected fragments or collections of other people's writings patched together.</note> of others or their own "bubble-like" trifles—mere swellings, one might say, and inflated words, cracking and gaping in various ways. They are marveled at only by themselves and those like them, but are laughed at by the wise.

244. If, therefore, this second class of our *Latin Studies* <note>original: "Latinitatis"</note> can adequately teach how to express the thoughts of the mind in direct and pure speech (namely, through the *native and proper meaning of words*), at least in ne-

(180)

...[things which must be] expressed (necessary things in the meantime); for otherwise, might we not be judged to have failed in laying a correct foundation for the higher structure that follows?

245. Yet one can arrive at that point by no surer way than through the path of etymological study <term>Etymology: the study of the origins and historical development of words.</term>. For if the genuine meanings of all words are left to be learned by usage alone, it requires far too vast a span of time, and the memory is overwhelmed when everything is not organized into specific classes. Indeed, the intellect will always waver, unfenced by any boundaries of reason, so that it cannot see how those things which belong together are truly connected. *But when you see from where a word has its origin, you more quickly understand the essence of that thing which you desire to examine, says Isidore.* <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 CE) was a scholar and bishop whose work, the "Etymologies," served as the standard encyclopedia of the Middle Ages. Original: "At dum vides, unde ortum sit verbum, citius intelligis vim ejus Rei, quam cupis intro-spicere."</note>

246. The most renowned Becmannus <note>Christianus Becmann (1580–1648) was a German theologian and linguist famous for his work on Latin origins.</note> (in his "golden" work *On the Origins of the Latin Language*) reckons and illustrates with examples seven utilities of this study, which inquires into the reasons behind words. Namely: (1.) It leads to a clear understanding of the proper use of words, so that one word is not indiscriminately stuffed in for another. (2.) It opens a path to the knowledge of things; (just as Aulus Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – after 180 CE) was a Roman author known for his "Attic Nights," a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note> reports that Labeo Antistius <note>Marcus Antistius Labeo (died c. 10 CE) was an eminent Roman jurist who applied grammatical and etymological analysis to legal problems.</note> untangled countless knots in civil law by no other means more than by the diligent investigation of the origins of words. For [he says] that almost no term is used haphazardly...)

<meta>This page continues the enumeration of the seven benefits of etymological study, picking up from the second point regarding the purposeful "imposition" or naming of things.
