<meta>catchword: tione,</meta>

[not] fabricated, but applied with purpose: so that the *Origin of the Word* is usually an index of its *nature*, that is, of the unique property and the end for which the thing was ordered. (3.) This etymology provides Orators with the benefit that, once the property is recognized, they may know what force and efficacy resides in the word; this allows for a better-established choice of words, which *Caesar* (as quoted by *Cicero*) calls the very origin of Eloquence. <note>Julius Caesar wrote a lost grammatical work titled *De Analogia*, which Cicero praised for its precision.</note> (4.) It serves in recognizing the quantities of syllables <note>In Latin, the length of a vowel (long or short) often depends on the word's root.</note>; (5.) and it leads to a more exact spelling of words. (6.) And a great defense is prepared for the Memory, while many expressions are reduced to one simple, primary root; and many meanings are brought back to a single chief principle. (7.) Finally, it is a thing full of sincere pleasure, and "sweeter than sweet honey," to see how aptly things and words descend from one another, and embrace each other in turn.

247. It is no wonder, therefore, that the most excellent minds of every age have not merely toyed with researching the origins of words, but have applied serious and severe labor to it. Consider, for example, *Plato, Aristotle, Cicero, Caesar, Galen, Varro, Festus, both of the Scaligers*, <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558) and his son Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) were towering figures in Renaissance scholarship and linguistics.</note> and others. (182)

<meta>catchword: Hebrew)</meta>

[distinc]tion, <note>original: "tione"</note> "I have given you an example, so that you may do more and better things") <note>This appears to be a variation of John 13:15, "For I have given you an example, that ye should do as I have done to you."</note> **Matthias Martinius, Georg Cruciger, Christian Becmannus himself, D. Gregorius Francus**, etc. And **Julius Caesar Scaliger** writes: *He who thinks that care for words is unworthy of a wise man, unteaches us the very fact that we are human.*

248. All these points show that these aids should not be denied to school-aged youth (so that they may be moved more promptly and effectively toward the goal of a true and solid knowledge of **Things and Words** <note>The distinction between "Res" (Things/Reality) and "Verba" (Words/Language) was a cornerstone of Renaissance education; the idea was that one cannot understand the essence of a thing without first mastering the word that names it.</note>): rather, we must take care to transfer this most useful and pleasant matter from the laborious workshops of the professional critics to the games of the schoolboys. Thus, through these lighter matters (**the rational speculation of words**), a beginning may be made in philosophizing about the things themselves. For it is rightly said: *Contemplation of things is more important, but contemplation of words comes first.* And **Galen** <note>A famous Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose works dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> says: *To wish to know a thing without an explanation of its name is to waste one's time. For the logic of etymology is the best principle for understanding.*

249. We will therefore best provide for the studies of children if we make them clear and accurate regarding etymologies, at least regarding those things derived from Latin sources. As for the rest, which are held as primary roots among the Latins, it will be enough for them to be derived from the Greek (and a very few even from the Hebrew).

<summary>This page concludes a list of scholarly authorities who support etymology and argues that studying the origins of words is the essential first step for children to understand the nature of reality and philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, pedagogy, Latin, Greek, Hebrew, Things vs. Words, Julius Caesar Scaliger, Galen, education, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for teaching etymology to children, specifically using the relationship between Hebrew, Greek, and Latin to foster a love for ancient languages. It then outlines the three-part pedagogical structure for the "Second School of Latin."</meta>

(183)

<meta>catchword: etymologi-</meta>

Hebrew) words: so that the boys, noticing that Greek is the mother and Latin the daughter, may be drawn into a love for the former as being more ancient and pure, and by gradually acquiring a taste for it, they may become accustomed to reading and understanding Greek texts.

250. And because words by themselves do not complete a speech, but rather words joined together and arranged in their proper order, it will not be enough to master the exact meanings of words and the ways they signify; rather, it will be necessary to additionally know their lawful "marriages" <note>original: *connubia*; a metaphorical term for how words join together in syntax</note>. And this will be the third objective that this second School of Latin proposes to fulfill: ***Congruity of Speech*** <note>term: *Sermonis Congruitas*: This refers to syntax and grammar, ensuring that words agree with one another in a sentence.</note>.

251. *Therefore, there will be a threefold apparatus here for exercising young minds. First is the text of the **Gate** <note>original: *Janua*; referring to Comenius’s famous textbook *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Tongues Unlocked), which taught language through descriptions of the world</note> itself, woven from the common vocabulary of the entire Latin language and arranged according to the order of **Things** themselves—so that each word presents and introduces itself to the intellect only in its proper place and with its native meaning. Whatever more accurate observation and more extensive practice show to still be lacking in this text for completeness and elegance can be gradually perfected through our own care and that of others.*

252. *The second part is that which we shall shortly provide, **with God's help** <note>original Greek: *σὺν θεῷ* (syn theō)</note>, the "Rational" of all words: that is, an **Etymological Lexicon**, woven from the principal etymologists of our age...*

<summary>This page outlines a pedagogical strategy for teaching Latin and Greek through their historical relationships and introduces a three-part system for linguistic mastery: the "Janua" text for vocabulary, an Etymological Lexicon for the "reason" of words, and the study of "Congruity" (syntax).</summary>

(184)

<meta>catchword: gulas,</meta>

etymologists, especially *Martinius, Beckman, Alsted,* and *Colding* <note>These were prominent 17th-century scholars—Matthias Martinius, Christian Beckman, Johann Heinrich Alsted, and Erasmus Colding—who wrote influential works on word origins and education.</note>: but with greater brevity and ease (*as the work itself will prove*) than has been done until now: so that I hope wise judges of these matters will say, *“Those things which belonged to old men have been made child's play.”*

253. To this, we will add that promised treatise on ***Homonyms, Paronyms, and Synonyms*** (that is, ambiguous words which either have a different meaning with the same or similar sound, or again, have plainly the same or a very close meaning with a different sound), which is most useful for students.

254. And finally, the ***rules and precepts of Grammar, arranged for brief ease and swift practice according to the true laws of natural Education*** <note>original: *Didacticæ*</note>. **The lawful use of all these things must now be taught here.**

255. From here on, we want the **GATE** <note>original: *JANVAM*; referring to the intermediate textbook *The Gate of Tongues Unlocked*</note> to be passed through ten times over, just as the **ENTRANCE** <note>original: *VESTIBVLVM*; the introductory textbook</note> was before: not only with the eyes and the tongue, but also with the hand and exercises of the memory and the intellect, so that we may make the students truly fit to be admitted to the **PALACE** <note>original: *PALATIO*; the most advanced stage of Latin study involving eloquence and rhetoric</note>.

256. *First, let it be run through in a plain reading, for the sake of correct and swift pronunciation, and for a general and preliminary acquaintance with the whole book. Therefore, let the students read in order, each one reading individual sentences* <note>original: *periodos*</note>—

I shall add: *Why do I advise that everything being learned should be transcribed by the students' own hands? First, so that they may acquire a ready hand (for only a frequently repeated action grants a habit). Second, so that it may be a more evident proof to Parents and Schoolmasters <term>Scholarchis</term> <note>officials or inspectors in charge of overseeing schools</note> that they have entirely learned what they ought to. Third, as a support for the Memory <term>Memoriæ subsidium</term>: which grasps everything more easily and stores it more deeply the more senses it uses, and the longer it dwells on a single thing. Hence they say of Philip Melanchthon <note>1497–1560, a famous German reformer and educator known as the "Teacher of Germany"</note> that whenever he wanted to make some small book remarkably familiar to himself,*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the benefits of transcription, completing a thought from the previous page about why students should copy texts by hand. It moves on to discuss the necessity of speaking Latin at all times during this stage of education.</meta>

(186)

was accustomed to transcribe, even though he had the printed version in his own library. Finally, we want the boys to become accustomed to engaging attentively in the reading of Authors, and to record in diaries or **commonplace books** <term>locos communes</term> <note>personal notebooks used to collect useful quotes, ideas, or vocabulary for future reference</note>, or in some other way transfer whatever useful things they read for immediate use. ->N B.<- <note>Latin: *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note well"</note>

259. This also must be advised, and fixed like a law: in this second **School of Latinity**, perpetual exercises of the Language are required: namely, that the conversations of the boys, both among themselves and with the Teacher (and the Teachers’ speech to them), must be in nothing but Latin. This is just as that great reformer of schools, **Sturm** <note>Johannes Sturm (1507–1589), an influential German educator who established a model for humanist secondary education</note>, had once solemnly decreed: "When the boys enter the schools," he says, "when they return home, when they play together, when they walk together, when they meet one another, let the speech be Latin (or Greek). There is no place for pardon if anyone willfully breaks this rule." (*Classical Letters*, book 3). Indeed, the loose morals of the schools have profaned this decree; however, it is greatly in the interest of both those teaching and those learning that it be called back into force.

### II.

260. *Second, let the booklet of the Gate* <note>original: *Januæ libellus*; referring to the textbook *The Gate of Tongues Unlocked*</note> *be reassumed...*

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the educational philosophy that prioritized the understanding of physical and conceptual "Things" before the mastery of "Words." It transitions from the physical act of speaking Latin to the psychological process of connecting language to reality, specifically referencing the use of Comenius's textbook, the *Janua Linguarum Reserata*.</meta>

(187)

...a little book, *for the sake of explaining things* <note>original: *Rerum enucleandarum gratiâ*</note>. Namely, so that before they learn to express *Things through Words* correctly, they first learn to understand them correctly.

->N B.<- <note>Latin: *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note well"</note>

*For we maintain perpetually that we are shaping Men, an intelligent Creature: not for chatter and trifles, but for the rational use of Things and wise discourse about them. Neither of these can be obtained without a prior, clear, and distinct knowledge of Things, as they reveal themselves through their differences. For if the knowledge of Things is confused, speech will also be confused. Indeed, language does not touch or express Things directly, but rather the Concepts of things as they shine within the mind. Therefore, Concepts must first be formed so that they are correct; and then it will be easy to form the language so that it correctly expresses what the Mind correctly understands.*

261. *Therefore, it is simply necessary that before the words of the **Gate** <term>Janua</term> <note>referring to 'The Gate of Tongues Unlocked,' a famous textbook by Comenius that taught grammar through meaningful sentences about the world</note> are more accurately analyzed, the Things contained under them must first become known. This will require some diligence from the Teachers. For although the 'Gate' contains nothing so far above a child’s grasp, nevertheless, because children are children—whose minds wander aimlessly and only graze the surface of things everywhere in passing—their attention must be captured in some way, so that [they stick] more firmly to Things...*

<meta>catchword: more firmly</meta>

<summary>This page argues that education must prioritize the clear understanding of concepts and physical reality ("Things") before teaching the vocabulary ("Words") used to describe them. It emphasizes that since language reflects the mind's concepts, correct thinking is the essential prerequisite for correct speech.</summary>

<keywords>pedagogy, Janua Linguarum, Things vs Words, cognition, linguistics, child psychology, Comenius, logic</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the method of teaching with the Janua Linguarum Reserata (The Gate of Tongues Unlocked). The previous page ended with the need to capture children's wandering attention so they might focus on the "Things" being described.</meta>

(188)

...to attend more fixedly, and become accustomed to observing their differences more exactly.

262. This cannot be accomplished more conveniently in any other way than if **Teachers** <term>Præceptores</term> conduct an accurate inventory of **Things** <note>original: *censum Rerum*</note> in the presence of their students, by showing what each individual thing is, and how it differs from others of the same kind. If the **Gate** <term>Janua</term> <note>Comenius’s textbook, which taught Latin through descriptions of the natural and social world</note> speaks too concisely in its usual manner, the teacher should narrate more fully as the need arises. (He must observe diligently that he makes all **Students** <term>Discipulos</term> completely attentive to him—a teaching technique we demonstrated previously in section 175.)

263. *For example:* Before the explanation of Chapter II (*concerning the origin of the World*), the teacher could say: that the wise men (whom we call **Philosophers**) investigated the origin of the World quite anxiously—asking whether it came from itself or from elsewhere—but they could discover nothing certain, since no one was present to witness those first beginnings of things, and so on. However, he can explain that God revealed through **Moses** how the matter was done. (Then he can recite the summary of the first chapter of **Genesis** and add:) Our *Gate* has expressed this summarily thus: *God created all things out of nothing <note>original: *è nihilo*</note>, etc.*

264. In re-reading the text of the *Gate*, as often as he comes across something needing explanation, he shall explain it either through examples or through **sensory demonstration** <term>sensualem demonstrationem</term> <note>The pedagogical practice of showing a physical object or an image to the students so they learn through their senses rather than just through abstract words</note>. As seen in chapter [X], section 20.

<summary>This page outlines practical classroom methods for using the Janua textbook, emphasizing that teachers should provide a "census of things" and use sensory demonstrations to ensure students understand the reality behind the words.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the instructional guidance for teachers using the *Janua Linguarum Reserata*. It provides specific examples of how to expand upon the brief descriptions of Creation found in the textbook to make them more vivid for young students.</meta>

(189)

*In formless darkness*) <note>original: *Informi caligine*</note> let the teacher add: **Moses** calls this a mixture of Earth and Water—dark, empty, and void. The **Philosophers** <note>Ancient Greek thinkers</note> called this **Chaos**, which means a confused state of things, and so on. **Likewise**, in section 21, regarding the phrase *He had conceived the Idea within himself*, let him add: For craftsmen, before they build a work, are accustomed to devise a form or pattern (**a model, a prototype, a blueprint, etc.** <note>original German: *ein Muſter, Vorbild, Grundriß*</note>) in their mind, or even to fashion it out of material, **so that one actually sees what it is meant to become, etc.**

**Likewise**, in section 22: *He assigned to each its own place, measure, and kind*, let the teacher illustrate this through examples, thus: 

*Place*) So that the Earth is in the lower place and the heavens in the higher; that on the Earth, stones, trees, and animals might contain themselves, while the Sun, the Stars, and the Clouds remain on high, and so on.

*Measure*) <note>original: *Modum*</note> That there be no more water than is proper, nor more fire, but rather a balance <note>original: *temperaturam*</note> suited to the World. That a **Mouse** does not grow to the size of a **Cat**, nor an **Ox** remain as small as a **Sheep**, but each remains as the **Creator** ordained. 

*Kind*) <note>original: *Genus*</note> That from a seed of **Wheat**, wheat is born and not **Oats**; from a **Walnut**, a walnut tree grows, not an **Oak** or a **Willow**; and from a **Human**, a human is born, not a donkey, an ape, or a frog.

265. It is impossible that the minds of children should not be delighted and enticed by such lessons, if they are delivered in a "childish" manner—that is, using **plain wisdom** <note>original: *crassiusculâ Minervâ*, a Latin idiom for simple, down-to-earth instruction rather than complex theory</note> and with familiar, gentle gestures and words.

<margin>1</margin>
...be brought forth. It is to be hoped that someone would compile such *short commentaries on the Gate* <note>The *Janua*, or "Gate," was a famous 17th-century textbook that taught Latin through descriptions of the world</note> for the benefit of Teachers who are not yet well-versed in "realities" <note>original: *realibus*—the study of physical objects and nature rather than just grammar</note>: since, by an unfortunate method, some instructors never progress beyond grammatical rules. Whenever they encounter these realities (whether natural or man-made), they seem to themselves to be transported into another world; and therefore, they either avoid such subjects or explain them miserably.

266. *The exercise of style during these days* remains the same as before: the transcribing of the Text of the *Gate*.

### III.

267. *Thirdly, let the Gate be run through again for an even richer understanding of Things and words*: which should be done in this manner. All being ordered to close their books, let the Teacher <note>original: *Praeceptor*</note> recite a Latin sentence, or a whole section, and order one student or another (in no particular order) to say the same thing in the Mother Tongue. Then, let him examine how individual words are rendered in the mother tongue. Thus, it will be necessary for all students to come prepared for anything, having performed a diligent repetition at home beforehand. For if the lesson is conducted as a competition, each student will think: *Let the itch take the hindmost!* <note>original: *Occupet extremum scabies!*—a common Latin idiom, similar to "the devil take the hindmost," meaning no one wants to be last or slowest</note> and would prefer to be praised more often for surpassing others with examples of diligence, rather than to be left behind and laughed at or embarrassed.

268. *The Exercise of Style here is the Transcribing of the Grammar, which is attached to the Gate.*

...at least of its first part, which is called *Etymology* <note>original: *Etymologia*—in this period, this term referred to the study of the parts of speech and their inflections, rather than just the history of words</note>.

**269.** *The fourth treatment of the Gate* will be for the beginning of *grammatical practice*: namely, so that whatever remains of Etymology may be learned. This includes, for instance, the *accurate distinction of all parts of speech*; the *declension of nouns and verbs*, especially the *irregular ones*; but above all, that they may thoroughly learn the *principal tenses of all verbs* (*Present, Preterite, and Supine* <note>original: *supinum*—a Latin verbal noun used to indicate purpose</note>). To this end, therefore, let the entire text of the *Gate* be gone through, so that all *words* and *particles* <note>original: *Voces & Voculæ*—referring to both major vocabulary and smaller functional words</note> may be examined: what they are, and how they are inflected? Let whatever needs to be noted about any of them be demonstrated from the attached *Grammar* and *Lexicon* <note>original: *Lexico*—a dictionary or word-reference book</note>. The Teacher <note>original: *Præceptor*</note> will do this for a time, and will show the method of gathering those observations from the Lexicon and Grammar; afterward, he shall require it of the students, so that they may learn to use the Grammar and Lexicon with complete facility.

**270.** *In place of a writing exercise*, let there be a continuation of *transcribing the Grammar*: so that they may prepare for themselves a general and (as yet) undifferentiated knowledge of all the rules, before the use of those rules begins to be demonstrated and insisted upon.

### V.

**271. For the fifth** time, let the *Gate* be gone through for the sake of learning the *Origins of words* <note>original: *Originationum*—the study of root words and how new words are derived from them</note>. So that this may succeed more happily, let it be read through...

Once this is completed within two, three, or four hours, let them go through the entire ***Gate*** <note>original: *Janua*—the main textbook</note>, and let all the vocabulary be examined one by one: where each word comes from, and what is the logic behind its meaning? The Lexicon will facilitate all of this in such a way that nothing remains for the **Teachers** and **Students** except the pleasant labor of inquiring, re-reading, and repeating often, until everything is deeply seated in the mind. For this study, as we have said before, holds no difficulty or boredom, much sweetness, and most of all—more than the unexperienced can imagine—immense usefulness.

272. *During the afternoon hours, let them transcribe the same Lexicon while the **Teacher** walks about, keeping watch so that everyone writes diligently, cleanly, and correctly.*

### VI.

273. *The **Treatise on Homonyms, Paronyms, and Synonyms** <note>Homonyms: words that sound alike but have different meanings; Paronyms: words with a similar form or root; Synonyms: different words with similar meanings</note>, so that it may be properly understood and serve the purpose for which it is intended (that is, it should leave nothing confused in the meanings of words, but make everything distinct and clear, as much as this elementary study permits):* it must be re-read and explained to the boys; and soon the entire *Gate* must be run through (and this now for the sixth time) in that

...in such a manner that the students are ordered to inquire into and point out for themselves those words—both the **homonyms** and **paronyms** <note>original: *homonymas & paronymas*—words that sound the same but have different meanings, and words derived from the same root</note>—and to distinguish their meanings. They will not be able to achieve this by any other method than by frequent re-reading, and especially by reviewing that treatise, along with its attached **Index** of all homonyms and paronyms, before every **Lesson**, until everything is firmly fixed in the memory. For eventually, they will be required to recite that entire little **Treatise** <term>Tractatulum</term> <note>A small handbook or formal discourse used as a study aid.</note>.

For it will not consist of more than eight or ten small leaves <note>original: *foliola*—referring to small sheets of paper or pages</note>.*

->VII.<-

275. *But now it will be time for the structure of **connected words** <note>original: *Vocum conjunctarum*—referring to the way words are joined together into phrases and sentences</note> to be shown to the students: Therefore, let them return to the entrance of the **Gate** <note>original: *Janua*—referring to the textbook *The Gate of Languages*</note>, and while going (for the seventh time) through the whole **Gate**, let the logic of the construction be shown in every sentence through the **Syntactic Rules** <term>syntacticas... Regulas</term> <note>Syntax refers to the rules governing how words are arranged to form correct sentences.</note> appended to the **Gate**.* In order for this to proceed more efficiently, we plan to mark the rules of the entire **syntax** with a single continuous set of numbers: and then to assign those same numbers within the text of the **Gate** itself, so that it may be immediately clear which **Rule** explains why each thing is phrased in such a way. In this way, no labor will remain except the construction...

In this way, we seem likely to provide for both the diligent and ingenious—so that, seeing everything made clear, they may fly through this study of syntax even more eagerly—as well as for the lazy and slower students, so that, seeing that they too can make progress, they may continue to move forward.

276. *To ensure that those syntactic Rules stick more easily in the memory, let them be copied out during these days, in the hours destined for the **exercise of the pen** <note>original: *exercitio styli*—time specifically set aside for handwriting practice</note>. But let them not be bare Rules, as the printed copy will have them: but with examples added from the text of the **Gate** <note>original: *Januæ*—referring to the textbook *Janua Linguarum Reserata*</note>, which the numbers placed under each Rule will indicate. This exercise will hold more in its hidden depth than it promises at the outset.*

### VIII.

277. *For the eighth time, let the **Gate** be run through for the sake of **Orthography** <term>Orthographia</term> <note>The study of correct spelling and writing</note> and **Prosody** <term>Prosodia</term> <note>The study of correct pronunciation, accent, and rhythm</note>.* Therefore, let the precepts of both from the attached **Grammar** be presented beforehand: and in re-reading the text itself, let there be shown (and a little later, required in examination) the method of correctly writing and pronouncing Letters, Syllables, Words, sentences, and **Periods** <note>original: *Periodos*—complete sentences or rhetorical units</note>.

->N.B.<- <note>original: *Nota Bene*—Note Well</note>

...of pronouncing. (For all those things are to be brought under the rules of Orthography and Prosody; Orthography is not to be restricted merely to Letters, nor Prosody merely to Syllables:

<meta>catchword: gendæ:</meta>

<summary>This page describes the final steps of mastering the Janua textbook, emphasizing the transcription of syntax rules and the transition into studying orthography and prosody. The author argues that spelling and pronunciation rules should apply to whole sentences and rhetorical units, not just individual letters or syllables.</summary>
<keywords>syntax, orthography, prosody, transcription, pedagogy, Janua Linguarum, Latin grammar, handwriting exercises</keywords>

(195)

...to be observed: as is commonly done ineptly. Since even entire **Periods** <note>original: *Periodi*—complete rhetorical sentences</note> and **Paragraphs**, indeed the **Text** itself, have their own laws of correct writing and pronunciation.

278 *But it seems that on this occasion, the **Recitation** of the text of the **Gate** from **Memory** should be added.* For by now, through reading, re-reading, explanation, analysis, etc., repeated so many times in various ways, all things seem to have been able to settle deeply enough into the mind. Therefore, let everyone repeat [the text] to themselves privately, and let those who are ordered to do so recite publicly, whether entire sheets, or two or three chapters within one hour. When it is established that everyone knows it, the rest of the hour should be devoted to an orthographic and prosodic examination <note>Testing correct spelling and pronunciation</note>, as said before: so that within two or three weeks, the end of the little book may be reached again. For we hope that everything will be easy, since we have come this far through the aforementioned steps.

279. *The hand exercises here will also be none other than the writing out of the **Orthographic** and **Prosodic** rules*, as often as time allows; or as the wise <term>Teacher</term> <note>original: *Informator*—one who shapes or instructs the student's mind</note> shall judge to be necessary.

->IX.<-

280. Ninthly, let the **GATE** <note>The *Janua Linguarum Reserata*</note> return, for the sake of a quasi-**Logical** <term>Logica</term> analysis, through **Questions** <term>Quaestiones</term>. This will be an excellent path toward [understanding] the power of Things and Words, arising from their various order, connection, and relationship to other things and words...

<meta>This page continues the description of the ninth "pass" through the textbook, which focuses on a logical analysis of the text through a question-and-answer method to sharpen the student's understanding of sentence structure.</meta>

(196)

...to be observed: it will likewise instill ingenuity <note>original: *solertiam*—skill or mental flexibility</note> wisely and with reason, both for considering anything and for answering about anything. Finally, it will serve the correct and natural practice of <term>Syntax</term> <note>The arrangement of words to create well-formed sentences</note> in such a way that the logic of structure will finally emerge truly clear and be readily available. For when the <term>Teacher</term> <note>original: *Præceptor*</note> takes from any sentence the main part—whether a Noun, a Verb, or both—to form a question from it: it will be the student's duty to form the answer from the remaining parts. Because this answer will consist of words placed neither in the same number nor the same order as the complete sentence itself, but rather shortened and changed, his attention and judgment will necessarily be sharpened here.

281. This will be easy in some places: namely, where questions and answers are already prepared. As in section 2: *What does it mean to be Learned?* <note>original: *Eruditum esse*</note> Answer: *To know the differences between things, and to be able to designate each one by its own name.* Elsewhere it will be a little more difficult: yet Reason will everywhere dictate what should be asked and what should be answered. As in section 4: *What are the foundations of <term>Learning</term>?* <note>original: *Eruditionis*</note> Answer: *The <term>Nomenclature</term> <note>A systematic set of names for things</note> of Nature and Art.* And again: *How are these foundations laid?* Answer: *By learning thoroughly the Nomenclature of Nature and Art, etc.*

->NB.<- <note>Latin: *Nota Bene*—"Note well" or "Pay attention"</note>

282. However simple this exercise...

<summary>This page explains a teaching method where students must deconstruct sentences to answer questions, a process intended to master syntax and sharpen logical thinking. ...and though it might seem childish, it will be so effective for sharpening the wit <note>original: *aciem*—literally the "edge" or "sharpness"</note> of a young mind, and for directing attention directly toward both Things and Words. Therefore, so that in this part even those who do not clearly see for themselves what ought to be done may find the path opened and marked: we shall take care to provide—through a popular adaptation of the <term>Logical method</term> <note>A system of analyzing the structure and validity of arguments and sentences</note>—specific questions that can be asked regarding <term>Substantive Names</term> <note>Nouns</note>, Adjectives, Verbs, and even those smaller words in which the emphasis of the subject matter lies hidden.

283. *No other practice of style will even be necessary here than for those very things which were discussed in the morning hours to be written down: namely, by resolving everything in the entire **Gate** <note>The *Janua*, the main textbook</note> into Questions and filling them with Answers. It is not easy to say how many benefits for style and judgment this excellent kind of exercise will bring.*

### X.

284. *Let the tenth repetition of the **Gate** again be competitive, just as it was before for the **Entrance** <note>original: *Vestibuli*—the introductory textbook for younger students</note>, section 229. Let pairs compete (while the whole assembly and the Master listen) regarding readiness in reciting from memory, interpreting, declining irregular forms <note>original: *anomala*</note> and conjugating, distinguishing <term>homonyms</term> <note>Words that sound the same but have different meanings</note> and <term>paronyms</term> <note>Words derived from the same root</note>, constructing sentences <term>syntactically</term> <note>According to the rules of grammar and structure</note>, forming questions, and responding to them, etc.*

For the winner, if no other prize is available, let there be public applause and the honor of a higher seat. However, it would be desirable if other incentives for diligence—small gifts—were provided by the <term>School Patrons</term> <note>Benefactors or local authorities who supported the school financially</note>.

285. *At this stage, writing practice <note>original: *stylus*—referring to the student's ability to compose text</note> may finally begin to be exercised more freely, now that the students have been sufficiently equipped with a wealth of vocabulary, their true meanings, and the art of sentence construction.*

Therefore, they shall translate topics dictated by the Teacher from their <term>native tongue</term> <note>original: *vernaculo*—the student's everyday language</note> into Latin. The words and sentences will be supplied either by their memory or by the native-language Index attached to the end of the **Gate** <note>The *Janua Linguarum Reserata*</note> for this very purpose. Whether these are, for example, short histories, fables and moral tales <note>original: *apologi & fabulæ*</note>, or little stories of another kind—whether invented by the Teacher or borrowed from elsewhere—it will not matter; provided they contain nothing foreign to the style of the **Gate**, nor anything too figurative or metaphorical <note>original: *tropicum*—using "tropes" or figures of speech</note>, nor anything far removed from the current modest level of the students' concepts and style.

286. For those running through these race-courses <note>original: *stadia*</note>, it is enough to be led to the finishing posts <note>original: *metas*</note>, and not beyond. Indeed, we have fixed the goal of the **Gate** once and for all, so that children may appropriately express their own youthful thoughts, so far as they can arise from the knowledge of the things contained in this **Gate of Things**. If we achieve this, we have hit the mark, and here we must stop.

And so, it is pleasing for us as well to plant our feet here, at the exit of the **GATE**.

Regarding the **PALACE & TREASURES of Latinity** <note>original: *Latinitatis*—referring to the mastery of pure Latin style and vocabulary</note>, the Architects <note>The authors or designers of these advanced curriculum levels</note> themselves will be able to give the best advice in their own time: to whom, O GOD, grant life and strength, for the honor of Your name!

288. It will be worth the effort to provide one additional warning here. ->N.B.<- <note>original: *Nota Bene*—Note well</note> *It would be quite beneficial for boys being trained by this method if they were given no abundance of books except those destined for their specific Class: for the first class the* **Vestibule** <note>The *Vestibulum*, the introductory textbook</note>, *for the second the* **Gate** <note>The *Janua Linguarum Reserata*</note>, *for the third the* **Palace** <note>The *Palatium*, the most advanced textbook in this series</note>, *and so on.* The reasons why I would suggest this are readily apparent:

(1.) When the mind is intent on many things, its focus on individual things is lessened. By doing many things at once, we do nothing correctly. Therefore, let them have only these books for the time being, crafted with such great study for their use; let them attend to these, so they are not distracted by others. *Assiduous practice devoted to a single thing often overcomes both natural talent and art*, as Cicero says <note>The author quotes Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman, to emphasize that mastery comes from focus</note>.

(2.) Boys are not yet able to exercise a selection of materials; they can easily fall into things that confuse and entangle them if there is an opportunity to handle just anything.

(3.) It is good to train boys in that which the Roman heralds used to proclaim at the Capitol to those entering to perform sacred rites: **DO THIS** <note>original: *Hoc AGE*—a ritual command to focus entirely on the sacred task at hand without distraction</note>. In this way, they may become accustomed to lingering with authors, not flying over them.

(4.) Finally, by immoderate study, the vigor of the mind—especially

289. *And these are the matters which I have had to explain for the present.* The opportunity to contemplate these things and share them publicly was provided by the requests of certain people who sought advice on the use of the **Vestibule** and the **Gate** <note>These refer to the *Vestibulum* and the *Janua Linguarum Reserata*, Comenius's famous introductory and intermediate Latin textbooks</note>.

293. **Diligent** and **skillful Teachers** <term>Didacticos—those who specialize in the art and science of teaching</term> [whoever has been equipped with a particular dexterity to teach others successfully, whether nature has so instructed them or long practice has trained them] we pray with due reverence: that if any good counsel is available to anyone regarding the present undertaking, let them not disdain to contribute it to the common good. 300. Therefore, let **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (the Younger), a Roman Stoic philosopher</note> close [this work] with the words with which he closes his **33rd Letter** <note>original: *Epistolam suam XXXIII*; this refers to Seneca's *Moral Letters to Lucilius*</note>.

Indeed, I will use the old way; but if I find a closer path, I will pave that one. Those who set these things in motion before us are not our masters, but our guides. Truth lies open to all; it has not yet been fully occupied. Much of it has been left even for those yet to come. <note>original: *Nunquam quidquam invenietur...*</note>

### COUNSEL
## ON THE VESTIBULE,
### AND FURTHERMORE ALSO
### THE GATE OF LATINITY
### TO BE EXCELLENTLY PERFECTED:

*Suggested by letter from G. V. D. to the original author of both, Lord J. A. C.* <note>J. A. C. refers to Jan Amos Comenius (1592–1670), the pioneering Czech educator. G. V. D. likely refers to a contemporary scholar, possibly Geeraert Vossius or a similar colleague.</note>

->**Most Distinguished Sir, Lord and most favored Colleague,**<- <note>original: *Compater*; literally "godfather" or "co-parent," but used here as a warm term for a close professional associate.</note>

For a long time now, those **LITTLE WORKS OF LATINITY** <note>original: *Opuscula*; refers to Comenius's famous textbooks, the *Vestibulum* (The Entrance-Hall) and the *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Tongues Unlocked)</note>, which you have brought forth into the public eye quite successfully and opportunely—cleverly devised for the benefit of the literary youth and elegantly prepared for these early stages of study—have delighted me greatly. <meta>This page continues the introductory letter to Jan Amos Comenius regarding the refinement of his educational works. It follows from page 205, completing the word "nimirum" (doubtless/truly).</meta>

(206)

[doubtless] the **ENTRANCE-HALL** and the **GATE**. <note>original: *Vestibulum* and *Janua*; referring to Comenius's foundational Latin textbooks</note> Yet, this very thing pleased me most in you, even above all others: that in these same **Little Works** <note>original: *Opusculis*</note>, although they were received so greedily and with general applause, you not only always bore the further application of the file <note>original: *limam*; a classical metaphor for the polishing and refining of a literary text</note> with a most grateful and fair mind, but you even requested, through both private and public vows, that this be done by the Learned <note>original: *Eruditis*</note> and those dedicated to this cause.

This finally moved me in recent days, while I was examining the progress of my sons who were occupied in your **Entrance-Hall**, to begin thinking a bit more deeply myself: by what further means that most welcome and most useful little work of yours might be perfected, so that beneath this **Entrance-Hall** prepared for children <note>original: *Pueris*</note>, it might also become something for men <note>original: *Viris*</note> to inspect from every side: *So that a tender age may be led, gradually and step-by-step, by that method toward Learning and Wisdom.*

Regarding this, since you are hardly satisfied that certain things were discussed by me only by word of mouth, but you greatly desire that my thoughts on the matter be recorded on paper, I shall comply for the sake of our friendship—which is no ordinary one—especially since I see that you are looking toward this: that in such a manner, individual points may be weighed with sufficient consideration, and the judgments and advice of others on the same matter may be sought out from every-

15. To subject many **Nouns** in a group to the same **Verb** seemed proper to some of those who refined the **Vestibulum** <note>The *Vestibulum* (Entrance-Hall) was the introductory level of Jan Amos Comenius's language system, designed for beginners.</note>. However, while that method is not entirely disapproved of, it is not nearly as well-suited to a child’s understanding and memory as if individual nouns were offered with their own specific **attribution** <note>meaning a specific quality or description</note>. Therefore, where several nouns of the same class come to be listed, they should instead for the most part be divided so that they are paired with their own specific **attribute** or **predicate** <term>the part of a sentence that says something about the subject, usually containing the verb</term>. In this way, a child will never need to imprint anything on the memory unless it is joined with a mutual explanation and prepared in some way to instruct the mind through the knowledge of things.

16. In this part, care should also be taken so that no **solecism** <term>a grammatical mistake or an absurdity in logic</term> occurs anywhere in such a connection; for example, that words might happen to be connected which, when the meaning of the individual words is correctly judged, are less able [to be joined together]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Rule 24 from page 214, which addresses whether students should learn simple root words first or dive directly into the more common derived and compound forms of those words.</meta>

<page-num>(215)</page-num>

...conceived [in theory]: but it is not the same in those matters which must be adapted as closely and as effectively as possible to use and practice. In such cases, without regarding the primary origin of a word or focusing solely on its simplicity, there is often a need for **Derived** <term>Deducta: words formed from others, like "action" from "act"</term> and **Compound** <term>Composita: words made by combining two or more words, like "notebook"</term> words first, before it is even necessary to think about the primary and simple roots.

It has also been discovered that in this area, there is another principle of **Simplicity** <term>Simplicitas</term> than the one that is always suited to a child's understanding and use. It is not always safe to use the simplest roots with the expectation or claim that **Derivation** <term>Deductio</term> and **Composition** <term>Compositio</term> will thereafter be easily mastered. 

On the contrary, it has been observed that **Derived** and **Compound** words are often much more common in everyday use than **Simple** and **Primitive** <term>Primitiva: root words that are not derived from any others</term> words, and are even more common in their general meaning. Furthermore, so many compound and Derived words descend from these Simple and Primary roots that "**Declension**" <term>Declinatio: here used in the sense of word-building rather than just noun endings</term>—the term *Varro* <note>Marcus Terentius Varro was a famous Roman scholar and grammarian of the 1st century BCE.</note> uses for what we otherwise call **Derivation** <term>Derivatio</term>—and **Composition** are by no means "natural." By this, I mean they do not always behave in the same way even in similar cases (as happens in the standard **Declensions** of nouns, as they are commonly called). Instead, they vary as much as possible, even among very similar words; thus, they depend entirely on the observation of specific usage. 

For it does not follow that because one word is derived from another in a certain way, a similar word can be derived from its root in the same way; or from whatever...

<meta>catchword: ...soever [vis]</meta>

<summary>This page argues that in language teaching, common compound and derived words should often be taught before their simpler roots because they are more frequent in speech and their formation is often irregular rather than following natural rules.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 215, explaining why teaching children Latin using only root words is impractical and why common compound words are often more effective for beginners.</meta>

<page-num>(216)</page-num>

...force, or anything else, to create words without the approval of actual usage. To carry this out successfully and skillfully requires a deep understanding of the nature of things that is beyond the reach of children; indeed, it can sometimes be a difficult task even for grown men. Often, the logic of meaning varies so much between **Primary** <term>Prima: original root words</term> and **Derived** <term>Deducta</term> words that the matter can hardly be governed by any certain law. It is certainly not easy for children to move from a knowledge of **Primitives** <term>Primitivorum</term> through **General Rules** <term>Generales Regulas</term> to **Derivatives** <term>Derivata</term>. This is especially true because the sense of words varies so much between different **Languages** <term>Linguae</term> in their **Vocabulary** <term>Vocabula</term>: what is a **Compound** or **Derived** word in the **Mother Tongue** <term>Vernaculo</term> might be a **simple** and **Primitive** word in **Latin**, and vice versa.

Given these circumstances, it must be recognized as better, and indeed necessary, that nothing regarding **Derivatives** and **Compounds** <term>Compofitis</term> should be withheld from the **Vestibule** <note>The "Vestibule" (Vestibulum) was the title of the introductory level of Jan Amos Comenius's famous language-learning system.</note>. Instead, they should be admitted with this privilege: that they are not only included indiscriminately alongside **Primitives** and **simple** words, but may even be preferred over them if they happen to be in more common use, or if they represent a meaning better suited to a **Child's** understanding.

25. However, outside of that reasoning, if a particular case is clearly found to be arranged differently, then the priority could and indeed should be granted to **Primitives** and **Simple** words, so that in the **Vestibule**...

This opinion, as far as I can see at present, seems to demand something that is more commendable because of old custom—and perhaps recommended to most today because of their own early habits—rather than something that should be judged as entirely necessary or particularly useful.

In fact, that deficiency in the **Vestibule** will be more than sufficiently corrected by the fact that an **INDEX** will be added. This will clearly show the **NOMINATIVE CASES** and the **ROOTS** <term>Themata: the fundamental forms of a verb</term> of verbs, along with those things which it is proper to teach to **Children** alongside them. Through this index, provided the **Child** is thoroughly practiced in inflecting <note>“Inflecting” refers to changing the endings of nouns (declension) or verbs (conjugation) to show their grammatical role.</note> words, he will easily be able to investigate not only every **Noun**, but every **VERB** as well.

<meta>This page concludes a point about students reciting and competing with their peers before moving into a detailed defense of learning vocabulary in context. It addresses the pedagogical debate between teaching words in isolation (lists) versus teaching them within sentence structures.</meta>

<page-num>(218)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: song [original: cantum]</meta>

...be ordered to recite and occasionally compete with their fellow students.

In all other cases, every word—even if not presented in the nominative case <note>original: "Casu primo," the basic naming form of a noun</note> or according to its **Root Form** <term>Thema: the fundamental form of a word, such as a verb's present tense or a noun's nominative</term>—is learned both more easily and more surely when offered within a specific **Sentence Structure** than when presented as solitary words outside of a structure. Surely the power, meaning, and usage of words are clearly revealed and easily retained by this method. Among solitary words, the true meaning is often hidden; furthermore, their use outside of a structure is not revealed by any logical method, but is instead hammered in through laborious instruction.

Furthermore, so that the **Root Form** <note>original: "Thema"</note> may be easily and immediately apparent alongside the *Text* in every instance, and so there is no need to consult the *Index* during the first *Lesson* <term>Tractatio: the initial study or treatment of the material</term>, it would be advisable to print the more difficult forms in the margin <margin>difficult forms</margin>.

27. But if one still wishes to entice tender little ones at the very threshold of the **Vestibule** <note>The *Vestibulum* was the introductory level of Jan Amos Comenius's language system.</note> with certain **SOLITARY WORDS**, following the custom of our predecessors, so that they might enter the **Vestibule** itself more eagerly and quickly: then let a certain *Collection* <term>Sylloge: a gathered list or assembly</term> of words most familiar to children be provided first. Let these be presented from the beginning using a rhythmic method of **Similar Endings** <term>Homoioteleuta: a device where words end with the same sounds, creating a rhyme or rhythm to aid memory</term>, so that material is supplied for practicing *Declensions* <note>The patterns of changing noun endings to show their grammatical role</note> alone at the first approach.

This very thing, if not in nouns...

<summary>The author argues that vocabulary is best learned within the context of sentences rather than in isolated lists, though he concedes that rhyming word lists can be used to "entice" beginners into learning grammar.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, Latin instruction, Vestibulum, vocabulary lists, context-based learning, mnemonics, rhyming endings, grammar practice</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the pedagogical structure of the *Vestibulum* (the introductory language manual), specifically addressing the transition from practicing word forms to organized vocabulary lists and the thematic grouping of lessons.</meta>

<page-num>(219)</page-num>
<sig>L 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: 30.</meta>

...not only, but is also done with certain **Verbs**, then the material for similarly practicing **Conjugations** <note>original: "Conjugationibus," the inflectional patterns of verbs</note> will be ready at hand from the beginning.

And it will be sufficient for such things to be presented to the Boys in this manner and within that number: so that while they are busy learning to inflect those words, all other more important **Nouns** and **Verbs** can then be referred to one of those examples, as they will follow a similar pattern of inflection.

28. In that **INDEX** <term>Index: a vocabulary list or glossary</term> also, which will be placed after the **Vestibule** <note>The *Vestibulum* was Comenius's introductory textbook, designed as a "porch" to the more difficult "gate" of the language.</note>, it will be of no small value to observe this: that the derived and compound words be placed under their **Root Words** <note>original: "Primitivis," the primary forms from which other words are built</note>. By this method, whatever can contribute to easier understanding and a readier memory will be presented to the boys in the most reliable way.

29. Regarding the Order and the true Series or **Classes** through which the words, arranged into a structure, are presented to the Boys: about thirty **Chapters** <note>original: "Capita"</note> can certainly be made in this way. But it is not at all necessary that each individual item always be placed distinctly under its own specific Class. It is better if each thing is addressed whenever the occasion arises, under whichever chapter it is most relevant, where it can be touched upon through a useful and worthy **statement** <note>original: "enunciationem"</note>. A more precise order of things and words has been reserved for the **Janua** <term>Janua: refers to the *Janua Linguarum Reserata* (The Gate of Languages Unlocked), Comenius's more advanced textbook</term> according to a sufficiently sound plan.

<summary>This page describes how students should learn to categorize nouns and verbs by their patterns of inflection and argues that vocabulary indices should group related words by their roots. It also notes that while the introductory text uses roughly thirty chapters, a more rigorous systematic organization is reserved for the more advanced "Janua" textbook.</summary>

<keywords>pedagogy, Latin grammar, conjugations, nouns, verbs, vocabulary organization, Vestibulum, Janua Linguarum, mnemonic methods</keywords>

<page-num>(220)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: which</meta>

30. And finally, since those things which might seem to have been omitted under each chapter are generally found elsewhere, expressed in some appropriate place; and since, in the meantime, it is advantageous to have these same things in view under their own chapter or class, and held as if in the same unified connection: that deficiency can be supplied in this way: so that at the end of each chapter, the **Vocabulary** which might have seemed overlooked should be noted with a reference to the section (§) in which they may be read or found elsewhere.

31. So that, finally, all the more important points which have been said thus far may be gathered together as if into a **Summary** <term>Summam: a comprehensive overview or total collection of arguments</term>, we must look to the **VESTIBULE** <note>The *Vestibulum* was Jan Amos Comenius's introductory Latin textbook, intended as the "porch" to the language.</note>, and then in its own way also to the **GATE** <note>The *Janua linguarum reserata* (The Gate of Languages Unlocked) was Comenius's famous intermediate textbook.</note>, for their further polishing and perfecting.

*Do there not also occur in those works certain things that are:*
*1. Less than pure? 2. Not quite appropriate, or at least not common in usage? 3. Not sufficiently worthy or significant? 4. Only partially true? 5. Scarcely suited to the boys' understanding, or exceeding it? 6. Not sufficiently prepared for the practical use of life, etc.*

->So that in some places certain things might be removed,<-
->in other places some things added,<-
->and finally, in certain places, some things<-
->might be changed,<-
->and better and more correct things<-
->might be substituted.<-

And from here, once these things have been discovered and noted

<sig>L 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: not (ne)</meta>

...if there were certain things of that kind, care and labor must be taken so that not only those things which seem to be missing are supplied, but also that they are substituted in the places thus noticed, with things that are:

<note>The following list is grouped by a large curly brace in the original text, indicating these are the qualities of the newly substituted content.</note>
*Purer*
*More appropriate and common in use*
*More worthy*
*Of certain and full truth*
*Better suited to the students' understanding*
*Finally, more effective for the practical use of life*

In the **GATE** <note>original: *Janua*; referring to Comenius's intermediate textbook *Janua linguarum reserata*</note>, indeed, beyond these points, perhaps some other things will need to be observed individually, because of the **AIM** <note>original: *scopum*</note> that is set before it, which is broader and more elevated. But for now, I am content to stay in the **Vestibule** <note>original: *Vestibulo*; the introductory primer</note>, and not yet break through all the way to the Gate itself. Concerning that, there will be another time for me to express what I have in mind.

### **CONCERNING THE GERMAN TRANSLATION OF THE VESTIBULE**, I think these few things should be briefly noted.

1. It must indeed be observed that the translation, as far as it can be done through the **genius of the language** <term>Genium: the natural spirit, character, or inherent logic of a specific language</term>, should be as closely bound to the words as possible. Meanwhile, it would not be advisable to strive for such strictness anywhere that either the elegance of the native Latin language (which ought not to be darkened or corrupted even in the **Vestibule**) or the specific character of the German <note>original: *Germanicæ* / *Alemanicam*</note> language is neglected. For indeed, not exactly as in the former, so also in the latter, in the same precise order...

<summary>This page details the criteria for improving educational texts—emphasizing clarity, accuracy, and practical utility—and begins outlining the principles for translating the Vestibulum into German, balancing literal accuracy with the natural "genius" of the language.</summary>
<keywords>Comenius, Vestibulum, Janua linguarum, Latin pedagogy, German translation, linguistic genius, translation theory, educational reform</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific guidelines for translating the "Vestibulum" (the introductory textbook) into German, focusing on the balance between idiomatic translation and literal accuracy for pedagogical purposes.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>(222)</header>

...so that the words cannot always be connected with enough elegance or even appropriateness.

2. Effort must be made so that each item is rendered in a **pure German style** <term>Stylo Germanico: the natural idiom and phrasing of the German language</term>. One should not be overly concerned if certain phrases in German do not seem to correspond exactly, word-for-word, to the Latin.

3. However, lest the proper meaning fail to be precisely declared in any given place—and because this cannot be safely left to a less cautious **instructor**—it will be necessary to insert in parentheses the proper force of the **Latin words** <note>original: *Vocum Latinarum*</note> if the German translation seems to stray a bit too far from the literal sense.

4. It is not necessary for the German to be placed immediately beneath the **Latin text** <note>original: *Latino Textui*</note> as an interlinear translation; rather, it will be sufficient if they are set on the opposite side, facing one another. This arrangement is advisable because it will make the **examination of the boys** <note>original: *puerorum examinatio*</note> much more efficient, whether they are interpreting Latin into their native tongue or expressing the German back into Latin. Furthermore, the **WRITTEN WORK** which I saw fit to demonstrate and recommend in the **INSTRUCTIONAL MANUAL** <note>original: *Informatorio*; likely referring to Comenius's guide for teachers or parents</note> will proceed more conveniently if the Latin is kept distinct from the German.

These things I have thus hurriedly scribbled upon these papers...

For this purpose and use, I do not know whether it would be advisable for a **COMMENTARY** and something of a **"POSTIL"** <term>POSTILLAM: traditionally a series of notes or a marginal commentary on scripture, here adapted to mean a teacher's guide for a textbook</term> (to use a name of well-established usage today) to be written upon and after the *Vestibulum* <note>original: *Veſtibulum*; meaning 'The Porch' or 'Entrance,' the title of Jan Amos Comenius's introductory Latin textbook</note>: from which those who by themselves less [perceive] this...

<page-num>(228)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: com-</meta>

...can say of their intellectual offspring <note>original: "ingenii fœtibus"</note>, whoever piously attributing themselves and all they possess to God, offer them most humbly to Him as a sacrifice. Namely, that things which are done rightly are not achieved by themselves as authors, but it is God who discovered every path of knowledge, and bestows it [through whomever He finally wishes] upon Jacob His son and Israel His beloved, just as <term>Baruch<note>A book of the Apocrypha/Deuterocanon attributed to the scribe of the prophet Jeremiah.</note></term> says [3:37]. For God Himself testifies that He places Wisdom in the heart of every learned man, so that they may do what He commands [Exodus 31:6].

Now, God should be believed to command not only when He speaks from heaven and mandates in express words what He wishes to be done, as He did for His Prophets; but also when He illuminates from within and provides the opportunities for action from without [1 Kings 17:4 & 9 <note>In the Latin Vulgate, 1 Kings is referred to as 3 Kings (3 Regum), but Comenius cites it here as 1 Reg., likely following a different numbering convention common in his era.</note>]. This can be observed in both the greatest and smallest matters by anyone who has the mind to note the footprints of Divine providence and follow its guidance. 

I too, the least of all, experience how strongly that admirable wisdom of our God <term>reaches from end to end and how sweetly she orders all things<note>original: "quàm fortiter attingat, & suaviter disponat omnia." A reference to the Book of Wisdom 8:1.</note></term>; so that it is necessary to confess that whatever useful thoughts we may ever have are not ours, but God’s. Therefore, we may enjoy the solace that it is not primarily our own cause, but God’s, that is being advanced, even if malevolent men rise up to tear us and our works—which we have brought forth for God—to pieces. 

Some years ago, divine providence offered an opportunity...

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Comenius is explaining how his intellectual projects are directed by divine providence rather than his own ambition. He is about to describe the origin of his most famous educational works.</meta>

<page-num>(229)</page-num>
<meta>Catchword: lius,</meta>

...of composing a small work which prescribes a more concise way of teaching the Latin language—and indeed any other—through the very order of things <note>original: "per ipsam Rerum seriem." This refers to Comenius's revolutionary idea of teaching language alongside the study of the physical world.</note>. At the urging of friends and with the approval of my superiors, I published this under the title **THE GATE OF LANGUAGES** <note>original: "LINGVARVM JANVÆ." This was Comenius's first major success, a textbook that replaced dry grammar rules with useful sentences about the world.</note> [following the example of those from whom the opportunity arose, the Irish Fathers of the College at Salamanca in Spain <note>Referring to the Jesuit scholars at Salamanca who published a "Janua" in 1611, which inspired Comenius to improve upon their method.</note>]. This work was received with the general applause of scholars, and it gave me the occasion to think about constructing a **GATE OF THINGS** <note>original: "JANVA RERVM." While the first book taught words, this proposed book was intended to teach the fundamental principles of reality itself.</note> themselves. For it did not seem enough to teach what the words *White*, *Black*, *Hot*, *Cold*, *Plant*, *Man*, *Angel*, *Heaven*, *Church*, *God*, *Christ*, and so on, *mean*; one must also teach—and indeed, even more importantly—what those things actually *are*. My goal was so that minds might become accustomed not so much to Words as to Things.

As I progressed somewhat in this attempt, it began to offer hope of a successful outcome. Meanwhile, a work appeared under the title of **PAN-SOPHIA** <note>Greek: "Universal Wisdom."</note>—the *Encyclopedia of the Arts* by Master Peter Laurenberg. When I had most greedily acquired and examined it, I saw that it did not correspond to the grandeur of its title. (For there was nothing there concerning the object and indeed the source of true Wisdom, **CHRIST**; nothing concerning the life of the world to come—toward which he who is truly wise directs his wisdom—nor about the way to reach it, and similar matters.) I thought then that an opportunity was being given to supply what was missing: so that we might have whatever is necessary to be taught and learned in Christian schools collected into a single bundle; and indeed, by a method through which all those things might be taught to youth more briefly, more truly, and more...

<summary>Comenius describes the origins of his "Gate of Languages" and his transition toward "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom), critiquing existing encyclopedias for lacking a Christ-centered perspective on reality.</summary>

<keywords>Janua Linguarum, Janua Rerum, Pansophia, Peter Laurenberg, Irish Fathers of Salamanca, Christian education, pedagogical method, Christ-centered learning</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 229, Comenius is describing the transition from his successful language textbook, "The Gate of Languages," to his more ambitious project, "The Gate of Things" (Janua Rerum), which aimed to organize all human knowledge into a single, unified system known as Pansophia.</meta>

<page-num>(230)</page-num>

...[more usefully] <note>The text continues from the previous page's catchword "lius," likely completing the word "utilius," meaning "more usefully."</note>, that is, adapted to the ends of the present and future life, it might instill [wisdom]. For the **aim** <note>original: "Scopus"</note> was (to repeat it in passing) to compose a **summary** <note>original: "epitomen"</note> of the **Books of God**: Nature, Scripture, and the human Conscience: so that, *Whatever exists in Reality would here be held described in a single continuous series; whatever divine Revelations exist would here be applied to illuminating these Things; whatever common notions are born into the human Mind would here be arranged for their proper uses.*

Through these things we wished:
1. *To make the Holy Scripture more familiar to Christians than it has generally been until now.*
2. *To place in their hands a key that unlocks most of the greatest mysteries of Nature and Scripture.*
3. *To guide Men more and more from the studies of this life to the study of the eternal life.*

And those who have known these things from their foundation have judged, and still judge, that this is possible through paths by which progress toward the better can be made.

And so, five years ago already, there were those who both urged and demanded the publication of this little work (following the example of the previous one). But I, being more hesitant here than before, allowed myself to be persuaded only to send out the title first, inserted into a *Catalog of Books* to be published, for the sake of testing opinions <note>original: "judiciorum explorandorum gratia." Comenius often shared "proposals" or titles to see if there was enough public interest or scholarly support before committing to full publication.</note>. This was done, once and then again, so that the title **THE GATE OF THINGS** <note>original: "JANUÆ RERUM"</note>, or ***Christian Pansophia*** <note>Greek: "Pansophia" means "Universal Wisdom."</note>, was publicly offered for sale:

<summary>Comenius explains the purpose of his "Pansophia" project, which seeks to unify the "three books" of Nature, Scripture, and Mind into a single educational system to bring people closer to God. He notes his cautious approach in announcing the project under the title "The Gate of Things" before its full completion.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Gate of Things, Universal Wisdom, Nature and Scripture, Christian education, Janua Rerum, Janua Linguarum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Comenius's account of the publication of his philosophical works. The previous page ended with him mentioning a book catalog where his work was first advertised. ...might be sold: but because I soon thought it improper to **sound a trumpet before me** <note>original: "buccinare ante se." For although many learned and good men, forming great expectations of such a work, have greatly encouraged us to continue—and do not cease to encourage us (some even endowed with more excellent gifts offer their help)—there were others found at the same time who thought us guilty of unusual rashness, as if we were attempting impossible and superhuman things. <margin>The foundations of universal education.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative square woodcut initial 'C' featuring elaborate floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>The greatest thinkers have long ago attempted to gather a comprehensive summary of all education; they noticed well enough that the light of the mind does not stand firm unless the rays of the Truth of Things <note>original: "Veritatis Rerum." This refers to the objective reality of the world as created by God.</note> converge from every side. Aristotle himself wrote that the Wise Man ought to **KNOW ALL THINGS**, as far as is possible: and that he ought to establish such principles through which he may satisfy all inquiries: and that he ought to begin from those things which are more well-known, and therefore from those that are more universal: and that it is necessary for him to have a great certainty of things, and that not just in any way, but through their causes. (As Giovanni Bellarino <note>An Italian theologian and author of "The Practice of the Sciences" (1552–1630).</note> has collected these points from Aristotle in *The Practice of the Sciences*, book 4, distinction 1, number 1.) In this passage, the Philosopher <note>Commonly used in this period to refer specifically to Aristotle.</note> requires these three things for the **OMNISCIENCE** <note>original: "Omniscientiam." In this context, it refers to a human "all-knowledge" or universal learning rather than divine all-knowing.</note> that is possible for man:

1. *The Universality of Principles.*
2. *The True Method of Order.*
3. *The Infallible Certainty of Truth.*

<margin>And the various attempts of various men.</margin>
Not only did Aristotle himself and other ancients attempt to construct systematic arrangements of teachings according to these laws, but also very many writers of our own age [have attempted] Encyclopedias, *Polymatheias* <note>original: "Polymatheias" (Greek-derived). Literally "many-learnings," or works covering a vast range of subjects.</note>, *Panepistemones* <note>original: "Panepistemonas" (Greek-derived). A "universal knower" or a work encompassing all sciences.</note>, *Cyclognomic Arts* <note>original: "Artes Cyclognomicas." Arts relating to the full circle of knowledge.</note>, Syntaxes of the Marvelous Art, Great Instaurations <note>original: "Instaurationes magnas." Likely a reference to Francis Bacon’s *Instauratio Magna*, which sought to rebuild human knowledge.</note>, Transformations of the sciences, and Theaters of human Wisdom— <note>The Latin text cuts off at "hu-" with the catchword "manæ," completing the word "humanæ" (human).</note>

Although the attempts of all these authors are praiseworthy, their success was not equal; nor was their intention the same. Some proposed to reduce the entire variety of learning, as it is found scattered through libraries, into a single body; others applied a selection, seeking either subtlety, elegance, or utility for common life. Some used a concise method, tied strictly to the subject matter; others used a freer method, according to their own pleasure, in this way or that. Whatever each of them proposed or performed, one can nevertheless most truly say of all of them what Seneca declared:

<margin>Can something more still be attempted?</margin>

> *Those who were before us did much, but they did not finish everything. And among these, why not us as well, who urge the construction of a truly Universal Work: intended for the use not only of scholarly youth or the learned, but also for the whole of the Christian people, so that everyone everywhere may be taught to be more rightly wise. And since we are now deliberating on the best form of the method, an account must be given: Why should such a book be called the **TEMPLE OF CHRISTIAN UNIVERSAL WISDOM** <note>original: "CHRISTIANÆ PANSOPHIÆ TEMPLVM"</note> and be built according to the ideas, standards, and laws of the supreme Architect, **GOD**? Finally, why do we urge that it be consecrated for the *Uses* of the Universal <note>original: "Catholicæ," here used in the sense of "all-embracing" or "worldwide" rather than a specific denomination.</note> Church of **JESUS CHRIST**, gathered and to be gathered from all peoples and tongues? We must speak of each point in order.

<margin>Why must it be called a Temple? 1. Because it is a spacious theater for contemplation.</margin>

**TEMPLE** <note>original: "TEMPLVM"</note>, among the ancient Latins, was the name for any open place from which one could look out in all directions: as if it were a *Wide-view* <note>original: "Tu-emplum," a historical folk etymology suggesting a "wide space for viewing" (from *tueri*, to look, and *amplus*, wide).</note>, that is, a spacious area for gazing. From this also, to "Contemplate" <note>original: "Contemplari"</note> something is to gaze upon it freely and without impediments. In this sense, the name of "Temple" fits the Heavens most of all: because they are open to view from every side, and because **GOD** freely gazes upon all things from there. Just as David speaks: *The Lord is in his holy Temple, his throne is in heaven, his eyes behold, and his eyelids try the children of men* (*Psalm 11:4 and Psalm 33:13*). <note>The author quotes the Latin Vulgate version of the Psalms here.</note> Thus Lucretius spoke of the *Bright Temples of heaven*. <note>original: "Lucida cœli Templa"</note> And Ennius: *The great Temple of high-thundering Jove trembled.* <note>original: "Contremuit Templum magnum Iovis altitonantis." Ennius was an early Roman poet; "Jove" refers to Jupiter.</note> Because indeed antiquity chose the more elevated places of the earth, such as hills and mounds, from which a pleasant view was provided,

<summary>The author explains the choice of the title "Temple" for his work on Universal Wisdom, drawing on Latin etymology and classical poetry to define a temple as a wide, elevated space for "contemplating" the world and God's creation.</summary>
