--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the etymological exploration of the word "Temple," moving from its origins as an open viewing space to its use as a term for a sacred building, a place of political deliberation (the Senate), and ultimately, a metaphor for the human mind and wisdom itself.</meta>

<page-num>(242)</page-num>

<note>original: "numini colendo consecrabat..." - the text continues from the previous page's discussion of how an augur or priest marked out a space</note> consecrated it to the deity to be worshipped, and having erected Altars there for sacred uses, he marked them, and finally surrounded the Altars themselves with enclosures. From this it happened that the name **Temple** shifted to signify primarily a building destined for divine worship, and it is in this sense that it is now chiefly used.

It is certain, however, from **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and orator whose works defined Latin prose.</note>, that the **Curia** <note>The Senate House in Rome, the heart of political life.</note>—where the Senate meets for deliberation as if in the sight of God—was also honored with the title of "Temple." He says, *"The Senate House is the seat and Temple of public counsel."* And elsewhere (in his speech *For Milo* <note>original: "pro Milone"</note>) he calls the Senate House the *"Temple of Holiness."*

Finally, the Poet <note>Referring to Lucretius.</note> calls the "temples of the Mind" the innermost recesses of the human soul (especially that of the Wise man): from which the Mind, as if from a high watchtower, contemplates all things, and concerning those things which it perceives, it even judges according to conscience. From this, **Lucretius** <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BCE), a Roman poet and philosopher known for his work "On the Nature of Things."</note> again calls the very teaching of the wise "lofty Temples," in that well-known verse:

> *But nothing is more delightful than to hold the well-fortified*
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*high Temples, made serene by the teaching*
> *of the Wise; from where you may look down upon others,*
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*and see them wandering everywhere, and seeking*
> *a path for their straying life.*

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<meta>Vocabulary: Temple, Senate House (Curia), Cicero, Lucretius, Wisdom, Mind, Conscience, For Milo (Pro Milone)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author's justification for titling his encyclopedic work a "Temple." And this is the first reason why we think the name **Temple** should be given to our new Encyclopedia: because here a certain universal structure is prepared for the human mind, from which, by looking out in every direction, it may pleasantly contemplate all things that exist anywhere—visible and invisible, temporary and eternal—provided they have been revealed.

<margin>2. Because here all the Temples of God are delineated.</margin>
The second cause is of a more sublime consideration, which Divine Scripture itself suggested to us; namely, that whatever Eternal Wisdom has deigned to call its **TEMPLE**, its **DWELLING**, its **HOUSE**, its **TABERNACLE**, and its **SANCTUARY**, all of that is represented here. Christ’s humanity, in which the whole fullness of divinity dwells bodily. <note>Colossians 2:9.</note> Serious effort is given so that all these things receive their true <term>delineations</term> <note>Delineations: here meaning both a physical outline or diagram and a systematic description.</note> here.

<meta>This page continues the theological justification for the "Temple of Christian Wisdom." <margin>2. Because it is established for the same purposes for which the material Temple of God was built.</margin>
The purposes <note>original: "Teſt. Templi", likely an abbreviation for *Testimonia* (testimonies) or *Teloi* (ends/purposes) of the Temple.</note> can and therefore should be the same for this **Temple of Christian Wisdom**. (1.) That the people of **GOD**, by that solemn gathering to the **One place** [which God himself had chosen], might be kept in the unity of Faith—that is, in the worship of the **One GOD**.

(2.) (3.) That by solemnly remembering here the various benefits of **GOD**, they might be solemnly stirred up to praise and love **GOD**. Hence it is that they were commanded to *enter the gates of the house of GOD with thanksgiving, and his courts with hymns, and to praise his name*, **Psalm 100:4**.

(4.) That they might foster and inflame mutual concord and charity among themselves, as being fellow-servants of the same **GOD**.

(5.) Finally, that by this means they might be raised even more into the hope of a future life, where **GOD** will be praised forever in the great congregation of the **Elect Angels** and men in the presence of the throne of **GOD**. Now indeed, to these same [purposes] entirely...

<meta>This page continues the argument for why the author uses the term "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom). It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, explaining that knowledge should not be a "private property" of scholars but a universal right for all people, regardless of social status.</meta>

<page-num>(247)</page-num>
<sig>M 4</sig>

<margin>Because of 1. The breadth of the Subject. <note>original: "Subjecti amplitudine̅." In this context, "Subject" refers to the recipients of knowledge—humanity—rather than the topic of study.</note></margin>
...[were taught], not in vernacular languages commonly understood by the public, but in exotic ones that had to be learned specifically for that purpose (Latin and Greek). For that reason, Wisdom itself was regarded as the private property of a few—namely, those admitted to those original sources. But we contend that it is a possession granted to the whole of the human race, which should rightly be claimed for public right and use. We pray and testify by the glory of GOD that a way be sought and found, prepared and opened, by which everyone in general—even from the lowest common people—may be allowed to behold and adore the traces of their Creator’s power, wisdom, and goodness. These traces are imprinted everywhere in all those things that everyone sees, hears, tastes, touches, does, says, and thinks. For that primary end, I say, we profess not simply wisdom, but **WISDOM** owed to **EVERY** person.

<margin>2. The universality of the Object. <note>Here, "Object" refers to the fields of study or the totality of things that can be known.</note></margin>
As far as the object of Wisdom is concerned, scholars have hitherto divided it among themselves in such a way that one would claim <term>Philosophy</term> for himself, another <term>Theosophy</term> <note>Divine wisdom or mystical theology.</note> (or <term>Theology</term>), another <term>Medicine</term>, and another <term>Law</term>, neglecting any care for other fields or any regard for the common Truth. Indeed, even within Philosophy itself, one person chooses and cultivates this part, another that, without even greeting the others from the threshold. From this it happens that for most...

<summary>Comenius argues that wisdom should be "universal" (Pansophia) in two ways: first, it must be accessible to all people regardless of class or language; second, it must encompass all fields of study as a unified truth rather than fragmented specialties.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the fragmentation of knowledge. On the previous page, he argued that wisdom belongs to all people, not just a scholarly elite. Here, he shifts from the "Subject" (the people learning) to the "Object" (the interconnectedness of what is being learned), arguing that splitting knowledge into separate "sects" or disciplines like Philosophy and Theology is a mistake.</meta>

<page-num>(248)</page-num>

*...that knowledge should be only particular; or rather, what is worse, partial. For those who do not agree on the whole diverge into parts; and they establish sects, who do not understand how Truth spreads itself upon that same indivisible foundation.* <note>original: "insectilis Veritas." The author uses a term meaning "that which cannot be cut" to emphasize that Truth is a single, unified whole.</note> We, however, maintain that neither Order, which is the soul of things, nor Truth, which is the bond of things, can be fully known except from the entire complex of all things. Certainly, he does not perceive the Order of things who, fixed upon only one or a few matters, does not consider how all things cohere with all others. Nor does he grasp the whole Truth of things who does not observe how it is spread through all things, remaining consistent with itself. 

We urge, therefore, that the entire circle of Things and of human knowledge be taken up, and that the well-measured proportion of all things to all others be observed through the centers <note>The original Latin reads "cetra" (a short shield), but in this context of proportions and circles, it is almost certainly a printer's error for "centra" (centers).</note> of their essences. In this way, the venerable face of Truth may come into our sight in abstract forms; by that means, Truth provides the opportunity for itself to be more easily recognized elsewhere—namely, where it is manifested in concrete, physical things. 

From this, it can easily be gathered that we also disapprove of those **divorces of Theology from Philosophy, and of Philosophy from Theology**, which are seen everywhere. For for the most part, Theologians think it is their duty to devote themselves only to divine things; in turn, Philosophers, being too intent on the study of created things, often have no regard <note>The catchword "respectum" from the bottom of the page completes this sentence.</note> for Theology, and often none even for GOD himself.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument for a unified approach to knowledge. On the previous page, he argued that truth is an indivisible whole and that focusing on only one part of knowledge leads to error. Here, he addresses the specific rift between Philosophy (the study of nature) and Theology (the study of God), proposing "Pansophy" as the bridge between the two.</meta>

<page-num>(249)</page-num>
<sig>M 5</sig>

respect. <note>original: "respectum." This concludes the sentence from the previous page regarding the "well-measured proportion" of things.</note> From this it happens that some Philosophers, unaccustomed to God and religion, fall into atheism; on the other hand, some Theologians, weary of philosophers and all philosophy, detest them. There is manifest impiety in the former case, and injustice in the latter. For God, being the author of both His Works <note>Nature and the physical world.</note> and His Words <note>Holy Scripture.</note>, wishes and is able to be recognized and celebrated in both. For although He prepares us for a future life, He nevertheless educates and instructs us for that life through those things which He not only speaks, but also works here in this world. Therefore, He does not only mandate that we hear His voice, but He also commands us to behold His Works.

We, therefore, in order to reconcile Philosophy with Theology—that is, to reconcile the Works of God with the Words of God, or the Truth impressed upon Things with the Truth expressed in Words—and by that means to subordinate Reason to Faith, urge that among Christians neither Philosophy be taught separately, nor <term>Theosophy</term> <note>Divine wisdom or the study of God, used here as a synonym for Theology.</note> be taught separately, but both together: that is, **Pansophy**. <note>original: "Pansophiam." From the Greek for "All-Wisdom," the author's central concept of a universal, integrated system of knowledge.</note> *For this will properly be our All-Wisdom: to be wise for the future life, and not to be foolish in the present; to have GOD and the future life in our sights, and yet to feel how His majesty, diffused through all things, surrounds us even now on every side.*

If anyone should say (and indeed they do say it; these voices have already been hurled against us): *"But that Pansophy of yours will confuse Theology..."*

<summary>The author argues that God is the author of both nature (Works) and scripture (Words), and therefore Philosophy and Theology should be studied together as a single discipline called "Pansophy." --- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of "Pansophy" (universal wisdom). On the previous page, the author introduced the term and argued that it reconciles God's Works (nature) with His Words (Scripture). He now anticipates and answers the objection that mixing religious and secular knowledge causes confusion.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>

*...[it] will mix divine wisdom with philosophy, and human wisdom with divine, and heaven with earth:*

I will respond: 1. If these things are not confused in the mind of a scholar—for there are excellent men who are powerful in both sacred and secular knowledge—why should we fear confusion in a book? 2. Can distinct things not be taught distinctly? So that even though they are presented together, they nonetheless remain unconfused. 3. Finally, how is it that these things are not confused in **Holy Scripture**? For it does not only relate heavenly and eternal things, but also touches upon many things of this life.

You will say: *but these are subordinated to the eternal.* <note>The author anticipates a critic arguing that the Bible only mentions physical things as they relate to spiritual ones, whereas a "Pansophy" might give them too much weight.</note>

Correct; and this is the very thing we urge to be done in the book of **Pansophy**: that all things the world possesses be subordinated to Heaven, and that all Philosophical Science be compelled to serve that science which is "according to godliness." <note>original: "scientiæ illi quæ secundum pietatem est." This refers to Theology or divine wisdom.</note> *This, however, is for a threefold purpose.*

First, so that the children of God <note>Believers or those seeking divine truth.</note> may not be ignorant of those things which the <term>little-wise</term> <note>original: "sapientuli." A diminutive, dismissive term for those who think they are wise in worldly matters but lack spiritual depth.</note> of this world admire, and in which they take pride. But also, so that these men might cease to be so pleased with themselves, if they see that we understand these same things, and yet—because of the sweetness of greater things—we are drawn more toward another goal.

Secondly, because the works of **Nature**, and the manifold mysteries hidden within them, are not there only to provide delight to the impious and profane: rather, they are to delight the children of God, both Angels and Men, for whom this so admirable [display] <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note> of His power, wisdom, and goodness...

<summary>The author argues that integrating secular philosophy with divine wisdom in "Pansophy" does not cause confusion if the former is subordinated to the latter. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument for "Pansophy" (Universal Wisdom). He continues here by describing nature as a "theater" and an "alphabet" that leads the student toward God. The catchword from the previous page was "quæ" (which), continuing into the opening sentence.</meta>

<page-num>(251)</page-num>

...unfolds its theater. Let us therefore use our right, and trusting in this theater, let us ourselves rationally observe all things, rather than knowingly and willingly yielding it to the <term>profane</term> <note>Those who lack spiritual insight or treat sacred things with contempt.</note>. Finally, we wish all things to be coordinated and mutually subordinated to each other for this purpose: that through all lower things, the mind may be gradually raised to all more sublime things. *For it is certain that one does not reach the summits of things, or at least does not reach them conveniently, except by degrees. It is also certain that all natural things [and all artificial things] are alphabets for the Children of God, which are prepared for the better reading and understanding of those more sublime things which the **Law of GOD** dictates. This is shown by David: warning that we are taught great things about God by the daily course of the Heavens and Nature, but even greater things by the dictate of His Divine law. Psalm 19.*

<margin>3. Because of a fuller method of delivering Wisdom.</margin>
The third reason why we urge the use of the title **Pansophy** <note>original: "Pansophiæ." From the Greek for "all-wisdom," referring to a system that integrates all branches of knowledge.</note> is its broader method of instruction than that which Philosophy alone, or Theology alone, employs. For what **Aristotle** required of the Wise Man (who should know all things as far as possible), the same will necessarily be required in **Pansophy**: namely, the universality of Principles; a never-interrupted series of a continuously flowing Order, from beginning to end; and the infallible Truth of the things themselves. And indeed, the Principles laid down here are truly Universal, through

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the definition and method of "Pansophy" (Universal Wisdom). On the previous page, he began explaining why this title is superior to traditional "Philosophy" or "Theology" alone, citing Aristotle’s requirement that a wise man should know all things as far as possible.</meta>

<page-num>(252)</page-num>

...which fully satisfies all inquiries: *Sense*, *Reason*, and *Divine Revelation*. Moreover, the *Method* taught for use is the easiest, beginning from the most well-known things and concluding in the most unknown; leading to them, however, without any jump or gap, by a perpetual gradation. From which a third thing also emerges: the *Certainty and Truth of knowledge*, by which the learner sees himself progressing always established in the light—not retreating, not standing still, or hesitating. For the one who knows, knows that they know, and is not deceived.

Since we therefore teach how EVERYONE, regarding EVERYTHING, in EVERY WAY <note>original Greek: πάντες, πάντα, παντῶς (*pantes, panta, pantōs*)</note> may be wise, why should we not be seen as teaching **Pansophy**? <note>original Greek: πανσοφίαν (*pansophian*), meaning "all-wisdom."</note>

<margin>Why it should be called **CHRISTIAN PAN-SOPHY.**</margin>
But why, then, do we think **PAN-SOPHY** should be called so with the *addition* of **CHRISTIAN**? Firstly, because only those who are sufficiently instructed in the principles—Sense, Reason, and the Faith to be applied to divine revelations—are able to build and draw from Pansophy. But only Christians have the World before their eyes, the Word of God in their mouths, and the Spirit of God in their hearts, according to the promise of Isaiah 59:21. Therefore, **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian and philosopher.</note> vigorously contends that *Christianity alone is true Philosophy.* (Book 3, *Against the Academicians*, Chapter 19). Secondly, *all faithful and pious Christians, even the most simple, are for that very reason—because they are good Christians—Pansophists:* <note>Possessors of universal wisdom.</note> insofar as... <note>The text continues on the next page with "nus," completing the word *quatenus* (insofar as).</note>

<summary>The author defines the method of Pansophy as a gradual progression through sense, reason, and revelation, leading to absolute certainty. He argues that this system must be called "Christian Pansophy" because only through the combined light of nature and the Holy Spirit can true, universal wisdom be achieved.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophy, Sense, Reason, Divine Revelation, Certainty, Augustine, Christian Philosophy, Isaiah 59:21, Universal Wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s justification for the term "Pansophy." Having argued on the previous page that Christianity is the true philosophy because it relies on the World, the Word, and the Spirit, the author now transitions to why this "Universal Wisdom" should also be considered "Human" and accessible to all people, regardless of their faith, by building on the foundations of human reason and the "Truth of Things."</meta>

<page-num>(253)</page-num>

...[Christians] savor Heaven more than earth, the eternal more than the temporary, and they possess Christ (in whom all the treasures of Wisdom are hidden). <note>A reference to the New Testament, Colossians 2:3: "In whom are hid all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge."</note> This is possessed in an enfolded way, it is true, but nonetheless real—in the same way that any seed truly contains the whole tree or herb, along with its fruit.

But because these things ought to serve not only Christians (according to the intention of a Universal Education, which, by starting from the easiest things granted to everyone, leads to the unknown) but all who are born human—so that they might also have the power to illuminate and convince the minds of unbelievers (if it should seem good to God)—perhaps it might more suitably be called **HUMAN PAN-SOPHY**. This is also to exclude any mark of thoughtlessness, as if we were attempting something beyond the reach of man, when *we urge nothing but human things—that is, things possible and proper for man*.

Yet finally, whether this name or that one is preferred, we have judged that the title of **Pansophy** <note>From the Greek *pan* (all) and *sophia* (wisdom).</note> should be used for no greater purpose than that we wish people to be stirred up, so that *Everyone* might desire to be wise, and regarding *All Things*, completely filling their minds with the **Truth of Things** and not with the smoke of opinions. Aside from this consideration, we allow it to be called either *summit-wisdom* <note>original Greek: ἀκροσοφία (*akrosophia*), meaning the highest or most perfect wisdom.</note> or *useful-wisdom* <note>original Greek: χρηστοσοφία (*chrēstosophia*).</note> (*Wisdom regarding the best or selected things*), or indeed even Ignorance; most gladly with **Socrates** <note>The Greek philosopher Socrates (c. 470–399 BC) famously claimed his only wisdom was knowing that he knew nothing.</note>

<summary>The author argues that while Pansophy is inherently Christian, it should be called "Human Pansophy" to emphasize that it is achievable for all people through reason. The ultimate goal is to lead humanity away from mere opinions toward a universal "Truth of Things."</summary>
<keywords>Pansophy, Universal Education, Human Pansophy, Truth of Things, Socrates, Christian Wisdom, Reason, Certainty</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 253, where the author was discussing the naming of "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophy). The previous page ended with "eit..." (either), likely concluding that the name matters less than the pursuit of truth. This page begins with the word "attentively" (completing the word *attentè* from the previous page's catchword) and transitions into the biblical justification for the "Temple of Wisdom" metaphor.</meta>

<page-num>(254)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: attentively</meta>

...attentively professing this one thing: that we know that we know nothing. <note>original: "Nos nihil scire." This is a reference to the famous Socratic paradox, emphasizing intellectual humility.</note> And with the Apostle: *If anyone thinks he knows anything, he does not yet know it as he ought to know.* (1 Corinthians 8:2).

<margin>Why build according to the Idea of God?</margin>
It follows that we must state why we say this Temple of Pansophy <note>Universal Wisdom</note> is to be constructed according to the ideas, norms, and laws of the supreme architect, God Himself. The reason is at hand: because in the dimensions of the whole and the number, placement, and use of its parts, we follow the pattern that the Wisdom of God has delineated for itself. First with *Moses*, in the erecting of the Tabernacle; then with *Solomon* in the building of the Temple; and finally with *Ezekiel*, in the more glorious restoration of the destroyed Temple. For first, the Lord spoke thus to Moses:

> *Let them make me a Sanctuary, that I may dwell among them, entirely according to the likeness which I shall show you.* And later: *Look and do according to the pattern, which was shown to you on the mountain.* [Exodus 25:9, 40]

Next, God sent to David (through Nathan the Prophet, as it is believed) the pattern of the Temple and all its parts. When David handed this to Solomon, he commanded him not to deviate from that form, testifying thus: *All these things came written by the hand of the Lord to me, so that I might understand all the works of the pattern.* [1 Chronicles 28:19] Finally, he showed to Ezekiel the form of the mystical temple—that is, the new Church under Christ—with an express command to... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 254 regarding the divine blueprint for the Temple of Universal Wisdom. <page-num>(255)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: bus</meta>

...attentively <note>original: "attentè," completing the word from the previous page</note> to receive all things, and to announce them to the House of Israel. [Ezekiel 40:4]

**N. B.** <note>Latin: *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note Well."</note> It must be observed, however, that each subsequent form was fuller and more perfect than the one before it. This was not because God progressed through the passage of time or through labor and amended His works—**as happens to men in architecture, where later thoughts seem to be, and indeed are, better**—but because He wished to prefigure the progress of His Church, which would come with time. For He had ordered those things to be figured <note>In this context, "figured" means to be represented through symbolic forms or types.</note> not for His own sake, but for ours.

Therefore, the **Mosaic Tabernacle** <note>The portable dwelling place for the divine presence from the time of the Exodus.</note> was only tripartite, consisting of the *Outer Court*, the *Sanctuary*, and the *Holy of Holies*, as is clear from the 40th chapter of Exodus.

**Solomon’s Temple** had, before that final and innermost chamber [called the *Holy of Holies*], three outer courts: the *Court of the People*, the *Court of the Levites*, and the *Court of the Priests*; and finally that place open only to the High Priest once a year, the *Holy of Holies*.

The structure of **Ezekiel’s Temple** <note>The idealized, prophetic Temple described in the final chapters of the Book of Ezekiel.</note> consisted of the same number of parts [although with more walls and gates], yet with a different layout. For Solomon’s Temple lay on **Mount Moriah**, but entirely on the level ground of the mountain; but the outer parts of Ezekiel’s Temple were situated on the slope of the mountain, with each interior Court rising step by... <note>original: "gradi-", likely "gradatim," meaning "step by step" or "by degrees."</note>

<summary>This page explains that the progression of biblical structures—from the Tabernacle to Solomon's and Ezekiel's Temples—represents the gradual perfection and growth of the Church rather than an improvement in God's own design. It details the physical layout of these structures, noting the increasing complexity and the shift from level ground to a tiered, mountainous ascent.</summary>

<page-num>(256)</page-num>
...parts <note>original: "partibus," completing the word from the previous page</note>, each raised a few steps above the next; so that at last the Temple itself, that is, the **Holy of Holies** <note>original: "Sanctum Sanctorum"</note>, set upon the summit of the Mountain, might shine most splendidly in every direction. This diverse structure of the dwelling places of God among men showed the various stages of growth of the Church of God—always moving toward something greater. This growth occurs both by its expansion through many peoples and languages, and by the increase of knowledge and science as the Church ages.

For just as the World itself has its own ages, so too does the Church: infancy, childhood, manhood, and again the declining ages of old age. While old age suffers a decrease in bodily strength, it almost always intensifies the powers of the mind. This is namely because of the tendency of the human temperament toward dryness <note>In the medical theories of the time, old age was characterized by "dryness," which was believed to settle the "humors" and make a person more contemplative and less prone to impulsive passions.</note>, by which the movements of the soul become more settled and the **animal spirits** <note>term: "animal spirits" (Latin: *spiritus animales*). In early modern science, these were thought to be very subtle fluids that traveled through the nerves to carry messages between the brain and the body.</note> become more stable.

Since, therefore, it is fitting for the Church of the New Testament to be fully adult in its understanding—especially in this current age when the World is verging toward its own old age [1 Corinthians 14:20; Ephesians 4:14]—we borrow the form for this ***Temple of Christian Wisdom*** from the sketch of the final Temple given to ***Ezekiel***. We distinguish the entire apparatus of **Universal Wisdom** <note>term: "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom). The author’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, God-centered system.</note> into seven different parts. In each of these, different things are presented to be viewed, so that minds may be always raised from lower things to more sublime things, reaching at last the **Holy of Holies**, in b... <meta>catchword: "tam" (likely "beatam," meaning blessed/blissful)</meta>

The author uses Ezekiel’s visionary Temple as a model for "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom), organizing knowledge into seven ascending levels.</summary>

...as well as its eternity <note>original: "æternitatem," completing the thought from the previous page's catchword "que"</note>, might be derived. However, it must now be explained how this is understood from the form of Ezekiel’s Temple.

<margin>The seven principal parts of the description of Ezekiel’s Temple.</margin>

I. First, Ezekiel narrates generally and in few words *what he had seen in the visions of God* <note>original: "Quid in visionibus vidisset Dei"</note>. Namely:
(1.) A **City** situated on a mountain, the location of that most venerable Temple which he was about to describe (Chapter 40, verse 2). 
(2.) A **Man** standing by to show him all things, equipped with measuring rods and rules, and exhorting him to see, hear, and attend with his heart (verses 3, 4). 
(3.) Then the **Circuit of the Temple**, or the **Outermost Wall**, along with its measurements in width and height (verse 5).

II. Next, he describes the **Gate of the temple**, which was the only one in that entire circuit of the Temple, located on the eastern side (verses 6 to 17).

III. The **First Courtyard**, or the outermost one, which was called the **Court of the Common People** <note>original: "Vulgi," referring to the general public</note> or the "popular" court (because entry here was open to all the people), he describes from verse 17 to 28. This area had three gates through which one ascended by seven steps into the floor of the Courtyard itself (verses 6, 19, 22, 26).

IV. Then he describes the **Middle Courtyard**, where the sacred instruments were kept and where the Levites <note>The Levites were the tribe of Israel responsible for assisting the priests and maintaining the Tabernacle and Temple.</note> cared for those things that were required to precede the sacrifices and offerings according to the Law. The description of this part is completed from verse 28 to 44. And it should be noted—

Its location is at the very peak of the mountain (Chapter 43, verse 12). Therefore, in the description of its **Vestibule** (Chapter 40, the final verse), mention is made of steps, although without an explicit number of how many there were. Within the Temple itself, or the **House of Jehovah**, nothing is mentioned except for the **Cherubim**, the **Palm Trees** <note>Decorative motifs symbolizing divine presence and victory</note> (Chapter 41, verses 20, 21), and the **Table** which is before Jehovah (verse 24).

<margin>
**Therefore, the Universal Wisdom of the Temple consists of just as many parts.** <note>original: "Templi Pansophia." Pansophia is Comenius's term for a complete, harmonious system of all knowledge.</note>
</margin>

VII. Finally (after the repeated form of the worship of God in this new Temple in Chapters 43 and 44, and the form of the new civil order <note>original: "politiæ"</note> among the people of God in chapters 45 and 46), he describes a **river of waters** flowing out from the Temple of God through those three courtyards. It begins as a small stream, rising into a vast depth and spreading itself through all the earth, bringing fruitfulness and life to all things (Chapter 47, verse 1, etc.).

(259)

...structure, it too will consist of seven parts.

I. The first part will be the **General Prolegomena** <note>original: "Prolegomena," a formal introduction or preliminary discussion</note>, showing [1.] what the book of **Pansophia** <note>Universal Wisdom</note> is, and what its end and use are; (2.) What materials <note>original: "apparatus"</note> the goodness of God has provided to us for preparing this Universal Wisdom; (3.) What its general form is.

II. In place of that **Eastern Gate**, we will have the system of **Common Notions** <note>original: "Notionum communium," fundamental concepts or self-evident truths shared by all human beings</note>, the variety of which is naturally obvious to the human intellect. For our mind is created harmoniously, being the image of the Most High God, from whom the reasons of all things flow: and it has within itself innate standards for things, to distinguish truth from falsehood. These standards are certain common truths; which, once stated, need no proof, because they are voluntarily accepted by every sound mind. However, other specific things, about which doubt sometimes arises, are examined, proven, or rejected by means of these. A sufficient supply of such primary and common notions will truly be a **Sacred Gate**, for penetrating into the knowledge of all things easily, quickly, and safely <note>original: "facilè, citò, tutò," Comenius’s famous educational motto</note>.

III. The **Courtyard of the People** <note>original: "Atrium Vulgi," referring to the outer court of the Temple where the general public gathered</note> will give us the third part of Pansophia, explaining those things that lie within the reach of the senses, namely the **Visible World**, with all things visi-

<meta>This page continues the allegorical mapping of the sections of the Temple from Ezekiel's vision onto the structure of Universal Wisdom (Pansophia). It concludes the description of the "Courtyard of the People" (Nature) and introduces the "Middle Courtyard" (the world of human reason and artifice).</meta>

<page-num>260</page-num>

-ble creatures, and those things which occur in and around them *by the force of nature* <note>original: "vi naturae"</note>. For from the **Gate of Common Notions** <note>The "Porta communium notitiarum" mentioned on the previous page, representing the fundamental truths innate to the human mind</note>, there is a very close transition to this point, so that the common truth already tasted may first be sought and known in those things which are closest at hand, which can be seen and touched. This is the easiest path, and a certain step toward knowing more remote things more easily as well. Here, man will learn the natural requirements and structure of his life, so that he can and may know how to sustain and protect it by natural means, as long as the fates allow.

III. **The Middle Courtyard** <note>original: "Atrium medianum"</note> **will give us the fourth part of Pansophia, explaining those things that are subject to Reason: namely the Representational World** <note>original: "Mundum repraesentatitium," referring to the world of human culture, symbols, and artifacts that reflect the natural world</note>**, that is, all human works produced or to be produced by art and ingenuity, and whatever things are done by the power of our Mind.** From the **Courtyard of Nature** <note>original: "Atrium Naturae"</note>, there is again a very close entrance to this place: since our **Art** <term>Ars: In this context, "Art" refers to any human skill, craft, or technology</term> and **Prudence** <term>Prudentia: Practical wisdom or the ability to govern one’s actions through reason</term> do nothing except imitate, improve, or supplement **Nature**. In this theater, Man will learn those things which he possesses uniquely over the beasts: namely, the right use of **Reason**; wisdom in works of every kind, and the arts of **Language**. In a word, how to live a **rational life**, both in himself and in commerce with all creatures, and in human society of any kind.

<summary>This page describes the third and fourth parts of Comenius's "Pansophia," moving from the study of the physical, visible world (Nature) to the study of the world created by human reason and skill (Art and Culture).</summary>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the "Middle Courtyard" (representing human reason and artificial works) to the "Inner Courtyard," which corresponds to the fifth part of Pansophia. It moves the focus from external observation and logical reasoning to the internal spiritual life and the human will.</meta>

IV. **The Inner Courtyard** <note>original: "Atrium intimum"</note> **will give us the fifth part of Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: A 17th-century concept of "all-knowledge," a system designed to organize all human learning into a single, cohesive framework centered on God</term>**, in which Man will be taught to know himself intimately and to live a spiritual and divine life.** For here he will contemplate himself in that aspect which most closely resembles God: the absolute freedom of the **Will** <note>original: "Voluntas"</note> or **Free Choice** <note>original: "Arbitrium"</note>. Having been previously instructed by the service of the senses to know all things, and by the light of Reason to judge all things, he may now act as a King of unrestrained power. Whatever the senses reveal or hide, and whatever Reason advises or discourages, he may freely desire or reject according to his own judgment. 

For this is the highest peak of dignity that could be shared by the **Creator** <note>original: "Creator"</note> with a **Creature** <note>original: "Creatura"</note>, just as God expressed in His own deliberation regarding the creation of Man:

> *“Let us make Man,” He said, “in our image, who shall have dominion over the fish and the birds, etc.”* <note>original: "Faciamus inquiens, Hominem ad imaginem nostram, qui dominetur in pisces & aves, &c." — A reference to Genesis 1:26</note>

That is to say, let him act according to his own choice. Indeed, the perfection of the **World** <note>original: "Mundus"</note> required that such a creature be granted—one not restricted by nature in its actions, nor acting solely by natural impulse (as do the Elements, stones, plants, and brute animals), but one that could move or restrain itself at its own pleasure. It also serves the glory of **God’s Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapientia DEI"</note>, which shines more brightly in governing creatures that act by their own will than in those that are bound to their actions by natural law.

### VI.

The *final inner sanctuary of the Temple, the Holy of Holies* <note>original: "Ultimum Templi penetrale Sanctum Sanctorum." This refers to the most sacred part of the biblical Tabernacle, accessible only to the High Priest, here used as a metaphor for the highest level of knowledge</note>, will give us the sixth part of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: A 17th-century system of "all-knowledge" meant to harmonize all learning with divine truth</term>, in which the **GOD OF GODS**, inhabiting His own eternity, will be beheld: namely, insofar as He grants Himself and that infinite and eternal majesty and glory of His to be known in this life to those whom He destines for His eternal fellowship and draws toward it. In this Theater <note>original: "Theatro." Early modern writers often used "Theater" to describe the world or a field of study as a place where God’s glory is put on display for contemplation</note>, Man will recognize, by contemplating the archetype <note>The divine blueprint or original model in the mind of God</note> of himself and of the World, how truly all the works of God reflect God in their own way, and yet how infinitely more sublime all things are in God—whatever good, beautiful, pleasant, or desirable things are found in Creatures <note>In this context, "Creatures" refers to all created things (animals, plants, minerals), not just living beings</note>. The effect of this recognition will be that, captured by the sweetness of that eternal happiness and already as if intoxicated by a taste of it, he will know and be able to do nothing else but rejoice in his **God**, and melt away in His love, and permit himself to be entirely inhabited by Him, and with full delight...

<summary>This page describes the sixth and highest stage of Pansophia, equated to the biblical Holy of Holies, where the seeker moves beyond worldly philosophy to contemplate God as the ultimate archetype. This divine contemplation is intended to lead the individual into a state of mystical joy and union with the Creator.</summary>

### VII.

Finally, *that river of Waters flowing from the Temple* and spreading across the whole earth, will provide the final part of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: A 17th-century system of "all-knowledge" meant to harmonize all learning with divine truth</term>, *explaining the true use of the waters of true Wisdom*. Indeed, I know that by those waters bursting forth from the Temple of Ezekiel <note>original: "Ezechieliano." Referring to the prophetic vision in Ezekiel 47 of a river flowing from the temple that brings life to everything it touches</note>, the course of the Gospel was signified—tranquil at the start, yet gradually gaining in volume so that no one can hinder or halt it, just as a river cannot be restrained by any constructed dams. But it is also generally established that the doctrine of Wisdom is compared to waters. *The doctrine of Wisdom is a fountain of life*, says Solomon in *Proverbs 13:14*. Likewise: *Deep waters are the words from the mouth of a great man; a bubbling brook is the fountain of Wisdom. Proverbs 18:4.*

In the writings of Sirach <note>original: "Syracidem." This refers to the Book of Sirach or Ecclesiasticus, a book of ethical teachings in the biblical tradition</note>, Wisdom herself is introduced speaking thus: *I, Wisdom, have poured out rivers; like a water-conduit I have come forth into Paradise. I said, I will water my garden, and I will soak my meadow, etc.* (24.) <note>referring to Sirach 24:30-31</note> We must therefore consider through which designated channels all these waters ought to drench the little gardens of our soul, and all the green spaces of the business of our life, and the entire paradise of the Church. This, I say, is to be investigated and made plain in this final...

<margin>
Why should the Temple of Wisdom be dedicated to the Universal Church?
</margin>
And this, among other reasons, is why we have said that this Temple of Wisdom must be consecrated to the Universal <term>Catholic: Comenius uses the word "Catholic" in its original sense of "universal" or "whole," referring to the global community of believers across all nations rather than a specific denomination.</term> Church of Jesus Christ, gathered and yet to be gathered from all nations. Namely because:

1. Whatever her children are capable of and know is owed to this Mother, for her comfort and honor.

2. *They do not light a lamp,* says Christ, *to put it under a bushel basket, but on a lampstand, so that it may give light to all who are in the house (Matthew 5:15).* Now, this light of universal wisdom—which subordinates all things to its own ends—is a certain lamp of God; therefore, it must by all means be hung in the House of the Church, so that it may shine for everyone.

3. This house of the living God, the Church (*as is said in 1 Timothy 3:15*), is built according to the same archetype <note>original: "ideam." In 17th-century philosophy, an "idea" is the perfect mental pattern or blueprint used by a creator.</note> upon which this very Temple of Wisdom was founded, so that by contemplating this Temple, the Church might be advanced into a fuller knowledge of herself. This follows the passage in the *Song of Songs 1:8*: *If you do not know yourself, O most beautiful among women, go forth and follow the tracks of the flocks, etc.* For here, through the "tracks" of things <note>The "vestigia rerum" or traces of God's handiwork left in the physical and spiritual world.</note> which have been traced out, the Church is moved forward to recognize her own beauty and that of her eternal Bridegroom. And since the *manifold wisdom* of God is made known to the Angels through the Church (*Ephesians 3:10*), the Church herself, [becoming a teacher] of her own [and] of the Ange-

<summary>Comenius argues that his system of Universal Wisdom belongs to the Church, as it acts as a divine lamp meant to illuminate all believers and serves as a mirror through which the Church recognizes its own beauty and divine purpose.</summary>

<margin>A solution to objections</margin>
And so it can now be clear what kind of book it is that we are urging to be composed, and for what reasons we urge it. Let people, therefore, cease to fear or suspect that sacred things will here be confused with profane <note>In this context, "profane" does not mean "disrespectful," but rather "secular" or "worldly" knowledge (science, history, etc.) as opposed to "sacred" scripture.</note> things. For first, *To the pure, all things are pure* (Titus 1:15). Neither the Israelites nor the structure of the Tabernacle <term>Tabernacle</term><note>The portable dwelling place for the divine presence from the time of the Exodus.</note> were defiled by the Gold and Silver taken from the impure Egyptians <note>A common historical argument (popularized by St. Augustine) that Christians can "plunder" the useful truths of pagan philosophy just as the Israelites took Egyptian gold to build their temple.</note>, etc. Second, nothing is confused here; rather, remedies are prepared for the confusions with which the minds of men struggle, through a distinct knowledge of all things that raises itself by its own steps to its own summits...

<summary>This page concludes a vision of a globally expanded Church and defends the author's project of a "Universal Book" of knowledge. The author argues that integrating secular learning with religious truth does not defile the faith, but rather provides a remedy for human confusion.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Temple of Wisdom, Song of Moses, Revelation 15, sacred vs. profane knowledge, Gold of the Egyptians, Church expansion, universal education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Comenius's "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom). The previous page ended with the catchword "which," transitioning into this discussion of things that "can, deserve to, and ought to be known," arguing that sensory knowledge, reason, and divine revelation all work together in a single, divine harmony.</meta>

(268)

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

...[things] which can be known, deserve to be known, and ought to be known. Therefore, we first demonstrate those most general and well-known principles which are self-evidently true by the testimony of **common sense** <term>common sense</term><note>In this historical context, "common sense" (sensus communis) refers to the internal faculty that unites the data from all five external senses into a single perception.</note> itself. Then we present those things which the **External Senses** see and touch, and which they know they are seeing and touching. Next, we present those things derived from these by certain **Reason**; but in such a way that their truth is also brought back to sensory experiences. Finally, we present those things that come from **Divine Revelation** and are grasped by faith alone; yet in such a way that **Reason** and the senses are simultaneously taught to bear witness to God, and to recognize the truth of invisible revelations through the analogy <note>The author argues that we can understand high spiritual truths by looking at how physical things work in the natural world.</note> of visible things. Thus, the one eternal harmony of the entire eternal Truth, scattered through all things, may be consistent with itself in every way. *What confusion, then, is here, where there is pure harmony?* <note>original: "Quid ergo hic confusionis ubi harmonia mera?"</note>

But people say: "Christ did not teach such things; He handed down only the way of salvation."
I respond: 1. Why then do you teach—or care that they are taught—even those lesser things pertaining to this life to your children: namely, the studies of Letters, Numbers, **Syllogisms** <term>syllogism</term><note>A form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two shared premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore, Socrates is mortal).</note>, etc.? 2. Granted, Christ did not teach these things, yet he certainly did not forbid them; rather, he suggested throughout that they should not be ignored, since he [drew] from natural and artificial things various [ways] of declaring spiritual mysteries...

<summary>This page outlines a hierarchy of knowledge—moving from common sense and physical sensation to reason and finally divine revelation—arguing that all these forms of truth are harmonious. It also defends the study of secular subjects like logic and math, noting that Christ used natural metaphors to explain spiritual truths.</summary>
<keywords>common sense, external senses, reason, divine revelation, harmony of truth, Christ's teaching, syllogisms, pansophia</keywords>

<sig>N 3</sig>

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

...and took up both occasions and forms, showing indeed that these are the relationships of visible things to invisible things, so that the latter can hardly be understood by us in any more convenient way than by the guidance of the former. <note>The author argues that we need the "ladder" of the physical world to climb toward spiritual understanding.</note> Therefore, those who desire and boast of only spiritual and heavenly things think themselves wiser than Christ <note>original: "Supra Christum sapiunt"</note>, having removed from the study of Christianity all those things with which (as they say) the "profane" are occupied. Let those people indeed be occupied and fix themselves here; we, mindful of our ***Further Beyond*** <note>original: "Plus ultra" - a famous motto of the era suggesting there is always more to discover beyond the known horizon</note>, will not be occupied *by* those things, but rather we shall occupy *them*, as steps and ladders to promote ourselves more easily and quickly toward those further and higher goals of ours. 

3. If Christ did not teach such things by Himself, He taught them through others; if He did not teach them then, He teaches them now. For He is the same ***yesterday, today, and forever*** (Hebrews 13:8). He is ***that Wisdom of God through which all things are made*** (Proverbs 8 and 1:9). Whatever light, order, or wisdom exists anywhere, it is from Him: through whomever among men and in whatever century it was unearthed from the Treasury of His works and words. He Himself had come into the World, not to arrange these lesser matters, but to give His life as a <term>ransom</term><note>original Greek: λύτρον (lytron), a price paid to release a captive or debt</note>, ***as a redemption for many*** (Matthew 20:28). For this reason, He also entrusted the tasks of writing the books of the Gospel and of establishing external order in His Church to His disciples; promising His presence to them and to their successors...

<summary>The author argues that physical and "profane" knowledge serves as a necessary ladder to reach spiritual truth, asserting that all wisdom—scientific or divine—originates from Christ as the eternal Wisdom of God.</summary>

<margin>Rules to be observed by the Architects of the Temple of Wisdom.</margin>
It will be right for us to remember, if the decision to build this Temple of Universal Wisdom stands:

1. That the Temple must be built (as David advised Solomon) *great, excellent, famous, and glorious throughout all lands* (1 Chronicles 22:5), because our God is great above all gods (2 Chronicles 2:5). Therefore, craftsmen worthy of such a great work must be sought from everywhere—those who know how to skillfully devise <note>original: "adinvenire prudenter"</note> whatever shall be necessary (2 Chronicles 2:7, 14).

2. Solomon's Temple was founded, by God's command, on Mount *Moriah*, which signifies "the vision of God" <note>The author uses the traditional etymology of Moriah (from the Hebrew 'ra’ah', to see) to link physical sight with spiritual insight.</note>. Likewise, the foundation of the Temple of Wisdom will be the *Vision of the Lord*: that is, all things shall lead to this—that through all visible things, the invisible ruler of the universe himself may be perceived by the mind, with his power, wisdom, and goodness spread throughout all things.

3. The material of Solomon's Temple was of three kinds: *Stones, Wood, and Metals*. These included more precious stones, such as *Marble and Gems*; sap-filled and fragrant woods, such as *Fir and Cedar*; and *Metals*

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

...purest metals <note>The OCR "talla" is likely the conclusion of "metalla" (metals) from the previous page.</note>; refined Gold <note>original: "Aurum obryzum," referring to gold of the highest purity, often tested by fire.</note>. The material for the Temple of Wisdom will be provided by those three "forests" <note>In the scholarly traditions of this era, a *sylva* or "forest" referred to a vast collection of raw data or source materials.</note> of principles: *Sense*, *Reason*, and *Divine Revelation*. Sense, through its tangibility, represents the nature of stone; Reason, with its budding power, represents the vigor of trees; and the Word of God, through its virtue that remains forever, represents the incorruptibility of the purest gold.

4. *The walls were erected from Stones; the panels to cover the walls were made of Wood; and gold plates were used to overlay the panels* [2 Chronicles 3:5, 6, 7]. Moreover, even the *marble floor* (2 Chronicles 3:6) *was covered with Gold* (1 Kings 6:30); but the *sacred vessels, the Altar, the Table, the Lampstands, the Lamps, the Censors, and everything else were made of the purest gold* (2 Chronicles 4:19, etc.). In the same way, let the foundations and walls of the Temple of Wisdom be established only from those things whose truth has been solidified even to the point of sensory evidence <note>The author argues that true wisdom must start with what we can see and touch—empirical evidence.</note>. Yet, the causes—the reasons why each thing must necessarily be exactly as it is—should be added and brought into the open by the power of reasoning. Finally, the splendor of divine testimonies should be added above all, so that the majesty of Truth may be fully and everywhere established in all things. However, the sacred furnishings, which pertain to the mysteries of faith and salvation, must be made of the purest gold of God’s own words.

5. Solomon’s Temple was built from stones that were hewn and perfected,

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page continues the architectural metaphor of the "Temple of Wisdom," drawing a parallel between the silent, precise construction of Solomon's Temple and the ideal method for establishing philosophical and theological truths. It emphasizes that intellectual "materials" (doctrines) should be perfectly prepared beforehand so that the final assembly is harmonious and free from the "noise" of public dispute.</meta>

so that during the building, neither hammer, nor axe, nor any iron tool was heard (1 Kings 6:7). In the same way, during the building of the Temple of Wisdom, it is fitting that no noises of people disputing or quarreling be heard. Instead, all things should be composed from Truth and reduced to a perfect square <note>original: "quadraturam." In architecture, this refers to the precise squaring of stones so they fit together without gaps; here, it refers to logical consistency.</note>: that is, with dogmas and doctrines not rashly heaped together—only to be chipped away, hewn, shaped into some likeness of truth, or adapted to the intellect at the very moment they are being placed as part of the structure.

Rather, these doctrines should come forth as if already prepared from the *previously established workshop of Principles*. They should show no bends, gaps, or any inequalities relative to the rest, but should appear to square perfectly according to the rule <note>original: "ex amussim," a technical term for using a carpenter's rule or plumb line to ensure absolute precision.</note> with all things previously stated, currently connected, or yet to be connected. Only in this way can the Truth of things be set in the light and be freed from contradictions.

6. The parts of that material Temple <note>Referring to the physical Temple of Solomon in Jerusalem.</note> were most perfectly proportioned; therefore, the history of its building is full of numbers and measurements (1 Kings 6). And the Angel, about to show the structure of the symbolic Temple to the Prophet, approached equipped with a linen cord and a measuring rod (Ezekiel 40:3). In the same way, in the Temple of Wisdom, all things must be reduced to a universal symmetry, so that for the wandering agitations of our mind, there may be fixed, certain, and immovable boundaries <note>original: "cancelli," literally lattices or railings used to mark off a space, here used metaphorically for the constraints of logic and evidence.</note>.

<summary>The author argues that the "Temple of Wisdom" must be built with the same silent precision as Solomon's Temple, using pre-fitted principles to avoid the "noise" of intellectual disputes. He emphasizes that universal symmetry and measurement are necessary to provide fixed boundaries for human thought.</summary>
<keywords>Temple of Wisdom, Solomon's Temple, logic, symmetry, architectural metaphor, 1 Kings 6, Ezekiel 40, precision, theological method</keywords>

To these ends, all things, from the greatest to the least, are directed, each in its own order.

fountains of Truth, all the
streams of specialized
sciences will flow forth.

->III.<-

# Of the Temple of Wisdom

## THE FIRST COURT
<note>original: *ATRIUM PRIMUM*. In the architectural metaphor of the "Temple of Wisdom," the court (atrium) represents the first stage of learning where a student observes the physical world before entering into deeper, more abstract truths.</note>

In which the visible World, with
everything that occurs within
and around it by the power of 
NATURE, is presented for 
observation. This is done to the 
end that human beings may 
learn to know the requirements 
and the structure of their 
natural life, and to religiously 
revere the Wisdom of 
their Creator.

# IV.
## Of the Temple of Wisdom
->THE MIDDLE COURT<-
<note>original: *ATRIUM MEDIUM*. In this architectural metaphor for education, the "Middle Court" represents the stage of learning where a student moves from studying nature to studying the human being and the products of human reason.</note>

In which the rational creature, **H u m a n i t y**, along with all those things which are produced—or can be produced—by human **I n g e n u i t y**, are displayed for examination. This is done with the aim that human beings may learn to recognize the **P O W E R  O F  T H E I R  M I N D**, and use these gifts to honor God their Creator <note>original: *plasmatori*, literally "molder" or "shaper," suggesting a craftsman at work.</note>, and be taught how to **L I V E  A  T R U L Y  R A T I O N A L  L I F E**.

<meta>This page serves as the formal title page for the "Didactica Magna," following the theological introduction on the previous page. It establishes the pedagogical program that follows.</meta>

<meta>The text is contained within a rectangular double-line border. The page number is placed in a separate box at the top.</meta>

# **D I D A C T I C S**
## **G R E A T ,**
### **Displaying a universal art**
### **of teaching all things**
### **to all people.**
<note>original: *Didactica Magna*. "Didactics" refers to the science or art of instruction.</note>

->OR<-

*A certain and exquisite method for establishing such Schools in all communities, towns, and villages of any Christian Kingdom, so that all the youth of both sexes, with no one being neglected anywhere, may be educated in Letters, refined in Manners, and imbued with Piety, and by that reasoning may be instructed within the years of puberty in all things which belong to the present and future life.*

->Briefly, Pleasantly, Thoroughly.<-
<note>original: *Compendiosè, Jucundè, Solidè*. This was Comenius's famous pedagogical motto, emphasizing efficiency, student engagement, and lasting knowledge.</note>

->Where, of all things which are advised,<-

**FOUNDATIONS**, *are drawn out from the very Nature of things.*
**TRUTH**, *is shown by parallel examples from the mechanical arts.*
**THE SEQUENCE**, *is arranged by Years, Months, Days, and Hours.*
**A WAY**, *finally, is shown for successfully putting these into effect, easily and surely.*

->Through the Work and Study of J. A. C.<-
<note>J. A. C. refers to Johannes Amos Comenius (Jan Amos Komenský), the Moravian philosopher and pedagogue.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of Comenius's "The Great Didactic," outlining his revolutionary vision for a universal education system that teaches all subjects to all children regardless of gender or social status.</summary>
<keywords>The Great Didactic, universal education, pedagogy, Johannes Amos Comenius, foundations of teaching, school reform, co-education</keywords>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page follows the main title page of the "Great Didactic" and serves as a programmatic statement or "motto" page. It defines the core objective of Comenius's educational reform: to make teaching more efficient and learning more joyful for the benefit of society.</meta>

(283)

# OF DIDACTICS
->**Let the Bow and the Stern of our [work] be:**<-
<note>original: *Prora & Puppis*. A Latin idiom meaning "the beginning and the end" or "the most important part." Comenius uses the metaphor of a ship to describe his pedagogical system.</note>

->*To investigate the method by which:*<-

**TEACHERS** may teach less, while Learners may learn more.

**SCHOOLS** may have less noise, nausea, and empty labor, but more leisure, delight, and solid progress.

**THE CHRISTIAN COMMONWEALTH** may have less darkness, confusion, and discord, but more light, order, peace, and tranquility.
<note>original: *Respublica Christiana*. This refers to the collective body of Christian nations, which Comenius hoped to reform through universal education.</note>

---

->*Psalm 67, verses 1 & 2.*<-

> May God have mercy on us, and bless us! May He cause His face to shine upon us and have mercy on us! So that we may know Your way upon the Earth, and Your saving health among all nations.
<note>original: *Salutare Tuum*. Often translated as "Your salvation" or "Your saving power."</note>

---

<summary>This page presents the core mission statement of Comenius's educational philosophy: reducing the burden on teachers while increasing the effectiveness and joy of learning for students, ultimately aiming for a more peaceful society. It concludes with a biblical invocation from the Psalms to frame the work as a divine mission.</summary>
<keywords>educational reform, pedagogy, efficiency in teaching, student-centered learning, Christian Commonwealth, Psalm 67, Jan Amos Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page serves as the Table of Contents (Lemmata) for the "Great Didactic." It follows the opening mission statement and marks the beginning of the formal treatise, outlining the anthropological and theological foundations of Comenius's educational system.</meta>

(284)

<image-desc>A horizontal row of nine decorative floral fleurons.</image-desc>

->THE HEADINGS OF THE CHAPTERS<-
<note>original: *Lemmata Capitum*. This serves as the table of contents for the following work.</note>

# OF THE GREAT
## DIDACTIC.

*In the Preface, the diligent care of the education of children is earnestly commended to the Magistrate, Parents, and Teachers through various reasons and examples. In the work itself, the following subjects are treated:*

**Chapter I.** It is proven that Man is the last, most complete, and most excellent of all Created Things.
<note>original: *Creaturarum*. In the 17th-century context, this refers to all things created by God, placing humans at the pinnacle of the natural hierarchy.</note>

**Chapter II.** That the ultimate goal of Man lies beyond this life.

**Chapter III.** That this life is nothing but a preparation for eternity.

**Chapter IV.** That there are three stages of preparation for eternity: (1) To KNOW oneself (and all things with oneself), (2) To RULE oneself, and (3) To DIRECT oneself toward GOD.
<note>original: *Nosse, Regere, & ad Deum Dirigere*. These three pillars correspond to Comenius's three goals of education: Erudition (intellect), Virtue (morals), and Piety (religion).</note>

**Chapter V.** That the seeds of these three (Learning, Morals, and Religion) are implanted in us by Nature.

**Chapter VI.** That Man, if he is to become a human being, must be formed.
<note>Comenius famously argued that humans are not born "human" in the functional sense, but must be shaped through education to realize their potential.</note>

**Chapter VII.** That the formation of Man is most suitably carried out in early youth; and that unless it happens then, it can hardly be done at all.

<meta>catchword: Chapter VIII.</meta>
<sig>f</sig>

<summary>This page lists the first seven chapters of the Great Didactic, establishing Comenius's philosophical foundation: that humans are divine creations destined for eternity who must be "formed" through education in knowledge, virtue, and piety from a young age.</summary>
<keywords>Table of Contents, human nature, purpose of life, preparation for eternity, three pillars of education, early childhood education, Jan Amos Comenius</keywords>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for Comenius's *Great Didactic*. It transitions from the theological foundations of education to the practical necessity of schools and the methodology of teaching based on the "Order of Nature."</meta>

(285)

is done in youth, it cannot be done at all.
<note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "unless it is carried out in early youth..."</note>

**Chapter VIII.** That youth must be formed together; and that there is a need for Schools.
<note>original: *Scholisque opus esse*. Comenius argues that while parents are the first teachers, specialized institutions (schools) are necessary for systematic communal learning.</note>

**Chapter IX.** That the entire youth of both sexes must be committed to Schools.
<note>A revolutionary statement for the 17th century; Comenius insisted that girls as well as boys were created in God’s image and possessed minds capable of wisdom.</note>

**Chapter X.** That instruction in Schools should be Universal, or that in schools everyone must be taught all things.
<note>original: *Omnes omnia docendos esse*. This is the core of "Pansophy" (all-wisdom): not that every person becomes an expert in every field, but that everyone receives a foundational understanding of the whole world.</note>

**Chapter XI.** That perfect Schools have been lacking until now.

**Chapter XII.** That Schools can be reformed for the better.

**Chapter XIII.** That the foundation for reforming Schools is an accurate order in all things.

**Chapter XIV.** That the accurate order of the School must be borrowed from Nature. Therefore:
<note>Comenius believed that because God created both Nature and the human mind, the most effective way to teach is to follow the natural cycles and patterns observed in the world (like a bird tending its nest or a seed growing).</note>

**Chapter XV.** There are shown, from Nature, the Foundations for prolonging life.

**Chapter XVI.** General requirements for teaching and learning, that is, How teaching and learning should be done with Certainty, so that the result cannot fail to follow.

**Chapter XVII.** Foundations of **Ease** in teaching and learning.
<note>original: *Facilitatis*. Comenius sought to remove the "torture" and "drudgery" of 17th-century schooling, making it pleasant for the child.</note>

**Chapter XVIII.** Foundations of **Thoroughness** in teaching and learning.
<note>original: *Soliditatis*. This refers to "solid" learning—ensuring that knowledge is deeply rooted and not just memorized by rote.</note>

**Chapter XIX.** Foundations of concise **Speed** in teaching. Where among other things it is shown how one Teacher can be sufficient for any number of students.
<term>Præceptor<gloss>Teacher or Instructor</gloss></term>
<note>Comenius proposes a system where one teacher can lead a large class through organized group instruction, a major shift from the individualized tutoring of his era.</note>

**Chapter XX.** The Method of the Sciences in particular is proposed.

<column-break/>

<summary>This page outlines the Table of Contents for the core chapters of the Great Didactic, transitioning from the necessity of universal schools to the mechanical "Order of Nature" that ensures successful teaching.</summary>
<keywords>Universal Education, Co-education, Pansophy, Order of Nature, Educational Reform, Pedagogy, Classroom Management</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Table of Contents for Comenius's Great Didactic, transitioning from general principles to specific methods for different subjects and the organizational structure of his proposed school system.</meta>

(286)

**Chapter XXI.** Method of the Arts.
<note>original: *Artium Methodus*. This refers to the "practical arts" or skills, teaching students how to do things rather than just know things.</note>

**Chapter XXII.** Method of Languages.

**Chapter XXIII.** Method of Morals.
<note>original: *Methodus Morum*. Comenius believed virtue could be taught through habit and example, not just rules.</note>

**Chapter XXIV.** Method of Instilling Piety.

**Chapter XXV.** Demonstrating that if we want Schools to be fully reformed according to the true standards of true Christianity, the books of the Pagans must either be removed, or certainly handled more cautiously than they have been until now.
<note>original: *Gentilium libros*. Comenius argued that relying too heavily on non-Christian classical authors (like Ovid or Cicero) could corrupt young minds, and that Christian texts should take precedence.</note>

**Chapter XXVI.** Concerning the fourfold structure of Schools according to the grades of age and progress. And from here:
<note>Comenius proposed a revolutionary four-stage school system, with each stage lasting six years.</note>

**Chapter XXVII.** The Idea of the Mother School is presented.
<note>original: *Scholæ maternæ*. This is the "School of the Mother's Knee," covering ages 0–6, emphasizing early sensory learning at home.</note>

**Chapter XXVIII.** The Idea of the Vernacular School.
<note>original: *Scholæ Vernaculæ*. A public primary school for ages 6–12, taught in the students' native language rather than Latin, intended for all children regardless of career path.</note>

**Chapter XXIX.** A Sketch of the Latin School.
<note>original: *Scholæ Latinæ*. A secondary school for ages 12–18 for those aspiring to higher studies, focusing on the four languages (Vernacular, Latin, Greek, and Hebrew) and the arts.</note>

**Chapter XXX.** Concerning the Academy.
<note>original: *De Academia*. The University level (ages 18–24), where students specialize in a specific field and travel for wider experience.</note>

**Chapter XXXI.** Concerning a Universal and fully accurate order for Schools.

**Chapter XXXII.** Concerning the requirements necessary to begin the practice of this Universal method.
<note>original: *Universariæ hujus methodi praxin*. The "praxis" or practical implementation of his "Great Didactic."</note>

---

->THE END.<-

*All these things have already been worked out by the Author; they only await his final touch, which he will apply sooner or later depending on the favor and help he receives from the learned world.*
<note>A common closing sentiment in 17th-century academic works, appealing for patronage or support to finalize the publication.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the table of contents, outlining Comenius's specific teaching methods for various subjects and his revolutionary four-stage educational system—the Mother, Vernacular, Latin, and Academy schools.</summary>
<keywords>Method of Arts, Method of Languages, Moral Education, Pagan Books, Mother School, Vernacular School, Latin School, Academy, Educational Reform</keywords>

### The High German in some paragraphs of this book, translated into English.
<note>original: "High-Dutch." In the 17th century, "Dutch" was a general term for Germanic languages; "High-Dutch" specifically refers to High German (Hochdeutsch).</note>

§ 200. **Come you here, boys, and learn the Latin language.**

§ 201. **Come here, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; you boys.**

§ 203. **God will,**
**God will help you.**
**God &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; will help &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; you.**

§ 210. **You, Master. How do you say in Latin, "Come here, boys"? You, Master. How do you say, "God will help you"? etc.**
<note>original: "M." This stands for Magister, meaning a schoolmaster or teacher.</note>

§ 214. **Who? O you? Whose? To whom? Whom? With whom?**
<note>These questions correspond to the Latin grammatical cases: Nominative, Vocative, Genitive, Dative, Accusative, and Ablative.</note>

**Behold, a boy.**
**O you boy.**
**A boy’s hand.**
**Give to the boy.**
**See that boy.**
**Take from the boy.**
<note>These sentences provide examples of the six Latin noun cases for the word "boy."</note>

§ 231. **My son, if you diligently learn the elegant Latin tongue, then I will certainly love you, for there is great wisdom to be found within it. You have a good teacher who shows you how you may understand it. It is also very easy if one observes strictly. The beginning is indeed somewhat difficult, but the end will certainly be pleasant.**

**God help you, my child.**
